Active morphodynamics of intracellular organelles in the trafficking pathway
S. Alex Rautu 1,2, Richard G. Morris 3,4,5, Madan Rao 2

Abstract
From the Golgi apparatus to endosomes, organelles in the endomembrane system exhibit complex and varied morphologies that are often related to their function. These membrane-bound organelles operate far from equilibrium due to directed fluxes of trafficking vesicles, yet the physical principles governing the emergence and maintenance of these morphologies remain elusive. By coarse-graining the active mechanochemical cycles associated with individual fission and fusion events, we show how vesicular trafficking manifests at the hydrodynamic scale, generating not only fluxes of membrane area and encapsulated volume but also active stresses that mediate momentum transfer between an organelle and its cytosolic environment. The fluid and deformable nature of the bounding membrane gives rise to novel physics by coupling these nonequilibrium forces to organelle composition, morphology, and hydrodynamic flows. We demonstrate how both stable compartment drift and ramified sac-like morphologies, reminiscent of Golgi cisternal motion and shape, emerge from the same nonequilibrium dynamics of fission and fusion.



I
Introduction

Nonequilibrium self-assembly is thought to play a crucial role in the emergence and spatiotemporal patterning of intracellular structures
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
, such as organelles, mitotic spindle
[
6
,
4
]
, centrioles
[
7
]
, filopodia, flagella and cilia
[
8
]
.
A key feature of such nonequilibrium structure formation and maintenance is the ATP/GTP-driven addition-removal of its subunits; for instance, actin or microtubule based structures form and adapt dynamically via the polymerization-depolymerization of monomers maintained out of equilibrium
[
9
,
10
]
.

In this work, we explore such ideas in the context of the organelles in the endomembrane system
[
11
]
, such as the Golgi apparatus, lysosomes, endosomes, and vacuoles. Such organelles are often identified by their lipid and protein composition, and governed by local material fluxes and synthesis
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
. They are also identified by their morphology, which remarkably is maintained in the face of continual nonequilibrium driving from the flux of transport vesicles
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
15
,
20
,
16
]
.
Indeed, perturbations to vesicular flux have been observed to dramatically alter the shape of Golgi cisternae
[
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
, highlighting the role of nonequilibrium driving in determining the steady-state shape.

Due to the complexity and intricate nature of the relevant soft-matter physics, a principled physical description of the nonequilibrium dynamics of such micron-size membrane compartments embedded in the cytosol and subject to vesicular trafficking has been a challenge. Although a few studies have treated the out-of-equilibrium shape changes induced by the incorporation of additional membrane material
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
]
, developing a principled physical description of organelle morphodynamics under sustained vesicular trafficking remains a challenge.

To this end, we incorporate three fundamental features that are common to the dynamics of membrane-bound organelles in the trafficking pathway. First, fission and fusion of transport vesicles are active processes
[
27
,
36
]
that require specific energy-consuming macromolecules associated with
nonequilibrium mechanochemical cycles

[
37
]
. Second, each completed fission or fusion cycle has two distinct coarse-grained effects: it exchanges membrane area and lumenal volume with the organelle, and it exerts a localized distribution of active forces on the adjacent cytosolic fluid
[
38
,
39
,
27
,
36
]
. Third, membrane shape deformations couple hydrodynamically to the surrounding medium, inducing both membrane and fluid flows, which dynamically change the local membrane composition. Taken together, these ingredients yield a coarse-grained hydrodynamic framework for membrane-bound organelles subject to active trafficking. By representing fission and fusion through generic material fluxes and active force distributions rather than organelle-specific molecular details, the framework applies to a broad class of organelles whose morphology and motion are regulated by trafficking. Organelle morphodynamics therefore provides a rich context for the physics of nonequilibrium soft matter, involving an interplay between fluid dynamics, deformable interfaces, composition, and active stresses arising from the microscopic transduction of chemical energy
[
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
]
.

II
Results

We derive active hydrodynamic equations for a closed membrane compartment embedded in a viscous solvent subject to active fission and fusion of transport vesicles, coupling membrane shape and composition to the surrounding fluid flow (see Appendix A). We find that each trafficking cycle is associated with a cycle-averaged force profile, characterized by its first and second moments (Fig.
1
). These active force moments dynamically renormalize membrane tension and spontaneous curvature: the former can drive shape instabilities, while the latter can promote segregation of fission and fusion components—our first main result. Linear stability analysis of the derived hydrodynamic equations (Appendix B) about a uniform spherical membrane compartment in the presence of isotropic vesicular flux leads to our second result: a spontaneous drift instability of the compartment beyond a threshold activity (Fig.
2
). Such motion can be either anterograde or retrograde relative to the incident vesicular flux, depending on the magnitude of the active force moment and the relative fission and fusion rates (Fig.
3
). Indeed the active stresses control a combined drift-shape instability, leading to moving spheres, prolates, and oblates. Importantly, the drift instability does not require a fore-aft symmetry breaking in shape and can be driven by a dipolar membrane-area flux. This has important implications for cisternal progression
[
15
,
44
,
45
,
16
]
. In addition, the compartment exhibits higher-order shape instabilities in the form of flattened sacs or ramified tubular structures (Fig.
2
and
3
). Such cisternal morphologies, commonly exhibited in a variety of cell types
[
46
,
47
,
16
]
, constitute our third result. To investigate whether the growing compositional perturbations saturate at finite amplitude, we extend the perturbative analysis to weakly nonlinear order, retaining composition dynamics through cubic order while keeping the mechanical shape response linear (Appendix C). The resulting reduced amplitude description reveals stable stationary and steadily drifting states, constituting our fourth main result; see Fig.
4
. We emphasize that all of these results are contingent on a hydrodynamic description of nonequilibrium fission and fusion processes and will not arise from a purely kinetic treatment.

Figure 1:

Organelle subject to active fission and fusion of transport vesicles.
(a) A membrane organelle that is continually subject to the fission and fusion of transport vesicles (small circles). The blue arrows indicate outgoing vesicles (fission), while the red arrows label incoming vesicles (fusion). We model fission and fusion as separate processes, each associated with a biochemical reaction cycle that violates detailed balance (insets depict four states). (b) Cross-section of a patch of the deformable membrane
\mathcal{M}
, showing the thin active layer adjacent to the membrane in which numerous fission and fusion events may occur. On large length and time scales, these events can be described by local densities
\rho_{k}
,
\rho_{0}
and velocities
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
of active (fissogens and fusogens) and passive components. Each completed cycle produces two distinct coarse-grained effects: exchange of membrane area and lumenal volume with the organelle, and localized forces exerted on the adjacent cytosolic fluid. The opposing arrows schematically depict these forces as a force dipole for fission and fusion events. More generally, the forcing is represented by a force-free distribution acting across the active layer, characterized by its first and second moments.

II.1
Fission and fusion processes are active mechanochemical pumps

Our coarse-grained hydrodynamic equations for membrane composition and shape are derived by treating fission and fusion as spatially local surface processes and averaging their effects over repeated trafficking events (see Appendix A). Individual trafficking events act over vesicle-sized membrane patches with a characteristic lateral size of approximately
50
–
100
nm, much smaller than the characteristic length
L\sim 1\,\mu\mathrm{m}
over which the membrane fields vary. Their effects can be represented as spatially local contributions relative to the membrane-scale variations. The estimated interval between vesicle-sized trafficking events,
T_{a}\sim 0.01\text{--}0.6
s, is much shorter than the lateral diffusion time,
T_{d}\sim 2\text{--}100
s, and shorter than the shape-relaxation time,
T_{s}\sim 2.5\text{--}50
s (Table
1
, Appendix A). Repeated fission and fusion can therefore maintain the membrane
away from equilibrium. This is the regime considered here. If instead successive trafficking events were separated by times much longer than the membrane-relaxation time, the membrane would relax between events toward a quasi-equilibrium shape at the updated area and volume.

The composition fields on the membrane consist of passive membrane components (lipids and proteins) and the protein machinery associated with fission and fusion, referred to throughout as
fissogens
and
fusogens
. Locally, these compositional fields evolve via recruitment, active mechanochemistry, and dynamics along the membrane. We describe the mechanochemical kinetics of fission and fusion using Markov cycles with multiple intermediate internal states. We adopt an adiabatic approximation in which the populations of these internal states relax rapidly relative to the membrane composition and shape dynamics. They can therefore be treated as remaining in an instantaneous stationary nonequilibrium distribution, with their relative populations determined by the transition rates and the instantaneous total fraction of fissogens or fusogens; as derived in Appendices A.1 and A.5 and illustrated in Fig.
1
.

The transitions between these states do not satisfy detailed balance because the cycle is driven by energy-consuming reactions. For simplicity, we model the biochemical cycles using first-order kinetics with constant transition rates. This neglects possible feedback of membrane elasticity, such as surface tension, on the transition rates
[
48
,
34
,
49
]
. We assume that the molecular machinery required for fission and fusion, together with incoming vesicles for fusion, is locally available and that their transport does not limit the prescribed cycle kinetics. Consequently, the nonequilibrium kinetics generate a local loop current for each trafficking cycle, denoted by
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, where
k=1,2
labels fission and fusion, respectively. The loop current measures the net rate at which the corresponding mechanochemical cycle is completed locally on the membrane (see Appendix A.5). For the prescribed kinetics,
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is proportional to the local abundance of the corresponding fission or fusion machinery, and we denote the proportionality coefficient by the cycle rate
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
.

This cycle rate is determined entirely by the internal transition rates and is proportional to the difference between the products of the transition rates around the cycle in the forward and reverse directions. At thermodynamic equilibrium, detailed balance is restored and the Kolmogorov cycle condition requires these two products to be equal
[
50
]
. Consequently,
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and the loop current vanish. A nonzero loop current therefore reflects the nonequilibrium driving of the mechanochemical cycle. This nonequilibrium cycling also has direct physical consequences: each completed fission (fusion) cycle removes (adds) membrane area and lumenal volume, including solvent and solute cargo, with the corresponding local transfer rates determined by
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and the amounts transferred per completed cycle. Fissogens and fusogens therefore act as
active pumps

[
51
,
36
,
52
]
for membrane area and lumenal volume.

In addition to exchanging membrane area and lumenal volume, each trafficking cycle also exerts a distribution of forces on the surrounding cytosolic fluid. We represent these forces by a body-force density localized within a thin layer of thickness
\Lambda\sim 50
–
100
nm on the cytosolic side of the membrane, extending along the membrane normal
[
39
,
38
,
53
]
. The resulting stresses and flows within this layer transmit the active forcing to the membrane through the hydrodynamic traction exerted by the adjacent fluid. Since
\Lambda/L\ll 1
, the finite active layer can be replaced, to leading order, by a sharp-interface description at the membrane. In this reduction, the moments of the force distribution are retained through effective interfacial stresses, bending moments, and a modified interfacial velocity condition
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
. These effective contributions are described in Section II.B; see Appendices A.7 and A.9 for details.

II.2
Active hydrodynamics of membranes subject to fission and fusion

The density of active membrane components is given by
\rho_{k}
, where
k=1
,
2
labels fissogens and fusogens, respectively, whilst the density of passive membrane components is
\rho_{0}
(see Fig.
1
). In our coarse-grained description, the membrane is incompressible, with a constant total density
\rho=\rho_{0}+\sum_{k}\rho_{k}
. By defining the local areal fractions
\Phi_{0}=\rho_{0}/\rho
and
\Phi_{k}=\rho_{k}/\rho
and assuming that the active components are dilute (
\Phi_{k}\ll\Phi_{0}
), the free energy of the closed organelle membrane
\mathcal{M}
is given by

\mathcal{F}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}F\,\mathrm{d}S
(see Appendix A.4), with

F=\Sigma+2\kappa H^{2}+2\kappa HC_{\mathrm{m}}+V_{\mathrm{m}},

(1)

where
\Sigma
is the surface tension,
\kappa
is the bending rigidity, and
H
is the mean curvature of the membrane. The adiabatic approximation allows the fractions of the internal mechanochemical states to be eliminated in favor of the total areal fractions
\Phi_{k}
. The spontaneous curvature and composition free-energy density consequently take the reduced forms
C_{\mathrm{m}}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\Phi_{0}+\sum_{k}\mathcal{C}_{k}\Phi_{k}
and
V_{\mathrm{m}}=\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\big[\Phi_{0}(\ln\Phi_{0}+\mathcal{E}_{0})+\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}(\ln\Phi_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{k})\big]
. Here,
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is the curvature-coupling strength of the passive components, while
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is the effective curvature-coupling strength obtained by averaging over the internal states of cycle
k
. Similarly,
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the dimensionless chemical-potential parameter of the passive components, while
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is the corresponding effective parameter for cycle
k
, and
b_{0}
is a coarse-graining area scale on the membrane. The explicit expressions for
\mathcal{C}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k}
are derived in Appendix A.5.

The evolution of the membrane areal fractions is governed by passive diffusive transport derived from
\mathcal{F}
and active membrane-area exchange associated with the mechanochemical cycles (Fig.
1
):

\frac{\partial{\Phi}_{k}}{\partial t}+v^{\alpha}{\nabla_{\hskip-1.0pt\alpha}\Phi}_{k}=-\Phi_{k}\mathbb{M}+2\hskip 1.0pt\Omega_{k}\Delta\hskip-0.25ptH+\gamma_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\Delta\hskip-0.5pt\ln\frac{\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{0}},

(2)

where
\gamma_{k}
is the surface diffusivity,
\Omega_{k}
is the curvature-coupling coefficient,
\Delta
is the Laplace–Beltrami operator, and
\nabla_{\hskip-1.0pt\alpha}
is the covariant derivative, with summation over repeated Greek indices implied. The active membrane-area flux is
\mathbb{M}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Phi_{k}
, where
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is the membrane-area transfer rate for cycle
k
, determined by
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and the net area change accumulated across its internal transitions. The corresponding local rate of cycle completion is the loop current
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\Phi_{k}\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, obtained under adiabatic approximation (see Appendix A.5). At equilibrium,
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
and hence
\mathbb{M}=0
. Away from equilibrium,
\mathbb{M}
must be accommodated by the local membrane motion. For an incompressible membrane, this requires

\mathbb{M}=\nabla_{\!\alpha}v^{\alpha}-2Hv.

(3)

where the right-hand side is the surface divergence of the membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}=v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+v\hskip 0.2pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, with tangential components
v^{\alpha}
along the surface basis vectors
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and normal component
v
along the outward unit normal
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
.

The evolution of membrane shape is determined by passive elastic forces, the hydrodynamic traction exerted by the ambient fluids, and effective active stresses associated with fission and fusion. We write the membrane force balance in the sharp-interface description, with the active contributions defined below. By conservation of linear and angular momentum, the force balance at the membrane is given by (Appendix A.8):

\boldsymbol{f}+\nabla_{\alpha}\!\left[\left(\sigma^{\alpha\beta}+b^{\beta}_{\gamma}M^{\gamma\alpha}\right)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\beta}}M^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right]\!=\boldsymbol{0},

(4)

where
\boldsymbol{f}
is the external hydrodynamic force per unit area exerted by the ambient fluids,
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}=\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}+\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{0}
is the total in-plane stress tensor, and
M^{\alpha\beta}=M^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}+M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}
is the total bending moment tensor. The subscripts
0
and
A
denote the passive and active contributions, respectively. The passive elastic stresses are given by (Appendix A.3):

\sigma_{0}^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\partial(\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0ptF)}{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}},\quad M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=\frac{\partial F}{\partial b_{\alpha\beta}},

(5)

where
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
are the metric and curvature tensors, respectively, and
g=\det[g_{\alpha\beta}]
.

To obtain the effective active contributions to the membrane force balance, we assume that the machinery driving fission and fusion generates localized body forces within a finite active layer on the cytosolic side of the membrane. We assume that this force profile has no preferred tangential direction and retain only its component along the outward normal
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. We denote the normal distance from the membrane by height
h
, with
0\leq h\leq\Lambda
, and define the cycle-averaged normal force profile associated with cycle
k
by
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)
, such that
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h
is the normal force per unit membrane area contributed by a slice between
h
and
h+\mathrm{d}h
(see Appendix A.7). We assume that each cycle-specific profile is locally force-free, so that its net force per unit membrane area vanishes:

\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h=0.

(6)

This force-free assumption amounts to representing the molecular machinery driving fission and fusion as generating internal force pairs within the layer, with oppositely directed forces acting at different normal heights. It also assumes that this machinery is not mechanically coupled to a larger structure, such as the cytoskeleton, that could support a nonzero force monopole.

Since
\Lambda/L\ll 1
, the fluid equations within the finite active layer can be expanded in this small ratio. A slice at height
h
occupies the physical volume
J(h)\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}h
, where
J(h)=1-2hH+h^{2}K
and
K
is the Gaussian curvature. Since
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}h
is the force acting within this slice, the corresponding body-force density is
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)/J(h)
. At leading order, this body-force density is balanced by the normal gradient of the fluid pressure
p_{k}
within the finite active layer,
-\partial_{h}p_{k}+{\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)}/{J(h)}=0
. Integrating this balance with respect to
h
yields the pressure profile within the finite active layer. Since pressure is isotropic, it also contributes to the in-plane stress, while tangential viscous stresses are negligible at leading order. Integrating across the layer gives the total in-plane force per unit length. For zero net force, its leading contribution is set by the first moment of the force profile,

\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}h\,\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

(7)

Matching the force transmitted in the finite-layer and sharp-interface descriptions identifies this contribution as an isotropic active membrane stress.

Moreover, because the in-plane stress is distributed over finite distances from the membrane, it also exerts a torque about
h=0
. Integrating this torque across the layer gives the bending moment, which is set by the second moment of the force profile,

\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}h^{2}\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}h.

(8)

For a curved interface, the curvature dependence of
J(h)
causes
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
to contribute also to the curvature-dependent part of the active in-plane stress. Collecting these contributions gives the effective active in-plane stress and bending moment (Appendix A.7):

\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}\!=\mathbb{P}\hskip 1.5ptg^{\alpha\beta}+\mathbb{Q}\,\left(\hskip-0.6ptHg^{\alpha\beta}\!+b^{\alpha\beta}\right),\;\;M^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}\!=\!-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{Q}\,g^{\alpha\beta},

(9)

where
\mathbb{P}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Phi_{k}
and
\mathbb{Q}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Phi_{k}
are the total first and second moments of the active force profile.

In the sharp-interface description, the active layer is no longer resolved explicitly, and the inner (
-
) and outer (
+
) ambient fluids satisfy the unforced incompressible Stokes equations
[
57
]
:
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=0
, where the fluid stress tensors are

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=-p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\boldsymbol{I}+\eta\big[\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right)+\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right)^{\!\mathsf{T}}\big],

(10)

and
\boldsymbol{I}
is the identity tensor, while
p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}
are the corresponding fluid pressures and velocities. For simplicity, we choose the inner and outer fluids to have the same shear viscosity
\eta
. The hydrodynamic force per unit area
\boldsymbol{f}
in Eq. (
4
) is given by the jump in fluid traction across the sharp interface:

\boldsymbol{f}=\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right)\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\big|_{\,\mathcal{M}}

(11)

Accordingly,
\boldsymbol{f}
is computed solely from the stresses of the unforced bulk fluids. The force and torque transmitted through the active layer are accounted for separately through the effective membrane stress and bending moment. The finite active layer also modifies the kinematic coupling between the membrane and the bulk fluid. Although no slip holds at the physical membrane, flow across the layer can cause the fluid velocity at its outer edge to differ from the membrane velocity. Together with volume transport across the membrane, this gives the following interfacial velocity condition:

\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}-{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\big|_{\,\mathcal{M}}.

(12)

The tangential term
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
accounts for the velocity offset due to flow within the finite active layer. Variations in the active force profile along the membrane generate pressure gradients and shear within the layer. When the layer is reduced to a sharp interface, the resulting offset appears as the slip velocity (see Appendix A.7),

\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=\frac{1}{2\eta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\nabla_{\!\alpha}\mathbb{Q}.

(13)

The normal term,
-{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, instead represents the velocity difference associated with volume transport across the membrane. The scalar flux
{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
gives the local rate of volume transfer per unit area (see Appendix A.10),

{{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}=\mathbb{V}-w_{0}\left(\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-P_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right),}

(14)

where
\mathbb{V}=\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is the active volume flux generated by fission and fusion, with
\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
the volume-transfer rate for cycle
k
. The second term describes passive osmotic permeation, with
w_{0}>0
the solvent-permeation coefficient, and
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
and
P_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
the osmotic and hydrostatic pressure differences between the outside and inside of the organelle, respectively. Positive values of
{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
increase the enclosed volume, so that
\mathrm{d}V/\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathcal{M}}{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\,\mathrm{d}S
.

The composition dynamics (
2
), local membrane-area balance (
3
), membrane force–balance (
4
), incompressible Stokes equations for the inner and outer bulk fluids, and interfacial velocity boundary condition (
12
) form a closed set of equations that describe the organelle morphodynamics, see the flowchart in Fig.
5
. For a given interfacial traction jump
\boldsymbol{f}
, the inner and outer fluid flows can be obtained from a boundary-integral representation over the membrane, as derived in Appendix A.11.

Even before solving the governing equations, the force balance already reveals how activity modifies the effective membrane mechanics. In its normal component, the terms linear in mean curvature define an activity-renormalized surface tension whose direct mechanical contribution is set by the total first moment
\mathbb{P}
, see Eq. (
183
). Likewise, the total second moment
\mathbb{Q}
shifts the effective spontaneous curvature, giving the activity-renormalized form in Eq. (
184
).

To illustrate the possible signs of these active moments, consider a two-point force distribution, with a distal force associated with the bud or transport vesicle at
h=\Lambda
and an equal and opposite force acting closer to the mother membrane. During fission, coat-driven budding and scission can generate forces that separate the nascent vesicle from the mother membrane
[
14
,
58
]
, whereas during fusion, docking and SNARE-mediated zippering pull the incoming vesicle towards the membrane
[
14
,
59
]
. We therefore represent fission by an extensile force dipole and fusion by a contractile one. The same force polarity can arise when the distal force is dominated by Stokesian drag, since the force exerted by the bud or vesicle on the fluid follows its normal motion, outward during fission and inward during fusion. Since the compensating force acts closer to the membrane, both
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
have the sign of the distal force, giving
\mathrm{sgn}(\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})=\mathrm{sgn}(\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})=(-1)^{k+1}
. More generally, this sign relation need not hold, since the two moments weight the force profile
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)
differently and may have either sign independently of one another.

For the particular case described above, these force moment signs imply that the first-moment contribution raises the activity-renormalized surface tension during fission and lowers it during fusion, whereas the second-moment contribution shifts the activity-renormalized spontaneous curvature in opposite directions for the two processes. Sufficiently strong fusion activity can reduce the effective tension and potentially drive it negative. This tendency is qualitatively consistent with theoretical studies of membranes exchanging material
[
27
,
28
]
and with
in vitro
observations of lipid uptake and membrane-area regulation
[
60
,
61
]
. The associated curvature preference can also favor enrichment of membrane components in regions of matching curvature, consistent with curvature-dependent partitioning at highly curved Golgi cisternal rims
[
62
]
.

Figure 2:

Generic instabilities in the composition and shape of a membrane organelle.
(a) Normalized axisymmetric spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell,0}(\theta)
, illustrating the spherical decomposition of membrane composition fields (color map) and shape distortions from a homogeneous sphere (dashed circle). (b) Stability diagram corresponding to composition perturbations for each spherical harmonic, in terms of the fission and fusion rates, made dimensionless using the characteristic diffusion time

. The onset of drift instability (
\ell=1
) is marked by the solid red line, while the boundaries of the higher-order (
\ell>1
) shape instabilities are shown as red dashed lines. Both green and blue regions correspond to the linearly stable regime, with the latter showing stable damped oscillations (each mode is shown by the dotted lines in the blue region). Insets show the growth rates
\lambda^{\psi}_{\ell=1}
of composition at two fixed values of
A
as a function of
\zeta
. (c) Stability diagram corresponding to shape perturbations. The fission and fusion active moments are taken to have equal magnitudes and opposite signs and are parameterized by the dimensionless quantity
Q
. The insets show the growth rates
\lambda_{\ell}^{u}
as a function of
\ell
at fixed values of
\zeta
,
Q
, and dimensionless bending rigidity
\mathcal{K}=10
. Red lines show the onset of shape instabilities for the different
\ell
modes. The composition stability diagram in (b) is independent of the active moment
Q
, whereas the shape stability diagram in (c) is independent of the dimensionless fission rate
A
. In both panels (b) and (c), the homogeneous base-state areal fraction is fixed at
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
.

We emphasize that activity affects the effective surface tension and spontaneous curvature in two distinct ways. The active force profile contributes directly through its first and second moments,
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, respectively. Material exchange associated with fission and fusion provides an additional, indirect contribution by changing the local fractions
\Phi_{k}
, whose dynamics depend on the membrane-area transfer rates
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
.

II.3
Spontaneous drift and shape instabilities

The set of nonlinear equations can be solved using a suitable numerical scheme (see Fig.
5
), such as boundary integral methods
[
63
]
. Instead, we find it instructive to study the dynamics perturbatively about the homogeneous spherical nonequilibrium steady-state (see Appendix B). We parameterize the membrane shape as
\boldsymbol{R}=R\left[1+u\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\right]\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
is the radial unit vector, and
u(\theta,\varphi)
is a small radial distortion about the undeformed sphere of radius
R
, with
\theta
and
\varphi
as the inclination and azimuthal angles. We perturb the composition fields
\Phi_{k}=\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k}(\theta,\varphi)
about the homogeneous value
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
.
The velocity fields of the fluid and membrane are expanded to linear order, with Eq. (
12
) appearing as a boundary condition on the undeformed sphere. Lamb’s solution to the Stokes equations
[
38
]
allows us to express the leading-order inner and outer fluid flows,
\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
, in terms of the membrane dynamics. Here, the superscript
{\scriptstyle(\mathtt{1})}
denotes the first-order contribution in the perturbative expansion about the homogeneous spherical base state. By expanding Eqs. (
2
) and (
4
) in spherical harmonics
Y_{\ell,m}(\theta,\varphi)
, we obtain the linear equations for the individual shape and composition amplitudes
u_{\ell,m}
and
\Psi_{k,\ell,m}
, together with the corresponding surface-tension amplitudes
\Sigma_{\ell,m}
; see Appendix B for details. At linear order, the azimuthal modes
m
are degenerate and obey the same equations for a given
\ell
. For compactness, we use
u_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{k,\ell}
to denote the complete contributions of degree
\ell
, constructed by summing over all
m
as defined in Eqs. (
222
) and (
341
) of Appendix B.

This approach allows us to study the instabilities of membrane composition and shape (Appendix B.7), arising from spatial modulations in composition and coupling to hydrodynamic flows induced by membrane stresses (Fig.
2
and
3
). The far-field decay of the surrounding fluid flow depends on the mode: the force-free
\ell=1
flow decays as
1/r^{3}
, whereas the
\ell=2
flow decays as
1/r^{2}
(Appendix B.9); see Fig.
3
(c) and (i). Our key result is the spontaneous drift instability of the organelle (
\ell=1
), along with higher–order shape instabilities (
\ell\geq 2
) in the form of flattened sacs and tubular ramified structures.

We construct stability diagrams, shown in Fig.
2
(b) and (c), in terms of the dimensionless fission parameter
A=-\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}R^{2}/\gamma
, the ratio
\zeta=-{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}/{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}
between fusion and fission rates, and the dimensionless active second moments
Q_{k}=\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}/(\gamma\eta)
, with both the dimensionless bending rigidity
\mathcal{K}=\kappa/(R\gamma\eta)
and homogeneous base-state areal fraction
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
of passive membrane components held fixed. Although the two second moments are independent in general, we restrict to the one-parameter case
Q_{2}=-Q_{1}=Q
, corresponding to equal magnitudes and opposite signs, with
Q
allowed to take either sign
1
1

1

For the illustrative two-point force model,
Q<0
corresponds to an extensile force dipole during fission and a contractile one during fusion, whereas
Q>0
reverses this arrangement.
. We also take the surface diffusivities to be equal,
\gamma_{k}=\gamma
, and neglect spontaneous curvature terms and active volume fluxes associated with fission and fusion, which are subdominant for transport vesicles much smaller than the organelle. We also assume a uniform, isotonic base state, with vanishing osmotic pressure
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
, and set the isotropic
\ell=0
perturbations to zero, excluding uniform dilation or shrinkage at linear order; see Appendix B.5 and B.6. We thus consider only the nonuniform modes, with the
\ell=1
mode corresponding to organelle translation, while modes with
\ell\geq 2
describe shape deformations of the membrane.

The spontaneous drift instability occurs when

A\geq A_{\star}=\frac{2(1+\zeta)^{2}}{\zeta(\zeta-1)(1\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip-1.0pt\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta>1,

(15)

where
A_{\star}
is the critical dimensionless fission parameter, corresponding to a threshold organelle radius. In smaller compartments, lateral diffusion rapidly homogenizes composition differences, whereas in larger compartments these differences can build up and drive the instability. To understand the spontaneous organelle drift, we solve the linearized equations for the degree-
\ell
components
u_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{k,\ell}
, obtaining the membrane stresses and fluid flows to linear order. Fig.
3
highlights this interplay between vesicular flux and shape deformations through the induced fluid flows and membrane stresses.

To make the physical origin and direction of the drift explicit, we take the perturbation in membrane-area exchange to have a dipolar profile aligned with the
z
-axis, as in Fig.
3
. For the translational
\ell=1
mode, this corresponds to retaining only the axisymmetric
m=0
component; since the
\ell=1
modes are rotationally degenerate, this choice is without loss of generality. The resulting imbalance between the rates at which membrane area is added on one side of the organelle and removed on the opposite side is described by the membrane-area flux profile
\mathbb{M}^{\mathsf{(1)}}_{\ell=1}(\theta)=\sum_{k}\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Psi_{k,1,0}Y_{1,0}(\theta)
. The underlying compositional perturbation enters the linearized shape equation and generates the corresponding translational displacement. We define
V^{\mathsf{(1)}}_{\mathrm{cm}}\equiv\boldsymbol{V}^{\mathsf{(1)}}_{\mathrm{cm}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{z}
as the center-of-mass velocity along the chosen
z
-axis, which at linear order is

V^{\mathsf{(1)}}_{\mathrm{cm}}={\frac{1}{\sqrt{12\pi}}}\sum_{k}\left(R\,\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-\frac{1}{\eta R}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right){\Psi_{k,1,0}}.

(16)

The two terms in this expression have distinct physical origins. The term proportional to
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is kinematic: local membrane-area addition and removal drive a tangential membrane flow from the region of net addition towards the region of net removal. This membrane motion exerts viscous traction on the surrounding fluid, causing the force-free organelle to translate in the opposite direction, towards the region of net addition, as in treadmilling. The term proportional to
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is mechanical: spatial gradients of the active second moment drive shear within the finite active layer, producing a Marangoni-like slip velocity along the membrane. These two contributions can reinforce or oppose one another. The direction of motion relative to the incident membrane-area (vesicular) flux is determined by the competition between these kinematic and mechanical contributions. We call the motion
retrograde
when it follows the treadmilling direction, and
anterograde
when it is reversed. For the parameters used in Fig.
3
(d), the
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
-dependent contribution opposes and dominates the treadmilling contribution, reversing the direction expected from membrane-area exchange alone.

Figure 4:

Weakly nonlinear analysis reveals stable
organelle motion and shape.

(a) Schematic phase portrait in the space of center-of-mass velocity
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and membrane-area flux amplitudes
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=1}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=2}
. Trajectories along the unstable manifold (pink) move away from the stationary fixed point (
V_{\mathrm{cm}}^{\ast}=0
), while those along the stable manifold (green) approach the drifting fixed point (
V_{\mathrm{cm}}^{\ast}\neq 0
).
(b) Streamlines of the nonlinear dynamics in the
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=1}
–
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=2}
plane, with the remaining dynamical variables held at their values at the stable fixed point. Color indicates the magnitude of the vector field governing the evolution of these two amplitudes. In contrast, the red curve shows the projection of a trajectory of the full reduced system, with all five dynamical amplitudes evolving subject to the fixed-volume and active-component conservation constraints. Starting from an initial state with
V_{\mathrm{cm}}(0)=0
within the basin of attraction of the stable drifting fixed point, the system relaxes to steady anterograde prolate motion (black point). The right-hand insets show the time evolution of
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
,
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=1}
, and
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=2}
along the red trajectory. Here,
\zeta=1.5
,
A=150
,
Q_{1}=-125
, and
Q_{2}=125
.
(c) Phase diagrams in the
\zeta
–
A
plane for the two choices of active second moments
(Q_{1},Q_{2})
. The phases are stationary sphere (SS), stationary prolate (SP), anterograde prolate (AP), anterograde oblate (AO), retrograde oblate (RO), and retrograde prolate (RP), with SP describing a stationary state (
V_{\mathrm{cm}}=0
) with finite prolate deformation. The dashed curve marks the linear
\ell=1
instability threshold of the homogeneous sphere, and the marked point in the left-hand diagram corresponds to the parameters used in (b). A region where no stable fixed points were found is also indicated (red). The insets beside the right-hand phase diagram show
\Xi=\operatorname{sgn}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell=1\!})V_{\mathrm{cm}}
along cuts at
A=240
(top) and
A=280
(bottom). In both (b) and (c),
\mathcal{K}=10
and
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
. Here, time, velocity, and membrane-area flux amplitudes are nondimensionalized by
R^{2}/\gamma
,
\gamma/R
, and
\gamma/R^{2}
, respectively.

Fig.
3
(e) shows transitions from stationary
\to
anterograde
\to
retrograde as we increase
A
, keeping
\zeta
and
Q
fixed. Along this path, the stationary state first becomes unstable, while at larger
A
the competition between the kinematic and mechanical contributions described above can reverse the drift direction. Similarly, the higher-order (
\ell\geq 2
) shape instabilities are driven by the imbalance
\zeta
and active moment
Q
, with their onset marked by the critical lines in Fig.
2
(c). The lowest shape mode,
\ell=2
, produces prolate or oblate deformations.

To find the shape corresponding to the
\ell=2
mode, we solve the linear equations for
u_{\ell=2}
and
\Psi_{k,\ell=2}
. We consider axisymmetric perturbations about the same
z
-axis used for drift, taking the initial quadrupolar membrane-area flux amplitude to be positive, with membrane addition near the poles and removal near the equator. Such a pattern may arise from vesicle exchange with neighboring compartments on opposite sides of the organelle. The deformation is prolate for
u_{2}>0
, elongated along the
z
-axis, and oblate for
u_{2}<0
, flattened along that axis. Reversing the initial
\ell=2
perturbation reverses both the flux and
u_{2}
. Fig.
3
(k) shows transitions from stationary sphere
\to
moving sphere
\to
moving oblate
\to
moving prolate as we increase
A
while holding
\zeta
and
Q
fixed. Therefore, combining Figs.
3
(e) and (k), we find that, within the linear theory, spherical and oblate shapes can accompany either anterograde or retrograde motion, whereas prolate shapes occur only with retrograde motion. Figs.
3
(f) and (l) show that this ordering persists throughout the
A
–
\zeta
–
Q
parameter space.

The combined drift and shape phase diagrams reveal two features of particular biological relevance. First, they permit anterograde motion accompanied by an oblate linear response, reminiscent of Golgi cisternal progression
[
15
,
16
]
. Second, the phase diagrams suggest how selective inhibition of fission can reverse organelle motion. We model this by reducing the fission cycle rate, namely,
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\to\chi\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, where
0<\chi\leq 1
, while leaving the fusion cycle rate unchanged. Hence,
A\to\chi A
and
\zeta\to\zeta/\chi
. Within the reduced parameter setting used to construct these phase diagrams, this inhibition defines a one-parameter trajectory through the four-dimensional space spanned by
A
,
\zeta
, and the two independent active second moments.
The arrows in Fig.
3
(f) and (l) show the projection of this trajectory onto the reduced
A
–
\zeta
–
Q
space, with
Q=-Q_{1}
in the plotted parameterization, so that
Q\to\chi Q
. The displayed boundary surfaces correspond to the equal-magnitude, opposite-sign choice defined above, whereas the arrows show the projection of the full trajectory onto this reduced phase space. This projection indicates how fission inhibition can drive an anterograde organelle into the retrograde regime. The same reduction in fission relative to fusion may also lower the activity–renormalized surface tension and, if sufficiently strong, drive it negative, potentially leading to a break-up of the cisterna
[
61
,
60
,
27
]
. The predicted retrograde motion and cisternal break-up are consistent with Brefeldin A treatment
[
18
,
65
,
66
]
, which inhibits vesicle coat formation and hence fission, causing retrograde redistribution and fragmentation of Golgi membranes, followed by their fusion with the endoplasmic reticulum (ER). This is also consistent with the observed fragmentation of Golgi cisternae in fibroblast cells upon loss of adhesion from a substrate
[
26
]
. To our knowledge, this is the first theoretical justification from first principles that may explain these experimental observations.

II.4
Weakly Nonlinear Analysis

The spontaneous drift instability identified above originates in the membrane composition. In the unstable regime, the growing compositional asymmetry generates membrane stresses and hydrodynamic flows that move the organelle. The preceding phase diagrams classify motion and shape according to the dominant modes of the linearized dynamics, but these perturbations eventually grow beyond the validity of the linear approximation. We thus extend the composition dynamics beyond linear order to investigate whether nonlinear interactions can arrest their growth and which states ultimately persist; see Fig.
4
(a).

To study steadily drifting states, we work in a frame comoving with the organelle. In the laboratory frame, the
\ell=1
mode represents rigid translation only at linear order; at higher orders, translation mixes this mode with higher spherical harmonics. The comoving frame allows us to separate the motion of the organelle as a whole from changes in its intrinsic shape and membrane composition. As in the linear analysis, we neglect active volume fluxes and consider an isotonic reference state. Since passive solvent permeation is slow on the timescales of interest, we fix the enclosed volume at its homogeneous reference value. We also conserve the total numbers of fissogens and fusogens on the membrane, as required by the general model (Appendix C). Here, the excess membrane area is determined by the deformed shape rather than imposed as an additional constraint.

We perform a Galerkin expansion of the composition dynamics in axisymmetric spherical harmonics, retaining modes up to
\ell=2
and nonlinear terms through cubic order in the composition dynamics and global constraints, while keeping the mechanical shape response linear (Appendix C). The resulting system has five evolving amplitudes: the dipolar (
\Psi_{k,\ell=1}
) and quadrupolar (
\Psi_{k,\ell=2}
) composition amplitudes for
k=1,2
, together with the quadrupolar shape amplitude
u_{2}
. The fixed-volume constraint and conservation of fissogen and fusogen numbers determine the three isotropic amplitudes
\Psi_{1,0}
,
\Psi_{2,0}
, and
u_{0}
algebraically in terms of these evolving modes. Nonlinear coupling between harmonics introduces feedback absent from the linear theory. For example, a growing dipolar compositional asymmetry induces isotropic and quadrupolar responses, which in turn influence the dipolar dynamics. This feedback can arrest the growth of the instability at finite amplitude.

By numerically determining the fixed points of the reduced system and assessing their linear stability, we identify stable stationary and steadily drifting states across parameter space. Fig.
4
(b) illustrates relaxation towards one such state: the organelle develops a finite drift velocity and settles into stable anterograde prolate motion, starting from an initial condition with
V_{\mathrm{cm}}(0)=0
within its basin of attraction. We classify the direction of motion by the signed order parameter
\Xi=V_{\mathrm{cm}}\operatorname{sgn}(\mathbb{M}_{\ell=1})
, with
\Xi<0
for anterograde and
\Xi>0
for retrograde motion, while
u_{2}>0
and
u_{2}<0
identify prolate and oblate shapes. By reflection symmetry, each stable drifting solution has an equally stable counterpart with opposite
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\ell=1}
but unchanged
u_{2}
, so that both have the same
\Xi
and belong to the same phase.

Allowing
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
to vary independently, we extend the fixed-point analysis across parameter space to obtain the phase diagrams in Fig.
4
(c) for two representative choices of the active second moments. The left-hand diagram, with
Q_{1}=-125
and
Q_{2}=125
, corresponding to
Q=125
in Fig.
3
, contains four phases: stationary sphere (SS), anterograde prolate (AP), retrograde prolate (RP), and stationary prolate (SP). In the SP phase, the drift velocity and dipolar flux vanish, while the quadrupolar composition and flux sustain a finite prolate deformation. The right-hand diagram, with
Q_{1}=250
and
Q_{2}=-575
, contains four phases: stationary sphere (SS), anterograde prolate (AP), anterograde oblate (AO), and retrograde oblate (RO). Of particular significance are the stable AO states, which combine anterograde motion with flattening along the direction of motion, consistent with Golgi cisternal progression. Unlike the left-hand diagram, the right-hand diagram also contains a region where no stable fixed points were found despite the nonlinear coupling.

To examine the transitions between these phases, we follow the nonlinear fixed points as
\zeta
varies at fixed
A
,
Q_{1}
, and
Q_{2}
. The insets beside the right-hand phase diagram in Fig.
4
(c) show
\Xi
along cuts at
A=240
(top) and
A=280
(bottom), revealing a region where both stationary and finite-amplitude drifting states are stable near the linear instability threshold, consistent with a subcritical onset of organelle drift. Along the
A=280
cut, the AP–AO shape transition precedes the AO–RO drift reversal, as the organelle changes from prolate to oblate while moving anterogradely before reversing its direction of motion. The corresponding changes in drift velocity, shape deformation, and membrane-area flux amplitudes are detailed in Appendix C.4 (Fig.
17
). For the left-hand diagram in Fig.
4
(c), the bifurcation curves along the
A=300
cut also reveal a discontinuous onset of drift from SS to RP, followed by an RP–AP drift reversal and a continuous AP–SP transition, in which the drift velocity and dipolar flux vanish smoothly while the prolate deformation and quadrupolar flux remain nonzero (Fig.
18
).

The phase regions in Fig.
4
(c) are two-dimensional cross sections of a larger four-dimensional parameter space
(\zeta,A,Q_{1},Q_{2})
. To explore the broader phase structure, we determine, at fixed
\zeta
and
A
, the critical pair
(Q_{1}^{\ast},Q_{2}^{\ast})
at which the drift-reversal (
V_{\mathrm{cm}}=0
) and prolate–oblate (
u_{2}=0
) boundaries intersect. These conditions yield two equations linear in the active second moments, allowing
Q_{1}^{\ast}
and
Q_{2}^{\ast}
to be expressed in terms of the composition amplitudes. Substituting these into the remaining nonlinear amplitude equations, we solve for the fixed points and retain only stable nontrivial solutions. The intersecting boundaries locally divide parameter space into four regimes combining anterograde or retrograde motion with prolate or oblate morphology. Fig.
16
in Appendix C.4 shows how the critical moments vary across the
\zeta
–
A
plane. We find
Q_{2}^{\ast}<0
throughout the region of stable critical solutions, while
Q_{1}^{\ast}
is predominantly positive at moderate
\zeta
and becomes negative at larger
\zeta
. Stable AO states occur predominantly for
Q_{1}>0
and
Q_{2}<0
, as illustrated by the right-hand phase diagram in Fig.
4
(c). The narrow AO region in this cross section does not necessarily reflect its extent in the full parameter space. These signs are consistent with the illustrative two-point force model discussed above, in which fission is associated with an extensile force pair and fusion with a contractile one.

The center-of-mass speeds predicted in the AO region of Fig.
4
(c) reach up to approximately
250~\mathrm{nm/min}
and average around
130~\mathrm{nm/min}
across AO states, using representative physical parameters from Tables I and II (see Appendix C.4). For comparison, the effective cis-to-trans cargo progression through the Golgi is
\sim 10~\mathrm{nm/min}

[
67
]
. Since cargo progression does not directly measure physical cisternal motion, a quantitative comparison remains difficult. We expect interactions with the cytoskeleton and neighbouring cisternae, which are not included in our model, to slow cisternal motion. Such a comparison would require extending our model to account for these mechanical constraints and for how cargo exchange and biochemical maturation govern cargo progression.

III
Discussion, caveats and outlook

In this paper, we provide a systematic analysis of organelle shapes subjected to nonequilibrium forces and hydrodynamic flows driven by energy-consuming active fission and fusion. Our work represents a step forward towards a quantitative study of the nonequilibrium mechanics of membrane-bound organelles, such as the Golgi apparatus, within trafficking pathways. Here, we focus on the maintenance of cisternal shape under continuous trafficking and on the instabilities that drive shape changes and organelle motion. While our approach centers on the dynamics of existing organelle structures, the question of
de novo
nonequilibrium assembly remains an intriguing direction for extending these concepts. We have demonstrated that the fluid, deformable nature of the membrane provides a coupling between composition and shape, which drives spontaneous drift and shape instabilities; nonlinearities in the fields result in stable organelle motion that depends on the levels of activity and membrane-area fluxes.

Over the years, there have been several proposed models of Golgi trafficking, such as vesicle transport, cisternal progression and their variants, often taken to be “diametrically” opposed
[
68
,
15
]
. Recent studies have provided strong evidence for cisternal progression in the budding yeast
[
44
,
45
]
. An outstanding question in the field has been:
“What drives cisternal progression?”

[
15
]
; our work may provide an answer to this, and identifies, for the first time, nonequilibrium fission-fusion as a driving force for cisternal movement.
An important result of our study, Fig.
4
(c), with far-reaching implications for intracellular patterning
[
2
]
, is that cisternal progression and vesicle transport can be realised as the moving and stationary phases, respectively, arising from the same underlying nonequilibrium dynamics of fission and fusion.

Another unresolved question has been
“What processes define cisternal morphology?”

[
15
]
, particularly, how Golgi cisternae maintain their flattened shapes. Earlier theoretical studies
[
69
,
33
]
proposed membrane adhesion in stacked cisternae as a cause for the flattened cisternal shapes. However, this is in conflict with two lines of evidence: (i) the observation of flattened shapes in unstacked cisternae in
S. cerevisiae

[
70
]
, and (ii) isolated Golgi cisternae in mammalian cells that have been unstacked by protease treatment
[
71
]
maintain their flattened shapes. This suggests that stacking is not likely the cause of flattened cisternae. Our results show that fission–fusion stresses can produce oblate deformations and sustain stable oblate states in the weakly nonlinear regime, suggesting a mechanism for maintaining flattened Golgi cisternae. These stresses can also induce higher-order shape instabilities, which may contribute to the ramified morphology of cisternae within Golgi stacks. Indeed, an implication of our study is that the Golgi morphology and dynamics is intimately associated with its primary function, namely glycosylation, which is dependent on the nonequilibrium trafficking of vesicles
[
12
,
72
]
. We emphasise that the key results highlighted here could not have been derived from a purely kinetic approach and require a careful treatment of active mechanics and hydrodynamics, as attempted here. In particular, active force moments control both anterograde–retrograde drift reversal and oblate–prolate shape transitions.

We now list the caveats regarding the present work and suggest ways of addressing them. One major shortcoming is that the transition rates in the fission–fusion cycles have been taken to be independent of the state of the organelle, such as surface tension and local composition or the availability of fissogens and fusogens. Such mechanochemical feedback may play an important role in the homeostatic regulation of organelle size.

Another important cellular aspect that we have ignored is the influence of the cytoskeleton. The presence of the cytoskeletal meshwork can result in a screening of hydrodynamic flows in the exterior fluid generated by active membrane stresses on the organelle. Direct interactions between the cytoskeleton and the membrane could suppress higher-order shape instabilities. In addition, in cells with stacked Golgi cisternae, the cytoskeletal scaffolding could lead to a suppression of the trafficking-induced cisternal drift instability.

We hope the ideas presented here will stimulate further theoretical studies and careful biophysical experiments on a system of organelles, such as Golgi and endosomes, in the trafficking pathway. An exciting challenge is to construct synthetic realisations of nonequilibrium soft fluidics capable of controlled fission and fusion.

Appendix

Appendix A
Nonequilibrium Model and Active Hydrodynamic Equations

Figure 5:
Flow chart summarizing the model. Starting at box
0
, each fission or fusion cycle is characterized by an cycle rate
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, determined by the underlying biochemical transition kinetics. This converts the net area and volume changes accumulated over one completed cycle into the transfer rates
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, and similarly sets the cycle-averaged active force profile. The latter is described in terms of the lowest nontrivial moments of the force profile normal to the membrane; namely, its first and second moments
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, respectively. The dynamical variables are the areal fractions
\Phi_{k}(\xi^{\alpha},t)
of active components (fissogens and fusogens) that reside on the membrane surface, which itself evolves, being described by the shape
\boldsymbol{R}(\xi^{\alpha},t)
embedded in a fluid with shear viscosity
\eta
. Here,
\xi^{\alpha}
are curvilinear coordinates that parameterize the two-dimensional surface of the compartment. Initial data for the composition and shape allow us to compute the in-plane stress tensor
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}
and the bending moment tensor
M^{\alpha\beta}
, which include passive contributions (due to the membrane elasticity and entropy-of-mixing terms), as well as active stresses that depend on
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
. The membrane traction balance couples these stresses to the hydrodynamic tractions exerted by the inner and outer ambient fluids, which satisfy the incompressible Stokes equations. Together with the membrane boundary conditions, the traction balance allows us to determine the fluid pressures
p_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{r},t)
and the fluid flows
\boldsymbol{V}_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{r},t)
at each point
\boldsymbol{r}
, using a boundary integral representation. All of the terms in
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}
and
M^{\alpha\beta}
are known, apart from the local surface tension
\Sigma(\xi^{\alpha},t)
, which acts as a Lagrange multiplier for the local membrane-area balance; the surface divergence of the membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
must equal the total active membrane-area flux,
\mathbb{M}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Phi_{k}
. The traction balance, fluid equations, slip boundary condition, and local membrane-area balance are solved self-consistently to determine the fluid fields, surface tension, and membrane velocity. The slip velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=\frac{1}{2\eta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\nabla_{\!\alpha}\mathbb{Q}
, where the total second moment
\mathbb{Q}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Phi_{k}
, arises from flow within the active layer, whereas the volume flux per unit area
{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
includes passive leakage of solvent through the membrane and the active volume flux
\mathbb{V}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Phi_{k}
. The passive volume flux is driven by the difference between the osmotic pressure
\Pi_{\Delta}
and the hydrostatic pressure across the interface. Once the coupled mechanical and hydrodynamic problem is solved, the membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
is obtained together with the surface tension
\Sigma
. This velocity can be decomposed into its tangential and normal components,
v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and
v\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, respectively. The tangential component tells us how the membrane constituents are advected on the deformed surface. The material derivative of the compositional fields is thus given by
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+v^{\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}
, where
\nabla_{\alpha}
denotes the covariant derivative. Here, the evolution of the areal fractions
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{k}
is governed by the surface diffusion of membrane components and the active exchange of membrane area, with the rates given by
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
. From an algorithmic viewpoint, this allows us to compute the areal fractions
\Phi_{k}
at the next time-step. Similarly, by using the normal velocity
v\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, we can evolve the surface to determine its shape at the subsequent time point. This method of time-marching describes a scheme for numerically solving the system of equations, starting from some initial data for the composition fields and the shape. Note that we have assumed that the rates entering the internal cycle kinetics are
a priori
given and not regulated by feedback from the membrane mechanics, allowing us to operate in a regime where the transitions can be modeled by first-order kinetics with constant transition rates.

Effective organelle size

R\sim 1~\mu\mathrm{m}

[
73
,
69
]

Transport vesicle radius

r=30
–
40~\mathrm{nm}

[
74
,
58
,
75
]

Total area of Golgi

\mathcal{A}_{0}\sim(1\text{--}3)\times 10^{3}~\mu\mathrm{m}^{2}

[
76
]

Turnover time of Golgi

\tau=10
–
25~\mathrm{min}

[
77
,
78
]

Membrane bending rigidity

\kappa\sim(2\text{--}4)\times 10^{-20}~\mathrm{J}

[
79
]

Effective cytoplasmic viscosity

\eta\sim 0.1\text{--}1~\mathrm{Pa}\cdot\mathrm{s}

[
80
]

Effective surface diffusivity

D\sim 0.01\text{--}0.5~\mu\mathrm{m}^{2}/\mathrm{s}

[
81
,
82
]

Trafficking event interval

T_{a}\sim 0.01\text{--}0.6~\mathrm{s}

Surface diffusion time

T_{d}\sim R^{2}/D\sim 2\text{--}100~\mathrm{s}

Shape relaxation time

T_{s}\sim\eta R^{3}/\kappa\sim 2.5\text{--}50~\mathrm{s}

Table 1:

Length scales, membrane mechanical and transport properties, and estimated time scales relevant to Golgi trafficking and cisternal dynamics.
The effective surface diffusivity
D
characterizes the relaxation of membrane composition. The trafficking event interval
T_{a}
is estimated from Golgi-wide turnover, with
f_{0}\sim 0.05
–
0.5
denoting the fraction of Golgi membrane area participating in vesicular turnover, corresponding to an effective turnover area
f_{0}\mathcal{A}_{0}\sim 50
–
1500~\mu\mathrm{m}^{2}
.

We study the role of membrane trafficking in the morphology of subcellular compartments, such as those encountered within the eukaryotic cell. These continuously interchange material amongst themselves via fission and fusion of small transport vesicles. The fission and fusion are orchestrated by a highly complex biochemical network of proteins driven by energy-consuming processes.

Table I contains a list of representative physiological quantities and ranges, which help us to determine the appropriate length and time scales in our system. This includes the time-scale associated with the lateral redistribution of membrane composition (
T_{d}
); the time-scale corresponding to the relaxation of membrane shape deformations (
T_{s}
); and lastly the time-scale associated with fission and fusion events of transport vesicles (
T_{a}
). Here,
R
is the radius of the spherical reference state used in the model and provides an effective measure of the organelle size. We estimate the surface-diffusion and shape-relaxation time scales from the corresponding scaling relations,
T_{d}\sim R^{2}/D
and
T_{s}\sim\eta R^{3}/\kappa
, respectively, where
D
is the effective surface diffusivity,
\eta
is the effective viscosity of the cytoplasmic fluid, and
\kappa
is the membrane bending rigidity.

The estimate of
T_{a}
requires an additional assumption. The measured Golgi turnover time
\tau
reflects cargo trafficking rather than membrane turnover directly, and we use it here as a proxy for the latter. We introduce an effective turnover fraction
f_{0}
to account for the fraction of the total Golgi membrane area participating in vesicular turnover over this time. We take
f_{0}\sim 0.05
–
0.5
as a broad estimate, reflecting both that cargo turnover does not correspond directly to membrane turnover and that vesicular trafficking is spatially heterogeneous across the Golgi membrane (for example, trafficking is enriched at the cisternal rims
[
73
,
68
]
). If an area
f_{0}\mathcal{A}_{0}
is turned over during a time
\tau
, and each transport vesicle of radius
r
carries a membrane area
4\pi r^{2}
, the characteristic interval between trafficking events is
T_{a}\sim 4\pi r^{2}\tau/(f_{0}\mathcal{A}_{0})
, giving
T_{a}\sim 0.01
–
0.6
s. These estimates indicate that vesicular trafficking across the Golgi occurs on a shorter time scale than surface diffusion and membrane shape relaxation. This implies that repeated fission and fusion can maintain the cisternal membrane out of equilibrium.

To describe this nonequilibrium regime, we develop a coarse-grained hydrodynamic theory coupling fission and fusion, membrane morphology, and the ambient fluid. Fig.
5
provides a flow chart that outlines the model with its key elements and the connections between them. This can be viewed as an algorithmic scheme for computing and time-marching the coupled dynamics, including membrane surface velocities, membrane stresses and surface tension, bulk fluid flows and pressures, as well as membrane composition and shape. The principal equations are boxed for ease of reference.

A.1
Mass balance and kinematics

We consider a closed membrane that is described by
\boldsymbol{R}(\xi^{\alpha},t)
at time
t
, where
\xi^{\alpha}
are the local surface coordinates parametrizing the membrane. The membrane comprises three distinct components: active components associated with membrane fission and fusion, denoted by the superscript
k=1
(fissogens) and
k=2
(fusogens), respectively; and the passive membrane background component, indicated by the subscript ‘0’. Fission and fusion involve highly specific proteins and lipids, which undergo a number of biochemical reactions that primarily require the hydrolysis of either adenosine triphosphate (ATP) or guanosine triphosphate (GTP).

We describe the internal mechanochemical processes of fission and fusion using a nonequilibrium Markov chain model, in which the different intermediary stages of each process are represented by a set of states connected by transitions that form at least one closed loop, or cycle, in state space, as shown in Fig.
6
. The completion of one such cycle corresponds to a single fission or fusion event. Starting from an idle state denoted by
n=0
, the membrane constituents associated with each cycle
k
can enter a sequence of intermediate states indexed by
n\in\{1,2,3,\dots,\aleph_{k}\}
, where
\aleph_{k}
is the number of internal states associated with that cycle. The transition from the final state back to the first closes the loop and completes the cycle. The system may then proceed directly into another cycle or first return to the idle state
n=0
, where it remains for a dwell time before a new cycle begins, as in Fig.
6
. This dwell time therefore represents the waiting time between two successive fission (or fusion) events.

Figure 6:
Kinetic diagram corresponding to the biochemical cycles of fission or fusion, where the number of internal active states is
\hskip 0.5pt\aleph_{k}=4
, in addition to the idle state
n=0
. The diagram therefore contains 10 possible directed transitions. Here, the forward (backward) transitions are indicated by the blue (red) arrows, and each has a first-order transition rate associated with it. We denote the latter by
r_{nm}
for
m\!\rightarrow n
(superscript
k
is omitted here).

The density of active membrane component
k
in the
n
-th internal state at time
t
is denoted by
\rho^{k}_{n}\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)
, whereas the density of the passive background component is given by
\rho_{0}\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)
. The total membrane density is written as

\rho\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)=\rho_{0}\!\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)+\sum_{k,n}\,\rho^{k}_{n}\!\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)\!,

(17)

where we use the notation that
\sum_{k,n}\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)=\sum_{k}\sum^{\aleph_{k}}_{n=0}\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)
. Moreover, the barycentric velocity of the membrane in the local surface basis is given by
\boldsymbol{v}=v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+v\hskip 0.2pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, with
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
and
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
as the normal and in-plane tangent vectors to the membrane, respectively (see Appendix D.1 for definitions). This barycentric velocity is defined by

\rho\boldsymbol{v}=\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}+\sum_{k,n}\rho^{k}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n},

(18)

where the background membrane velocity can be decomposed as
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=v^{\alpha}_{0}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+v\hskip 0.2pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, and the velocity of component
k
in state
n
is given by
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}^{k}=v^{k\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{n}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+v\hskip 0.2pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. Notice that the normal velocities of
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n}
must all be the same for all species and their associated internal states.

Furthermore, we denote the in-plane diffusive flux of component
k
in state
n
, by
\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}
, defined as follows:

\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}=\rho^{k}_{n}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n}-\!\boldsymbol{v}\right)=\rho^{k}_{n}\hskip-1.0pt\left(v^{k\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{n}-v^{\alpha}\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=j^{k\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{n}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha},

(19)

which shows that this flux lies solely within the tangent plane of the membrane, with components
j^{k\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{n}
. Similarly, the diffusive flux corresponding to
\rho_{0}
is defined by

\boldsymbol{j}_{0}=\rho_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}\right)\!.

(20)

As a corollary, it can be shown that the summation over all diffusive fluxes in Eqs. (
19
) and (
20
) gives

\boldsymbol{j}_{0}+\sum_{k,n}\,\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}=\mathbf{0}.

(21)

The amount of component
k
in internal state
n
over a membrane patch
\mathcal{S}
changes due to the diffusive flux at its boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
, as well as due to the internal transitions between states
n
and
m
in cycle
k
. Here,
r^{k}_{nm}
denotes the transition rate from state
m
to state
n
in cycle
k
(see Fig.
6
). Thus,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\rho^{k}_{n}\,\mathrm{d}S=-\!\!\int\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}\cdot\,\boldsymbol{\nu}\;\mathrm{d}l\,+\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}\rho\,\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}\,\mathrm{d}S,

(22)

where
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is the outward unit vector tangent to the membrane and normal to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
, with
\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
. The index set
\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-0.5ptn}^{\hskip-0.1ptk}
gives all the internal states of cycle
k
which are directly connected to state
n
; for example, the states one and zero in Fig.
6
have
\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-0.5pt1}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}=\left\{0,2,4\right\}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-0.5pt0}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}=\left\{1\right\}
, respectively. Here, we define the material derivative to be
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\boldsymbol{\cdot})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+v^{\alpha}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\cdot}).
In the last term of Eq. (
22
),
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}
denotes the internal transition current:

\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}=r^{k}_{nm}\phi^{k}_{m}-r^{k}_{mn}\phi^{k}_{n},

(23)

where the internal-state fractions are defined by
\phi^{k}_{n}=\rho^{k}_{n}/\rho
, while the areal fraction of the passive background component is
\Phi_{0}=\rho_{0}/\rho
. Note that
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}
is an antisymmetric object with respect to the lower indices, that is, we have
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}\!=\!-\mathbb{J}^{k}_{mn}
. By using the Reynolds transport theorem on the left-hand-side of (
22
), namely

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathcal{S}}{\rho_{n}^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}S=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{\rho_{n}^{k}}}{\mathrm{d}t}+{\rho_{n}^{k}}\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}\right)\!\mathrm{d}S,

(24)

and then by applying the divergence theorem to the first term on the right-hand-side of Eq. (
22
), the local density balance is

\boxed{\dot{\rho}^{k}_{n}+\rho^{k}_{n}\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=-\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt\big(\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}\big)+\rho\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm},}

(25)

where the dot notation denotes the material derivative. Here, the divergence of the diffusive current can be also written as
\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt\big(\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}\big)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}j^{k\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{n}}
. At equilibrium, detailed balance requires
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{mn}=0
, whereas in a nonequilibrium steady state these currents may be nonzero.

Similarly, the rate of change in the amount of the passive background component is given by

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\rho_{0}\,\mathrm{d}S=-\!\!\int\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\boldsymbol{j}_{0}\cdot\,\boldsymbol{\nu}\;\mathrm{d}l\,+\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\rho\,\mathbb{M}\;\mathrm{d}S,

(26)

where the last term accounts for membrane material removed from (
k\!=\!1
) or added to (
k\!=\!2
) the surface
\mathcal{S}
as a result of the internal chemical reactions. The corresponding total active membrane-area flux is

\mathbb{M}\!=\!\sum_{k,n,m}\mathbb{M}^{k}_{nm}r^{k}_{nm}\phi^{k}_{m},

(27)

with
\sum_{k,n,m}\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)=\sum_{k}\sum^{\aleph_{k}}_{n=0}\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)
, and
\mathbb{M}^{k}_{nm}
characterizes the signed membrane-area transfer associated with a transition from state
m
to
n
in cycle
k
. Again, by means of the Reynolds transport theorem and the divergence theorem, this leads to the following local balance for the background density:

\boxed{\dot{\rho}_{0}+\rho_{0}\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=-\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt\big(\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{j}_{0}\big)+\rho\,\mathbb{M},}

(28)

with
\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt\big(\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{j}_{0}\big)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}j^{\alpha}_{0}}
. Thus, by summing over all densities from Eqs. (
25
) and (
28
), the local balance of the total membrane density can be written as follows:

\boxed{\dot{\rho}+\rho\;\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=\rho\,\mathbb{M}.}

(29)

Notice that the diffusive currents vanish due to Eq. (
21
), and also
\sum_{n}\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}\,\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}=0
as
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}=-\mathbb{J}^{k}_{mn}
.

Using the total-density balance in Eq. (
29
), the dynamics of the areal fraction
\Phi_{0}
is governed by

\dot{\Phi}_{0}=\left(1-\Phi_{0}\right)\mathbb{M}+\frac{1}{\rho}\sum_{k,n}\hskip 1.0pt\text{div}\,\big(\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}\big),

(30)

where Eq. (
21
) and the chain rule,
\dot{\rho}_{0}=\rho\hskip 1.5pt\dot{\Phi}_{0}+\dot{\rho}\hskip 1.5pt\Phi_{0}
, are used to derive the above expression. Note that
\Phi_{0}
is linked to the other areal fractions through Eq. (
17
),

1-\Phi_{0}=\sum_{k,n}\phi^{k}_{n},

(31)

where
k=1,2
. Thus there are only
\sum_{k}(1+\aleph_{k})
linearly independent fractions. Analogously to Eq. (
30
), a dynamical equation for the internal-state fractions
\phi_{n}^{k}
can be derived, that is,
