\dot{\phi}^{k}_{n}=-\phi^{k}_{n}\,\mathbb{M}\,+\!\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}-\frac{1}{\rho}\hskip 1.0pt\text{div}\,\big(\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}\big).

(32)

A.2
Force balance and kinetics

The membrane patch
\mathcal{S}
is subjected to a traction
\boldsymbol{T}
at the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
of the domain. As a consequence, the total linear momentum over all membrane constituents, that is,
\int_{\mathcal{S}}\big(\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}+\sum_{k,n}\rho^{k}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n}\big)\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}S
, changes as follows:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}S=\!\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\boldsymbol{f}\,\mathrm{d}S\,+\!\!\!\int\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\!\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}\nu_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}l\,+\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathbb{M}\,\mathrm{d}S,

(33)

where
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\nu_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
is the outward unit vector tangent to the surface patch
\mathcal{S}
and normal to its boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
, and
\boldsymbol{f}
is the external force per unit area acting on the membrane.
The last term in Eq. (
33
) gives the momentum carried by membrane material added to or removed from the membrane through active membrane-area exchange. This assumes that the added or removed material moves with a velocity given by the barycentric velocity
\boldsymbol{v}(t)
. By means of the Reynolds transport theorem,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathcal{S}}(\rho\boldsymbol{v})\,\mathrm{d}S=\!\int_{\mathcal{S}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\rho\boldsymbol{v})+\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}\right]\mathrm{d}S
, together with the mass balance relation in Eq. (
29
), we obtain that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}S=\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\left(\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}+\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathbb{M}\right)\mathrm{d}S.

(34)

Moreover, by applying the divergence theorem to the boundary term of Eq. (
33
), this results in the following local form of linear momentum balance:

\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=\boldsymbol{f}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}}.

(35)

The stress vectors
\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}
are the projections of the tractions onto the constant surface lines, parametrized by
\xi^{\alpha}
. This can be further decomposed into the basis of the surface:

\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}=T^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}+N^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},

(36)

where
N^{\alpha}
describes the out-of-plane shear stresses, and
T^{\alpha\beta}
are the components of the in-plane stress tensor. Decomposing the force
\boldsymbol{f}
in the basis
\{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha},\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\}
, that is,
\boldsymbol{f}=f^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+f\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, the normal component of the momentum balance relation in Eq. (
35
) is found to be

\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=f+\,T^{\alpha\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}N^{\alpha}},

(37)

whereas the in-plane component is given by

\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\beta}=f^{\beta}+\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}T^{\alpha\beta}}\!-N^{\alpha}b_{\alpha}^{\beta},

(38)

by using
Gauss-Weingarten
equations, cf. Eq. (
516
).

In addition, the rate of change of angular momentum is given by the sum of the torques that arise from the body forces and tractions, as well as a director traction
\boldsymbol{M}
that acts on the boundary of membrane patch
\mathcal{S}
. In our case, the coarse-grained membrane is assumed to be an object of zero thickness, and its associated director field is chosen to be the same as the unit vector normal
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
to the surface, which is known as the Kirchhoff–Love assumption
[
83
]
. At each point along the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
, the director traction
\boldsymbol{M}
produces a moment per unit length
\boldsymbol{m}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\times\boldsymbol{M}
, giving rise to an overall boundary torque
\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{m}\,\mathrm{d}l
. This implies that
\boldsymbol{m}
has only tangential components, even though the director traction may have in general both normal and in-plane components. Hence, the corresponding stress vectors
\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}
, which are obtained by projecting
\boldsymbol{M}
along the curves of constant
\xi^{\alpha}
(that is,
\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}\nu_{\alpha}
), can be chosen without any loss of generality to be in the tangent plane of the membrane; namely, we define

\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}=-M^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta},

(39)

where
M^{\alpha\beta}
is known as the bending moment tensor. As a result, the conservation of angular momentum yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\boldsymbol{R}\times\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathrm{d}S\;=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\boldsymbol{R}\times\left(\boldsymbol{f}+\rho\boldsymbol{v}\,\mathbb{M}\right)\mathrm{d}S

\displaystyle\;\;\;+{\int\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{m}+\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{T}\right)\mathrm{d}l},

(40)

which, by applying Reynolds transport theorem, becomes

\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\boldsymbol{R}\times\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\,\mathrm{d}S=\!\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{f}\,\mathrm{d}S\,+{\!\!\int\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\!\!\left(\boldsymbol{m}+\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{T}\right)\mathrm{d}l}.

(41)

By means of divergence theorem, a local form of the angular momentum balance can be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}\times\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=

\displaystyle\;\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{f}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\times\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{R}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}}-b^{\beta}_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\times\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha},

(42)

where the covariant derivatives have been expanded out and the Gauss-Weingarten equations have been used to derive the above expression. By subtracting out the cross product of
\boldsymbol{R}
with the momentum balance relation in Eq. (
35
), then
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\times\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}}-b^{\beta}_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\times\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Therefore, by using the definition of the bending moment tensor in Eq. (
39
), the latter equation leads to

\Big[\!\left(T^{\alpha\beta}\!-b^{\beta}_{\gamma}M^{\gamma\alpha}\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}+\left(N^{\alpha}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\beta}M^{\beta\alpha}}\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\,\Big]\!\times\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{0},

(43)

which readily implies that

\sigma^{\alpha\beta}=T^{\alpha\beta}\!-b^{\beta}_{\gamma}M^{\gamma\alpha}

(44)

must be a symmetric tensor, and

N^{\alpha}=-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\beta}M^{\beta\alpha}},

(45)

so that the linear and angular momentum are conserved. Notice that in a system where the boundary moment
\boldsymbol{m}
vanishes identically, the in-plane stress tensor
T^{\alpha\beta}
must be symmetric, and the out-of-plane shear stress
N^{\alpha}=0
.

As a result, the equation of motion of the membrane along the normal direction is found to be

\boxed{\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=f+\sigma^{\alpha\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}+M^{\alpha\beta}b_{\alpha\gamma}b_{\beta}^{\gamma}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\!\nabla_{\!\beta}M^{\alpha\beta}}}

(46)

which is known as
the shape equation
. The identity
b_{\alpha\gamma}b_{\beta}^{\gamma}=2H\hskip 1.0ptb_{\alpha\beta}-K\hskip 0.5ptg_{\alpha\beta}
can be used to further simplify this expression. The force balance along the surface reads

\boxed{\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=f^{\alpha}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\beta}\sigma^{\alpha\beta}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\gamma}\!\left(b^{\alpha}_{\beta}M^{\beta\gamma}\right)}+b^{\alpha}_{\beta}\hskip 0.5pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\gamma}M^{\gamma\beta}}.}

(47)

To solve these equations of motion, the symmetrized form of the in-plane stress tensor
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}
and the bending moment tensor
M^{\alpha\beta}
must be first prescribed. In general, these stress tensors include both passive (
e.g.
elasticity and viscous dissipation) and active contributions.

Hereinafter, the passive contributions to the traction and the director traction are denoted by
\boldsymbol{T}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}
and
\boldsymbol{M}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}
, respectively. These contributions follow from the elastic free-energy of the membrane and are derived in the next section. The active contributions are denoted by the subscript
A
, namely the traction
\boldsymbol{T}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
and director traction
\boldsymbol{M}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
, and arise from forces acting within the finite active layer on the cytosolic side of the membrane. Thus, the net traction is
\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{T}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}+\boldsymbol{T}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
, while the total director traction is given by
\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{M}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}+\boldsymbol{M}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
.

A.3
Surface variation and membrane stresses

We consider the geometric dependence of the elastic free-energy
\mathcal{F}
of a membrane patch
\mathcal{S}
at fixed membrane composition. Accordingly,
\mathcal{F}
is treated as a functional of the metric tensor
g_{\alpha\beta}
and the curvature tensor
b_{\alpha\beta}
. For this geometric dependence,

\mathcal{F}(g_{\alpha\beta},b_{\alpha\beta})=\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!{F}(g_{\alpha\beta},b_{\alpha\beta})\;\mathrm{d}S,

(48)

with
F
as the surface free-energy density, we seek to obtain the mechanical response of the membrane, namely its free-energy change
\delta\mathcal{F}
, due to a shape deformation
\boldsymbol{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{R}+\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}
. This requires us to compute the corresponding changes in the basis vectors,
\delta\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
, and the normal,
\delta\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, as well as the corresponding variations in both the metric tensor,
\delta g_{\alpha\beta}
, and the curvature tensor,
\delta b_{\alpha\beta}
. However, computing these objects is typically cumbersome
[
84
]
, so we adopt a Lagrange multiplier technique
[
85
]
which circumvents this difficulty by treating the variables
\boldsymbol{R}
,
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
,
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
,
g_{\alpha\beta}
, and
b_{\alpha\beta}
as independent, including the known geometrical relationships between these variables as constraints. Therefore, we construct the constrained functional
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}[\boldsymbol{R},\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha},g_{\alpha\beta},b_{\alpha\beta}]
, defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}=\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\;+\;\int_{\mathcal{S}}\!\mathrm{d}S\;\big[\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}\cdot\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{R}}\right)+\lambda^{\alpha}\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)\big]

\displaystyle\;+\;\int_{\mathcal{S}}\!\mathrm{d}S\,\left[\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{2}-1\right)+\frac{\lambda^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\left(g_{\alpha\beta}-\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\;+\;\int_{\mathcal{S}}\!\mathrm{d}S\;\left[\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}\left(b_{\alpha\beta}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}\right)\right]\!

(49)

where the Lagrange multiplier
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}
enforces the relation between the basis
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and the embedding function
\boldsymbol{R}
,
\lambda^{\alpha}
imposes the orthogonality condition between
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, and
\lambda
enforces the normalization of the unit vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. Similarly, the multipliers
\lambda^{\alpha\beta}
and
\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}
ensure the definitions of the metric tensor
g_{\alpha\beta}
and the curvature tensor
b_{\alpha\beta}
, respectively, in terms of the basis vectors
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and the normal vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. Note that both these surface tensors are required to be symmetric, namely we have
\lambda^{\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{\beta\alpha}
and
\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}=\Lambda^{\beta\alpha}
. The advantage of this approach is that we do not need to explicitly consider the changes in
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
due to a variation in the embedding
\boldsymbol{R}
, as these tensors are now treated as independent variables
[
85
]
.

To determine
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}\!
, the functional derivative of
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
with respect to
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
must vanish (
\delta\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}/\delta\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=0
), which yields

\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}=\left(\lambda^{\alpha\beta}+\Lambda^{\alpha\gamma}\hskip 1.0ptb^{\beta}_{\gamma}\hskip 1.0pt\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}-\lambda^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},

(50)

where the
Gauss–Weingarten
equations have been used, that is,
\nabla_{\!\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=b_{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, and
\partial_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=-b^{\hskip 0.5pt\beta}_{\alpha}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}
. These relations follow from the geometrical constraints imposed in Eq. (
49
). Likewise,
\lambda^{\alpha\beta}
and
\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}
are given by the Euler-Lagrange equations associated with
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
, namely,

\lambda^{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\partial(\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0ptF)}{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}},\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{\partial F}{\partial b_{\alpha\beta}},

(51)

respectively. Since the variable
\boldsymbol{R}
appears only in the second term of Eq. (
49
), the infinitesimal variation of the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
due to a deformation
\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}
is written as

\delta_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}\!\left(\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\right)=\!\int_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}}\hskip-1.0pt\cdot\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}\right)-\!\oint_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;}\!\mathrm{d}l\;\nu_{\alpha}\left(\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt\cdot\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}\right)\!,

(52)

with
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\nu_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
being the outward unit vector tangent to the membrane and normal to the boundary curve
\partial\mathcal{S}
. If the shape variation
\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}
vanishes on
\partial\mathcal{S}
, that is, the position vector
\boldsymbol{R}
is fixed at the boundary, then the last term in Eq. (
52
) is identically zero, and thus we have

\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}}{\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}}=0,

(53)

which states that at equilibrium the stress vector
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}
has vanishing surface divergence.

Similarly, the local variation of
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
due to an infinitesimal change
\delta\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
in the normal unit vector is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}\!\left(\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}\right)

\displaystyle=\!\int\limits_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\left[\left(\lambda+\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}\right)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}+\Big(\hskip-1.0pt\lambda^{\alpha}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\beta}\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}}\Big)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\right]\!\cdot\delta\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}

\displaystyle\;\qquad+\oint\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;}\!\mathrm{d}l\;\nu_{\beta}\left(\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\delta\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)\!.

(54)

If the normal unit vector is fixed at the boundary, that is,
\delta\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=0
on
\partial\mathcal{S}
, then the associated Euler-Lagrange equation with respect to
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
is found to be

\frac{\delta\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}}{\delta\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=\left(\lambda+\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}\right)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}+\Big(\hskip-1.0pt\lambda^{\alpha}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\beta}\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}}\Big)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=0,

(55)

and therefore we have that

\lambda=-\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\alpha}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\beta}\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}}.

(56)

However, in general, neither
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
nor
\boldsymbol{R}
is fixed on the boundary of the patch
\mathcal{S}
, and both are free to vary. Consequently, the boundary terms give rise to the traction and director traction. Using the Euler-Lagrange equations in (
53
) and (
55
), the total infinitesimal variation of the free-energy
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
is given by

\delta\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}=\oint\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;}\!\mathrm{d}l\;\nu_{\beta}\left(\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\delta\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\beta}\cdot\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}\right)\!.

(57)

This must be balanced by the traction
\boldsymbol{T}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}
and the director traction
\boldsymbol{M}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}
at the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
, which are (by definition) the passive contributions to the traction
\boldsymbol{T}
and director traction
\boldsymbol{M}
, respectively. Thus,
\delta\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
equals the virtual work,

\delta\mathcal{W}=\oint\limits_{\partial\mathcal{S}\;}\!\mathrm{d}l\;\nu_{\alpha}\left(\boldsymbol{T}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}^{\alpha}\cdot\delta\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}+\boldsymbol{M}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}^{\alpha}\cdot\delta\hskip 0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)\!,

(58)

where
\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}
and
\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}
are the projections of the tractions onto the surface lines of constant
\xi^{\alpha}
. From Eq. (
57
) and (
58
), we find that
\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}=-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}=\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}
. By decomposing these traction vectors into the surface basis, analogously to Eqs. (
39
) and (
36
),
\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}=-M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}
and
\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt0}=T^{\alpha\beta}_{0}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}+N_{0}^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, then we have that the bending moment tensor
M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=-\Lambda^{\alpha\beta}
, the in-plane stress tensor
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=T_{0}^{\alpha\beta}\!-b^{\beta}_{\gamma}M_{0}^{\gamma\alpha}=-\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\!
, cf. Eq. (
44
), and the out-of-plane shear stress
N^{\alpha}_{0}=\lambda^{\alpha}
. The latter, together with the second equation in (
56
), gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N^{\alpha}_{0}=-\nabla_{\!\beta}M^{\beta\alpha}_{0},}

(59)

as found previously in Eq. (
45
) for the overall shear stress
N^{\alpha}
. Since
\sigma_{0}^{\alpha\beta}
is symmetric, this shows that the passive contributions to the tractions, which arise from the free-energy
\mathcal{F}
, respect angular-momentum conservation. Thus, the passive stresses are given by

\boxed{\sigma_{0}^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\partial(\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0ptF)}{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}},\quad\text{and}\quad M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=\frac{\partial F}{\partial b_{\alpha\beta}},}

(60)

and the stress vector reduces to

{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}^{\alpha}=-T^{\alpha\beta}_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}+\nabla_{\!\beta}M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}.}

(61)

A.4
Free-energy and constitutive relations

At a coarse-grained level, the Helmholtz free-energy
\mathcal{F}
of a multi-component biomembrane (described by three distinct membrane components and their internal states) includes an elastic bending energy together with the surface-tension contribution (
i.e.
the Helfrich energy density)
[
86
]
, an entropy of mixing of the various species, and a phenomenological coupling between the mean curvature
H
of the surface and the local density of membrane components
[
87
]
. We consider that the membrane forms a closed surface, a membrane compartment, and we denote its surface by
\mathcal{M}
(formally, we say that
\mathcal{M}
is homeomorphic to the Euclidean 2-sphere). The overall free-energy of the compartment is therefore given by
\mathcal{F}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}F\,\mathrm{d}S
, where the free-energy density

\boxed{F\hskip-1.5pt=\hskip-1.0pt\Sigma+2\kappa\hskip 0.25ptH^{2}+2\kappa\hskip 0.25ptH\hskip 0.5ptC_{\mathrm{m}}+V_{\mathrm{m}},}

(62)

with
\kappa\hskip 0.25pt
and
\Sigma
as the bending rigidity and surface tension of the membrane, respectively
[
84
]
. The latter can be in general a function of the local coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
. Here,
V_{\mathrm{m}}
is the mean-field free-energy associated with membrane components in the dilute limit (expanded to lowest order in the areal fractions of each membrane component):

V_{\mathrm{m}}=\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}\,}\hskip 1.0pt\Big[\Phi_{0}\big(\!\ln\Phi_{0}+\mathcal{E}_{0}\big)+\sum_{k,n}\phi^{k}_{n}\big(\!\ln\phi^{k}_{n}+\mathcal{E}^{k}_{n}\big)\Big]\hskip-1.0pt,

(63)

which contains the entropy of mixing terms, as well as the linear-order energetic penalties for each membrane component. Herein,
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}^{k}_{n}
are dimensionless constants, representing the standard chemical potentials, measured in units of
k_{B}T
(thermal energy). The parameter
b_{0}
is a coarse-graining area scale on the membrane, with an associated length scale
\sqrt{b_{0}}\sim 50
–
100
nm. The local spontaneous curvature
C_{\mathrm{m}}
gives the interaction term via the coupling to curvature, namely

C_{\mathrm{m}}=\Phi_{0}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{C}_{0}+\sum_{k,n}\phi^{k}_{n}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{C}^{k}_{n}=\,\mathcal{C}_{0}+\sum_{k,n}\phi^{k}_{n}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{n}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\right)\!,

(64)

where
\mathcal{C}_{0}
specifies the curvature-coupling strength of the background membrane component, while
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{n}
specifies that of internal state
n
in cycle
k
. The second equality in (
64
) follows from Eq. (
31
), and
\mathcal{C}_{0}
can be interpreted as a global spontaneous curvature of the membrane, often included in the Helfrich theory
[
86
]
. Strictly positive values of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{n}
indicate a tendency to enrich regions of negative curvature with the corresponding membrane components. Note that the standard Helfrich free-energy functional
[
86
]
can also contain a Gaussian curvature term of the form
\int_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{\kappa}\hskip 1.0ptK\,\mathrm{d}S

[
84
]
.
For the closed surface considered here, the Gauss–Bonnet theorem fixes this contribution to the constant
4\pi\bar{\kappa}

[
88
]
, so it does not affect the membrane dynamics and can be omitted.

Using Eq. (
60
), the membrane surface stresses can thus be calculated from the free-energy density
F
, which is a function of the metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
and the curvature tensor
b_{\alpha\beta}
. The free-energy density
F
depends on the tensors
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
only via the mean curvature
H
for the constitutive form in Eq. (
62
). Hence, by the chain rule,

\frac{\partial F}{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}\,b^{\alpha\beta}\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial F}{\partial b_{\alpha\beta}}=\frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}\frac{\partial F}{\partial H},

(65)

which follow from the derivative identities
\frac{\partial H}{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5ptb^{\alpha\beta}
and
\frac{\partial H}{\partial b_{\alpha\beta}}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 0.5ptg^{\alpha\beta}
. The derivative of
F
with respect to the mean curvature
H
is obtained by using Eq. (
62
),

\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}={4\kappa\hskip 0.25ptH+2\kappa\hskip 0.25ptC_{\mathrm{m}}},

(66)

then the in-plane stress tensor
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{0}
readily follows:

\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=Fg^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\,b^{\alpha\beta},

(67)

by applying the identity
\partial g/\partial g_{\alpha\beta}=g\hskip 1.0ptg^{\alpha\beta}
. Similarly, the tensor
M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}\!
, as defined in Eq. (
60
), is given by

M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\,g^{\alpha\beta}.

(68)

Hence, the passive in-plane stress
T^{\alpha\beta}_{0}
and out-of-plane shear stress
N_{0}^{\alpha\phantom{\beta}}\!
are found to be

T^{\alpha\beta}_{0}=Fg^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\,b^{\alpha\beta},

(69)

and

N_{0}^{\alpha\phantom{\beta}}=-\frac{1}{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla^{\alpha}}\!\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\right).

(70)

In addition to the passive mechanical stresses, the free-energy density
F
also determines the chemical potentials of the membrane species. In general, these are defined by functional derivatives of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to the areal fractions. Since
F
is local in composition and contains no composition-gradient terms, these reduce here to partial derivatives of
F
. Because
\phi_{n}^{k}
and
\Phi_{0}
are constrained by Eq. (
31
), the relevant quantity is the chemical-potential difference relative to the background component,

\boxed{\mu_{n}^{k}=\frac{\partial F}{\partial\phi_{n}^{k}}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial\Phi_{0}},}

(71)

which gives the local free-energy cost per unit area associated with replacing background membrane by component
k
in internal state
n
.

A local chemical potential imbalance generates gradients in
\mu_{n}^{k}
, which drive the migration of membrane components and decrease the total free-energy of the system. Thus, the diffusive currents
j^{k\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{n}
must be directly related to the gradients
\nabla^{\alpha}\mu_{n}^{k}
. For general irreversible processes in the linear regime (
i.e.
close to equilibrium), the Onsager’s principle holds
[
89
]
, which establishes a linear relationship between the generalized forces and their corresponding thermodynamic fluxes
[
83
]
. For isothermal systems, this principle is shown to reduce to the principle of least energy dissipation
[
90
]
; namely, the system evolves dynamically so that it minimizes the competition between dissipation and energy release.

Therefore, this can be cast as a variational principle problem, where we seek to minimize a functional that is known as the
Rayleighian
, and which is defined by

\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{D}+\dot{\mathcal{F}},

(72)

with
\mathfrak{D}
as the dissipation potential
[
91
]
.
\mathcal{F}
is typically a function of the state variables, such as the metric tensor
g_{\alpha\beta}
, the curvature tensor
b_{\alpha\beta}
, and the internal-state fractions
\phi_{n}^{k}
, describing the state of the system
[
91
]
. On the other hand,
\mathfrak{D}
is a function of the
process variables
, such as the diffusive fluxes
\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}
, which characterize how the system changes its state and generates dissipation
[
91
]
. The Rayleighian is minimized with respect to the process variables rather than the state variables
[
91
]
. In general, the Rayleighian
\mathfrak{R}
may include terms that correspond to external power sources, as well as any other constraints that the process variables must additionally satisfy
[
91
]
. In Onsager’s variational approach, the dissipation potential
\mathfrak{D}
is quadratic in the process variables
[
92
]
. This can be generalized to nonlinear convex functions satisfying
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.5ptt}\mathcal{F}\leq 0

[
91
]
. Such a dissipation potential is positive and vanishes only when all process variables vanish
[
91
]
.

Since the free-energy density
F\equiv F\!\left[g_{\alpha\beta},b_{\alpha\beta},\phi_{n}^{k}\right]
, we find the rate of change of the total free-energy to be

\dot{\mathcal{F}}=\int\limits_{\mathcal{M}\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\,\Big(\,\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{0}^{\alpha\beta}\,\dot{g}_{\alpha\beta}+M_{0}^{\alpha\beta}\,\dot{b}_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{k,n}\mu_{n}^{k}\,\dot{\phi}_{n}^{k}\,\Big),

(73)

which follows from the Reynolds transport theorem for the closed surface
\mathcal{M}
, together with the results in Eq. (
60
) and the definition of
\mu_{n}^{k}
in Eq. (
71
).

In general, the in-plane membrane flow and the out-of-plane bending may also result in energy dissipation,
e.g.
the in-plane dissipation due to the two-dimensional shear and bulk viscosities of the membrane
[
93
]
. Here we neglect their contributions, since the viscosity of the ambient fluid adjacent to the membrane provides the dominant mechanism of dissipation in the system.

By contrast, dissipation associated with the diffusive motion of the membrane species relative to the background component must be retained, since it determines their finite mobility and hence the diffusive fluxes entering the composition dynamics. Consequently, in order to construct the dissipation potential associated with this mechanism, we assume that the drag force
\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{k}
(a force per unit area) on component
k
in internal state
n
within a coarse-grained membrane patch of area
b_{0}
is given by
\boldsymbol{d}^{k}_{n}=-\zeta^{k}_{n}\hskip 1.0pt\phi^{k}_{n}\!\left(\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n}-\!\boldsymbol{v}\right)
, where
\zeta^{k}_{n}
are the drag coefficients. Here,
\phi^{k}_{n}\!\left(\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n}-\!\boldsymbol{v}\right)
is the relative flux of this membrane component with respect to the barycentric motion. Thus the rate of energy loss per unit membrane area can be written as follows:
\mathfrak{D}^{k}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{k}_{n}\hskip 1.0pt\phi^{k}_{n}\left|\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{v}^{k}_{n}-\!\boldsymbol{v}\right|^{2}\hskip-1.0pt
. Since the concentration of membrane species is assumed to follow a dilute distribution of non-interacting particles, the drag on each component is unaffected by the others, so the local dissipation is additive over all components. As a result, we write the total dissipation potential as follows:

\mathfrak{D}=\int\limits_{\mathcal{M}\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\;\frac{1}{2\rho}\sum_{k,n}\zeta^{k}_{n}\,\big|\boldsymbol{j}_{n}^{k}\big|^{2},

(74)

using Eq. (
19
). The minimization of the Rayleighian
\mathfrak{R}
with respect to each diffusive flux
\boldsymbol{j}_{n}^{k}
gives us that

-\zeta^{k}_{n}\,\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}=\rho\,\frac{\delta\dot{\mathcal{F}}}{\delta\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}}=\rho\,\frac{\delta}{\delta\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}}\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\,\sum_{k,n}\mu_{n}^{k}\hskip 1.0pt\dot{\phi}_{n}^{k}\right)\!.

(75)

As the dynamics of
\phi_{n}^{k}
depends on their diffusive fluxes, via Eq. (
32
), we find, by integration by parts, that

\boxed{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{j}^{k}_{n}=-\frac{\rho}{\zeta^{k}_{n}}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\nabla_{\!\alpha}\!\left(\frac{\mu_{n}^{k}}{\rho}\right)\!,}}

(76)

which linearly relates the diffusive flux with the gradient in the chemical potential if the total density
\rho\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)
is spatially uniform along the membrane, namely
\nabla_{\!\alpha}\rho=0
.

A.5
Adiabatic approximation

Henceforth, only cyclic transitions are considered for the biochemical cycles associated with fusion and fission events, as depicted in Fig.
6
. In other words, the index set
\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}
, whose elements are the states
m
which directly connect to state
n
for a given
k
-th component (fissogens or fusogens), is defined to be

\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}=\begin{cases}\hskip 0.5pt\left\{1\right\}&\quad\mathrm{if}~n=0,\\[1.5pt]
\hskip 0.5pt\left\{0,2,\aleph_{k}\hskip-0.5pt\right\}&\quad\mathrm{if}~n=1,\\[1.0pt]
\hskip 0.5pt\{n-1,n+1\}&\quad\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(77)

with the cyclic condition that state
1+\aleph_{k}
is the same as, or maps back to, state one. Here, the state zero plays the role of an idle or inactivated state. The label
n=0
refers to the biochemical state of the membrane-associated machinery, rather than to a state carried by the transport vesicle. In the case of a fusion cycle, the transition out of this inactive state physically corresponds to the arrival of a small transport vesicle, such as a COPII-derived vesicle, near the membrane of the organelle,
e.g.
a Golgi cisterna, and the activation of the local fusion machinery; while the transition into the idle state signifies the inactivation of the membrane components that were previously recruited at that site,
e.g.
the SNARE-complex
disassembly
.
Similarly
, in the case of a fission cycle, the transition out of the idle state corresponds to the recruitment and activation of proteins that initiate fission at a local site on the membrane, such as coatomers; whereas a transition into the idle state represents the scission of a newly formed vesicle from the mother membrane and the return of the associated machinery to its inactive state.

In the physiological regime, the characteristic times associated with the mechanochemical kinetics of the internal, cyclic transitions are much smaller (on the order of
milliseconds
) than the relaxation times of the membrane
dynamics
on length scales larger than the coarse-graining length scale (
\sim 50
–
100
nm). Typically, we find the relaxation times of the latter to be in the range of
1
–
100
s (see Table I). This separation of scales allows us to consider the transitional dynamics of the internal states as an adiabatic process with respect to the shape deformations of the membrane, and the diffusion of its components within the membrane.

By using this adiabatic approximation, the densities
\rho_{n}^{k}
can be treated as rapidly relaxing fields that adjust to an instantaneous nonequilibrium steady state, which must satisfy the following flux relations:

\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}(r^{k}_{nm}\rho^{k}_{m}-r^{k}_{mn}\rho^{k}_{n})=0,\;\,\mathrm{or}\;\sum_{m\hskip 0.5pt\in\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{I}_{\hskip-1.0ptn}^{\hskip-0.15ptk}}\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}=0,

(78)

for all states
n
in cycle
k
. This follows from Eq. (
25
) in which the material-derivative and local surface-dilation terms, together with the diffusive terms, are neglected relative to the internal kinetics. Note that the internal currents
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}\hskip-1.0pt=r^{k}_{nm}\phi^{k}_{m}-r^{k}_{mn}\phi^{k}_{n}
vanish identically at thermodynamic equilibrium due to the detailed balance condition. In a nonequilibrium steady state, detailed balance is no longer satisfied, and the currents
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}\hskip-1.0pt\neq 0
in general. Since the transitions are cyclic (see Fig.
6
), Eq. (
78
) reduces, using Eq. (
77
), to

\begin{cases}\,\mathbb{J}^{k}_{0,1}=0,&\quad\mathrm{if}~n=0,\\[2.0pt]
\,\mathbb{J}^{k}_{1,0}+\mathbb{J}^{k}_{1,2}+\mathbb{J}^{k}_{1,\aleph_{k}}=0,&\quad\mathrm{if}~n=1,\\[2.0pt]
\,\mathbb{J}^{k}_{n,n-1}+\mathbb{J}^{k}_{n,n+1}=0,&\quad\mathrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(79)

Due to the antisymmetric property, that is,
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}=-\mathbb{J}^{k}_{mn}
, we can easily see that the only non-trivial solution to this recurrence equation is a constant current solution,

\boxed{\mathbb{J}^{k}_{n+1,n}=\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\;\;\textnormal{and}\;\;\mathbb{J}^{k}_{n,n+1}=-\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}},}

(80)

for all
k
, and
n\in\left\{1,2,\,\dots,\aleph_{k}\right\}
, with the property that the state
1+\aleph_{k}
is the same as state
1
. Here, the loop current
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\geq 0
, such that the internal currents
\mathbb{J}^{k}_{nm}
along a forward cyclic direction (
1\rightarrow 2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow 1
) are strictly positive, and negative otherwise. In addition to this, we must have the transition currents

\mathbb{J}^{k}_{0,1}=-\mathbb{J}^{k}_{1,0}=0,

(81)

for each species
k
. Note that if the cyclic part of cycle
k
contains only two internal states, excluding the idle state
n=0
, then
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
. With only a single pair of transitions between these states, stationarity requires the forward and reverse fluxes to balance. Therefore, a nonzero loop current requires at least three internal states in the cycle (
\aleph_{k}>2
).
The nonzero current
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is a nonequilibrium feature of the system that describes the steady circulation through the internal mechanochemical cycle (vanishing identically at equilibrium by detailed balance).

The internal-state fractions
\phi_{n}^{k}
are determined by solving the simultaneous equations in (
80
) and (
81
), which can be rewritten in the matrix form

\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}-r^{k}_{1,0}&r^{k}_{0,1}&0&0&\cdots&0\\[4.0pt]
0&\!-r^{k}_{2,1}&r^{k}_{1,2}&0&\cdots&0\\[4.0pt]
0&0&\!-r^{k}_{3,2}&r^{k}_{2,3}&\cdots&0\\[1.0pt]
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\[4.0pt]
0&r^{k}_{\aleph_{k},1}&0&0&\cdots&\!-r^{k}_{1,\aleph_{k}}\,\end{bmatrix}}_{=\,\mathsf{R}_{k}\,\textnormal{(a square matrix of order $1+\aleph_{k}$)}}\!\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{0}^{k}\\[4.0pt]
\phi_{1}^{k}\\[4.0pt]
\phi_{2}^{k}\\[1.0pt]
\vdots\\[4.0pt]
\phi_{\aleph_{k}}^{k}\end{bmatrix}}^{=\,\mathsf{f}_{k}}=\overbrace{\begin{bmatrix}0\\[4.0pt]
\,\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\\[4.0pt]
\,\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\\[1.0pt]
\vdots\\[4.0pt]
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\end{bmatrix}}^{=\,\mathsf{j}_{k}}

and thus succinctly expressed as follows:
\mathsf{R}_{k}\hskip 0.75pt\mathsf{f}_{k}\hskip-0.5pt=\mathsf{j}_{k}
. This can be easily solved by a matrix inversion, provided that
\det\mathsf{R}_{k}\neq 0
; namely,
\mathsf{f}_{k}=\mathsf{R}_{k}^{-1}\mathsf{j}_{k}
, where
\mathsf{R}_{k}^{-1}
is the inverse matrix of
\mathsf{R}_{k}
. This yields the solution of the mass fractions
\phi^{k}_{n}
in terms of the loop current
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
; namely,

\phi^{k}_{n}={\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}\,/\,{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}},

(82)

where
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}
is a coefficient that depends solely on the transition rates
r^{k}_{nm}
.
The loop current
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is not an independent parameter, and its explicit form can be obtained in terms of the total areal fraction associated with cycle
k
, which we define as follows:

\boxed{\Phi_{k}\!=\!\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\hskip 1.0pt\phi_{n}^{k}.}

(83)

Thus, the internal loop current can be rewritten as

\boxed{\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\Phi_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,}

(84)

with the parameter
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
satisfying
{1}/{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}{1}/{\mathbb{R}_{n}^{k}}
. A more explicit expression for
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
in terms of the underlying transition rates follows from Eq. (
80
). Along each cyclic transition, the constant-current condition can be rearranged as
\phi^{k}_{n+1}=(r^{k}_{n+1,n}/r^{k}_{n,n+1})\phi^{k}_{n}-\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}/r^{k}_{n,n+1}
. Iterating this relation once around the cycle and imposing
\phi^{k}_{\aleph_{k}+1}=\phi^{k}_{1}
shows that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\propto\prod_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}r^{k}_{n+1,n}-\prod_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}r^{k}_{n,n+1}.}

(85)

Thus, at thermodynamic equilibrium, the
Kolmogorov cycle condition
requires the products of the forward and reverse transition rates to be equal
[
50
]
, giving
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
and hence
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
. Using Eq. (
84
) in Eq. (
82
), the internal-state fractions can then be written as

\boxed{\phi^{k}_{n}=\Phi_{k}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}/{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}},}

(86)

which tells us that each of the relative internal-state fractions
\phi^{k}_{n}/\Phi_{k}
is set entirely by the biochemical transition rates. Nonetheless, the total areal fractions
\Phi_{k}
themselves are not determined by these transition rates, but remain slow dynamical variables, whose evolution is discussed in the next section.

Under the adiabatic approximation, the mean-field free-energy density in Eq. (
63
) can be written in terms of the total component fractions
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{k}
. This follows by eliminating the internal states
n
using Eq. (
86
), which expresses
\phi_{n}^{k}
in terms of
\Phi_{k}
and the transition rates of the
k
th cycle; namely,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V_{\mathrm{m}}=\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}\,}\Big[\Phi_{0}\big(\!\ln\Phi_{0}\!+\mathcal{E}_{0}\big)+\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\big(\!\ln\Phi_{k}\!+\mathcal{E}_{k}\big)\Big]\hskip-1.0pt,}

(87)

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}_{k}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\frac{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{\mathbb{R}_{n}^{k}}\Bigg[\mathcal{E}^{k}_{n}+\ln\frac{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{\mathbb{R}_{n}^{k}}\Bigg],}

(88)

using

{1}/{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}{1}/{\mathbb{R}_{n}^{k}}
.
Similarly, the local spontaneous curvature
C_{\mathrm{m}}
in Eq. (
64
) can be written in terms of the total component fractions:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\mathrm{m}}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\Phi_{0}+\sum_{k}\mathcal{C}_{k}\Phi_{k}},

(89)

where the effective curvature-coupling coefficient

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}_{k}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\mathcal{C}^{k}_{n}\hskip 1.0pt\frac{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}}.}

(90)

A.6
Dynamics of the areal fractions

In the adiabatic approximation, the total membrane-area flux, as given by Eq. (
27
), can be expressed in terms of the loop currents as follows:

{\mathbb{M}=\sum_{k}\,\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}\,\mathbb{M}^{k}_{n+1,n},}

(91)

where
\aleph_{k}+1\equiv 1
. This has been obtained by using the fact that the transition rates are cyclic, together with the antisymmetry
\mathbb{M}^{k}_{nm}=-\mathbb{M}^{k}_{mn}
; namely, the membrane area transferred during the biochemical transition
m\rightarrow n
must have the same magnitude as that transferred during the reverse transition
n\rightarrow m
, but with opposite sign. Notice that the membrane-area flux
\mathbb{M}
vanishes, as expected, if the system is at equilibrium. Thus, by using Eq. (
84
),
\mathbb{M}
can be rewritten as

\boxed{\mathbb{M}=\sum_{k}\,\Phi_{k}\,\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,,\vskip-1.0pt}

(92)

where
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is the membrane-area transfer rate associated with the
k
-th trafficking cycle, namely

\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}\,\mathbb{M}^{k}_{n+1,n},

(93)

whose sign is negative for
k=1
(fissogens) and positive for
k=2
(fusogens); specifically, we say that

\mathrm{sgn}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right)=(-1)^{k},

(94)

with
\mathrm{sgn}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)
denoting the
signum
function.

Moreover, we assume that the total membrane density
\rho
is a constant (say
\bar{\rho}
) on length scales larger than the coarse-graining length scale in our model (
\sim 50
–
100
nm). In other words, the membrane is assumed to be locally incompressible, and thus gradients in the total density
\rho
are not supported at that scale. As a corollary, Eq. (
29
) leads to the following condition:

\boxed{\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}v^{\alpha}}-2Hv=\mathbb{M},}

(95)

where the identity (
522
) is used. This equation acts as an imposed local constraint for the membrane area, with the surface tension
\Sigma
being the Lagrange multiplier.

As a result, the kinematic equation for the mass fraction
\Phi_{0}
in Eq. (
30
), together with Eq. (
76
), yields

\dot{\Phi}_{0}=\left(1-\Phi_{0}\right)\mathbb{M}-\sum_{k,n}\hskip 1.0pt\frac{\Delta\mu_{n}^{k}}{\bar{\rho}\hskip 1.2pt\zeta^{k}_{n}},

(96)

where
\Delta
is the two-dimensional Laplace–Beltrami operator.
On the other hand, the dynamics of the areal fractions
\Phi_{k}
is obtained from Eq. (
32
), and given by

\dot{\Phi}_{k}=-\Phi_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{M}+\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\hskip 1.0pt\frac{\Delta\mu_{n}^{k}}{\bar{\rho}\hskip 1.2pt\zeta^{k}_{n}}.

(97)

Notice that by summing this equation with respect to
k
, and using that
1-\Phi_{0}=\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}
, we retrieve Eq. (
96
).

From the free-energy density
F
in Eq. (
62
), the local chemical potential difference is found to be

\mu_{n}^{k}\hskip-1.0pt=2\kappa\hskip 0.25ptH\hskip-1.0pt\left(\mathcal{C}_{n}^{k}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\right)+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}\,}\!\hskip-1.0pt\left[\hskip-1.0pt\left(\mathcal{E}_{n}^{k}-\mathcal{E}_{0}\right)\!+\ln\frac{\phi_{n}^{k}}{\Phi_{0}}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt,

(98)

which must vanish identically at equilibrium. By using the form of the chemical potential in Eq. (
98
), this allows us to write the last term in Eq. (
97
) as follows:

\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\frac{\Delta\mu_{n}^{k}}{\bar{\rho}\hskip 1.2pt\zeta^{k}_{n}}=2\hskip 1.0pt\Omega_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\Delta H+\gamma_{k}\,\Delta\Big(\hskip-1.0pt\ln\frac{\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{0}}\hskip 1.0pt\Big),

(99)

where the effective surface diffusivities
\gamma_{k}
are defined by

\gamma_{k}=\frac{k_{B}T}{\bar{\rho}\hskip 0.5ptb_{0}\,}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\hskip 1.0pt\frac{1}{\zeta_{n}^{k}},

(100)

and the effective constants
\Omega_{k}
are given by

\Omega_{k}=\frac{\kappa\hskip 0.25pt}{\bar{\rho}}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\frac{\mathcal{C}_{n}^{k}-\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\zeta_{n}^{k}}.

(101)

As in Eqs. (
88
) and (
90
), these sums include the idle state
n=0
, since
\Phi_{k}
includes all internal states. The membrane-area transfer sums in Eqs. (
91
) and (
93
) instead begin at
n=1
, because transitions to and from the idle state carry no loop current, cf. Eq. (
81
).

Accordingly, Eq. (
97
) can be rewritten as follows:

\boxed{\dot{\Phi}_{k}=-\Phi_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{M}+2\hskip 1.0pt\Omega_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\Delta H+\gamma_{k}\,\Delta\Big(\hskip-1.0pt\ln\frac{\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{0}}\hskip 1.0pt\Big),}

(102)

where the Laplacian term, by chain rule, expands to

\displaystyle\Delta\Big(\hskip-1.5pt\ln\hskip-1.0pt\frac{\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{0}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{k}}-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\Phi_{k}\,\nabla^{\alpha}\Phi_{k}}}{(\Phi_{k})^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\frac{\Delta\Phi_{0}}{\Phi_{0}}}+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\Phi_{0}\,\nabla^{\alpha}\Phi_{0}}}{(\Phi_{0})^{2}}.

(103)

Thus, Eq. (
102
) represents the governing equation (at large scales) of the areal concentration of membrane components,
i.e.
fissogens (
k=1
) and fusogens (
k=2
).

A.7
Active membrane stresses

The internal biochemical transitions associated with fission and fusion generate localized forces in the neighboring cytosolic fluid. These forces act within a finite region on the cytosolic side of the membrane, which we refer to as the active layer. Its characteristic thickness is of order the coarse-graining length (
\sim\!50
–
100
nm).

Because the forces are distributed over a finite normal distance, the fluid transmits both force and torque to the membrane. In the sharp-interface description derived below, these effects are retained through the active contributions
\boldsymbol{T}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
and
\boldsymbol{M}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
to the membrane traction and director traction, respectively, together with the slip velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
. We first describe the force distribution within the finite layer and then derive this sharp-interface reduction for a layer whose thickness is small compared with the tangential length scales of the membrane
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
41
]
.

A.7.1
Force distribution within the active layer

We denote by
\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}(\boldsymbol{r})
the three-dimensional active body-force density exerted on the cytosolic fluid by the molecular machinery associated with the fission and fusion cycles. Using the surface coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
of the membrane compartment
\mathcal{M}
and the normal distance
h
from the membrane, this force density can be represented as

\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}\!\left(\boldsymbol{r}\right)=\int_{\mathcal{M}\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)\,\delta\big(\boldsymbol{r}-\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{R}}\big),

(104)

where the position vector
\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{R}}
is defined by

\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{R}}(\xi^{1}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0pt\xi^{2}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 0.5pth)=h\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}(\xi^{1}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0pt\xi^{2})+\boldsymbol{R}(\xi^{1}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0pt\xi^{2}).

(105)

Here and below, the dependence of local surface fields on the membrane coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
is left implicit unless it is required explicitly. The function
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)
is defined in these membrane-based coordinates such that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}h
gives the force associated with the normal interval between
h
and
h+\mathrm{d}h
above a membrane area element
\mathrm{d}S
. The force profile is obtained by coarse-graining the microscopic forces associated with the fission and fusion machinery over a thin active layer of thickness
\Lambda
, whose scale is set by the size of transport vesicles. Accordingly,
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)
has support only over
0\leq h\leq\Lambda
and vanishes for
h>\Lambda
. As the area of a surface displaced by a normal distance
h
differs from that of the membrane itself, the corresponding physical volume element is (see Fig.
8
):

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{d}\tilde{V}=J(h)\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}h,\quad J(h)=1-2hH+h^{2}K.}

(106)

For a point within the active layer located at
\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{R}}(\xi^{\alpha},h)
, the three-dimensional body-force density is therefore

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}\big(\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}\,\big)={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)}/{J(h)}.}

(107)

Hence,
\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}
represents the force per unit physical volume entering the bulk momentum balance, while
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}
gives the same force distribution per unit membrane area and unit normal distance. The normal moments introduced below are defined in terms of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}
.

We assume that, at the coarse-grained level considered here, the distribution of active forces has no preferred direction within the local tangent plane. The tangential components therefore average to zero, and we retain only the force component along the outward normal, writing

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)=\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},

(108)

where
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}
is the scalar normal force profile. It is obtained by summing the cycle-specific contributions,

\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)=\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\,\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h),

(109)

which includes the corresponding active force distributions
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
due to fissogens (
k=1
) and fusogens (
k=2
) over their entire cycle. Analogous to the membrane-area flux in Eq. (
91
), the force profile associated with cycle
k
is obtained by summing the mechanical contributions from its internal transitions over one complete cycle. Within the adiabatic approximation, this gives

\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)=\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}\mathbb{F}^{k}_{n+1,n}(h),

(110)

where
\mathbb{F}^{k}_{nm}(h)
denotes the momentum transferred during the active transition from the internal state
m
to
n
at normal height
h
. As for the membrane-area exchange, we assume that the transitions form a cyclic process and that
\mathbb{F}^{k}_{nm}=-\mathbb{F}^{k}_{mn}
. Using the loop current
\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\Phi_{k}\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, the total active force profile can thus be written as

\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)=\sum_{k}\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\sum_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}\mathbb{F}^{k}_{n+1,n}(h).

(111)

The active force
\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}
is exerted on the neighboring cytosolic fluid and is localized within
0<h<\Lambda
. We denote by
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}
the stress in the outer cytosolic fluid when this finite active layer is retained explicitly. The momentum balance for
h>0
is then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}+\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\boldsymbol{0},}

(112)

where
\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\boldsymbol{0}
for
h>\Lambda
, so that Eq. (
112
) reduces to the unforced Stokes equation outside the active layer.

Since
\Lambda
is much smaller than the characteristic tangential variation length
L
of the membrane geometry, composition, and outer fluid fields, we introduce the small parameter
\epsilon_{\Lambda}=\Lambda/L\ll 1
and take the corresponding sharp-interface limit. In this description, the finite active layer is no longer resolved explicitly, and the outer-fluid stress
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{+}}
satisfies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{+}}=\boldsymbol{0},\qquad h>0.}

(113)

The effects of the active layer are instead retained through effective active membrane stresses and a modified interfacial velocity condition at
h=0

[
54
,
55
,
56
]
.

As written in Eq. (
112
),
\boldsymbol{F}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}
enters the bulk momentum balance as a body-force density. We assume that the net force associated with each coarse-grained trafficking cycle vanishes locally. Since
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}h
gives the force associated with the normal interval
\mathrm{d}h
above a membrane area element
\mathrm{d}S
, this requires, at each position along the membrane,

\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)=0.

(114)

The zeroth normal moment therefore vanishes. The first and second moments are consequently the first nonzero moments of the force distribution and, as shown below, provide the leading contributions retained in the sharp-interface description.

The first and second normal moments associated with the
k
-th trafficking cycle are defined by

\boxed{\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h),}

(115)

and

\boxed{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h^{2}\,\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h),}

(116)

respectively. Using Eq. (
109
), the corresponding total first and second moments of the active force profile are

\mathbb{P}=\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}},\qquad\mathbb{Q}=\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}.

(117)

The moments
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
are determined by the corresponding cycle-specific force profile and may independently take either sign. Their signs are not fixed by the type of trafficking event alone, although particular force profiles may favor specific sign choices.

For illustrative purposes, we consider a simple point-force dipolar model:

\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)\!=f^{k}_{0}\left[\delta\!\left(h-\Lambda\right)-\delta(h)\right]\!,

(118)

where
f_{0}^{k}
sets the magnitude and sign of the cycle-averaged force profile for cycle
k
. Note that the monopole of this force distribution is identically zero, by construction. This is a simple model that captures the magnitude of the momentum transfer and its characteristic separation along the membrane normal. In this simple model, the first and second moments of the force profile are

\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=f^{k}_{0}\Lambda,\qquad\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=f^{k}_{0}\Lambda^{2},

(119)

respectively. Hence, for this particular force profile,
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
necessarily have the same sign, which is set by
f_{0}^{k}
. The sign of
f_{0}^{k}
is not determined simply by whether the event is fission or fusion.

One possible physical interpretation of this sign follows if the distal force is dominated by Stokesian drag on the bud or transport vesicle. The drag exerted by the fluid opposes its motion, so the equal and opposite force exerted by the bud or vesicle on the fluid is directed along its motion. Taking the outward normal as positive, this gives
f_{0}^{k}>0
for fission (
k=1
), when the bud moves outward, and
f_{0}^{k}<0
for fusion (
k=2
), when the vesicle moves towards the membrane. For this drag-dominated realisation, Eq. (
119
) therefore gives
\mathrm{sgn}(\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})=\mathrm{sgn}(\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})=(-1)^{k+1}
. The corresponding force dipoles are therefore extensile for fission and contractile for fusion. This sign relation is specific to this illustrative force profile and is not imposed in the general model.

As another illustrative example, we consider a different force profile to show that the first and second moments need not have the same sign. Specifically, we consider the force profile
\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
to be quadratic in the dimensionless normal coordinate
h/\Lambda
, that is,

\mathbb{F}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}(h)=f^{k}_{0}+f^{k}_{1}\left({h}/{\Lambda}\right)+f^{k}_{2}\left({h}/{\Lambda}\right)^{2},

(120)

where
f^{k}_{0}
,
f^{k}_{1}
and
f^{k}_{2}
are some constants. By imposing that force monopoles vanish, from Eq. (
114
), we find

f_{0}^{k}=-\frac{1}{6}\left(3f_{1}^{k}+2f_{2}^{k}\right),

(121)

which yields the first moment

\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\frac{1}{12}(f_{1}^{k}+f_{2}^{k})\Lambda^{2},

(122)

while the second moment is

\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\frac{1}{180}(15f_{1}^{k}+16f_{2}^{k})\Lambda^{3}.

(123)

In this case, the sign of
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
can be different from the sign of
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, provided that
-1<f_{2}^{k}/f_{1}^{k}<-15/16
. Thus, unlike the two-point force profile above, this quadratic force profile can produce first and second moments with opposite signs.

A.7.2
Locally planar sharp-interface reduction

To relate the explicit finite-thickness description to its sharp-interface reduction, we compare the corresponding stresses in the outer cytosolic fluid. In the finite-thickness formulation, the active layer of thickness
\Lambda
is retained explicitly; for brevity, we refer to this below as the
finite-
\Lambda
description
. In the sharp-interface description, the active layer is instead collapsed onto the membrane at
h=0
. The boundary conditions in the sharp-interface description are chosen so that its fluid solution coincides with that of the finite-
\Lambda
description outside the active layer,
h>\Lambda
. We define the resulting excess bulk stress by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{+}}.}

(124)

Here and below, the superscript
{\scriptstyle(\Lambda)}
identifies quantities in the finite-
\Lambda
description, while the corresponding quantities without this superscript are those of the sharp-interface description. Accordingly, the excess stress vanishes outside the active layer,
h>\Lambda
. Defining similarly the excess pressure and velocity by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{A}=p^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-p_{\boldsymbol{+}},\qquad\boldsymbol{V}_{\!A}=\boldsymbol{V}^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{+}}},}

(125)

the excess stress for a Newtonian fluid is

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}=-p_{A}\boldsymbol{I}+\eta\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!A}+\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!A}\right)^{\!\mathsf{T}}\right].}

(126)

We first determine these excess fields locally for a planar membrane, for which
H=K=0
and
J=1
, while allowing variations along both tangential directions. We denote the tangential coordinates by
x^{\alpha}
, with
\alpha=1,2
, and the corresponding gradient by
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}
. Since
\epsilon_{\Lambda}=\Lambda/L\ll 1
, normal variations across the active layer occur over the scale
\Lambda
, whereas tangential variations occur over the much larger scale
L
, so that
\partial_{h}\sim\Lambda^{-1}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}\sim L^{-1}
. Although
h/\Lambda
is generally of order unity within
0\leq h\leq\Lambda
, the ratio
h/L\leq\epsilon_{\Lambda}
remains small throughout the active layer. We decompose the excess velocity into tangential and normal components as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel},h)=\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel},h)+w_{A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel},h)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},}

(127)

where
\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel}=(x^{1},x^{2})
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}
contains both tangential components. Subtracting the homogeneous Stokes equations of the sharp-interface description from those of the finite-
\Lambda
description gives, within
0<h<\Lambda
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}p_{A}+\eta\left(\partial_{h}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}+\nabla_{\!\parallel}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}\right)=\boldsymbol{0},}

(128)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\partial_{h}p_{A}+\eta\left(\partial_{h}^{2}w_{A}+\nabla_{\!\parallel}^{2}w_{A}\right)+\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}=0,}

(129)

together with the incompressibility condition

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}+\partial_{h}w_{A}=0.}

(130)

If
\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}
has characteristic magnitude
U
, the incompressibility condition gives
w_{A}\sim\epsilon_{\Lambda}U
. In Eq. (
128
), the viscous term involving tangential derivatives,
\nabla_{\!\parallel}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}
, is smaller than
\partial_{h}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}
by a factor
\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}
. Similarly, the viscous terms in the normal momentum balance are higher order relative to the normal pressure gradient. To leading order in
\epsilon_{\Lambda}
, Eqs. (
128
) and (
129
) reduce to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\partial_{h}p_{A}+\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2})=0,}

(131)

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}p_{A}+\eta\partial_{h}^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2})=0.}

(132)

The tangential–tangential components of the excess viscous stress are smaller than the pressure contribution by the same order. In the locally planar Cartesian coordinates,
g^{\alpha\beta}
reduces to
\delta^{\alpha\beta}
, with
\delta^{\alpha\beta}
the Kronecker delta, so that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega^{\alpha\beta}_{\scriptstyle A}=-p_{A}\delta^{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}).}

(133)

The tangential–normal component of the excess stress must nevertheless be retained. We define the corresponding tangential traction across a surface of constant
h
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}=(\boldsymbol{I}-\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}.}

(134)

To the same order,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}=\eta\partial_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}).}

(135)

and Eq. (
132
) therefore gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{h}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}p_{A}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}).}

(136)

Since the two descriptions coincide outside the active layer, the excess pressure vanishes at its outer edge,
p_{A}(\Lambda)=0
. Integrating Eq. (
131
) with respect to the normal coordinate from
h
to
\Lambda
then gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel},h)=-\int_{h}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}s\;\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel},s)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}).}

(137)

Using the force-free condition in Eq. (
114
), Eq. (
137
) also shows that the leading-order excess pressure vanishes at the membrane,
p_{A}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\parallel},h=0)=0
. Integrating the pressure profile across the active layer and interchanging the order of integration then gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;p_{A}(h)=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)=\mathbb{P}.}

(138)

Also, its first moment about the membrane gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,p_{A}(h)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h^{2}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Q}.}

(139)

In the sharp-interface limit, the detailed stress distribution across the active layer is no longer resolved, but the net force and torque transmitted by the layer must be preserved. The effective in-plane stress is therefore obtained by integrating the tangential excess stress across the layer. In the locally planar limit,
b^{\beta}_{\gamma}=0
, so Eq. (
44
) reduces to
T^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}
, and we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=-\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;p_{A}(h)\delta^{\alpha\beta}=\mathbb{P}\,\delta^{\alpha\beta}.}

(140)

Because the excess stress is distributed at different normal distances from the membrane, it also transmits a torque about the surface
h=0
. Weighting the stress by the corresponding lever arm
h
therefore gives the effective bending moment. Using the definition of the bending moment tensor introduced in Eq. (
39
), we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,p_{A}(h)\delta^{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Q}\,\delta^{\alpha\beta}.}

(141)

The pressure profile also determines the tangential traction transmitted through the active layer. Since the two descriptions coincide at the outer edge of the layer, the excess tangential traction vanishes,
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}(h=\Lambda)=\boldsymbol{0}
. Integrating Eq. (
136
) with respect to the normal coordinate from
h
to
\Lambda
gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}(h)=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}\int_{h}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}s\;p_{A}(s).}

(142)

Evaluating Eq. (
142
) at the membrane,
h=0
, and using Eq. (
138
) gives the active-layer contribution to the tangential traction,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}(h=0)=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}\mathbb{P}.}

(143)

Using the definition of the excess stress in Eq. (
124
), together with
\partial_{\beta}\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\partial^{\alpha}\mathbb{P}
from Eq. (
140
), we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\tau}^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}(h=0)=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{+}}(h=0)+\partial_{\beta}\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}.}

(144)

Equation (
144
) makes the correspondence between the two descriptions explicit: the difference between the fluid traction at the physical membrane in the finite-
\Lambda
description and the fluid traction in the sharp-interface description is precisely the surface force generated by the active membrane surface stress
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}
. Thus, when the finite active layer is collapsed onto the membrane, the effect of the stresses distributed across that layer is retained through this active surface stress, while
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\boldsymbol{+}}(h=0)
remains the fluid traction acting directly on the membrane. The same tangential force balance is therefore preserved.

Finally, the finite active layer also modifies the relation between the tangential fluid and membrane velocities in the sharp-interface description. In the finite-
\Lambda
description, the cytosolic fluid obeys no slip at the physical membrane, whereas in the sharp-interface description its tangential velocity at the membrane differs from the membrane velocity by
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
. Since
\boldsymbol{V}_{\!A}=\boldsymbol{V}^{(\Lambda)}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{+}}}
, and the two fluid solutions coincide at the outer edge of the active layer, the tangential excess velocity satisfies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}(h=0)=-\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S},\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}(h=\Lambda)=\boldsymbol{0}.}

(145)

The difference between these two boundary values can be written as the integral of the normal derivative of the excess tangential velocity across the active layer,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\partial_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}=\frac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}(h),}

(146)

where the last equality follows from Eq. (
135
). Substituting the shear profile from Eq. (
142
) and interchanging the order of integration gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=-\frac{1}{\eta}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,p_{A}(h).}

(147)

Using Eq. (
139
), which relates this integral to the second moment
\mathbb{Q}
of the active force profile, we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boxed{\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=\frac{1}{2\eta}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\parallel}\mathbb{Q}.}}

(148)

A.7.3
Sharp-interface reduction for a curved membrane

Figure 7:
Diagram of a three-dimensional cell that includes the ambient fluid above a membrane surface patch
\mathcal{S}
, where the side boundaries are labeled by
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\{1,2\}
. Each point above the membrane is parameterized by the internal curvilinear coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
and the height
h
along the surface normal
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. Also, the side faces of the volume cell
\mathcal{B}
, that are associated with the boundaries
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
, are indicated by
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}
. Note that local tangent basis at height
h
above the membrane surface are slightly different than those on
\mathcal{S}
, due to the local change in curvature
b_{\alpha\beta}
; namely,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\hskip 0.5pt\big(\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}-h\hskip 1.0ptb^{\beta}_{\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\big)
.

The locally planar calculation above shows how the force and torque transmitted through the finite active layer are retained, in the sharp-interface limit, through an effective membrane stress, bending moment, and slip velocity. We now extend the same construction to a curved membrane. The finite-
\Lambda
and sharp-interface descriptions must transmit the same net force across any surface cutting through the active layer. In particular, the force carried by the excess bulk stress across a side face
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}
must be equal to that transmitted by the active membrane traction across the corresponding boundary curve
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. Thus, we must impose that

\int_{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}}\!\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{S}\,\;\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}\!\!\mathrm{d}l\;\nu_{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}.

(149)

Here,
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}
denotes the side face obtained by extending the corresponding boundary curve
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
of the membrane patch
\mathcal{S}
along the surface normal, as illustrated in Fig.
7
; its intersection with the membrane at
h=0
is
\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. Parameterizing this boundary curve by
\lambda
, such that
\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{R}[\xi^{1}(\lambda),\xi^{2}(\lambda)]
, the unit vector
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
normal to the side face
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}
is given by

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\textstyle\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi^{\beta}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\times\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)\left|\tilde{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi^{\beta}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\right|^{-1}\!\!,

(150)

whereas the tangent basis are found to be

\tilde{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{\alpha}\,\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{R}}}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\hskip 0.5pt\big(\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}-h\hskip 1.0ptb^{\beta}_{\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\big).

(151)

Furthermore, the area element
\mathrm{d}\tilde{S}
can be expressed in this parametrization as
\mathrm{d}\tilde{S}\!=\!\mathrm{d}h\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}\lambda\left|\tilde{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi^{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\right|
, leading to

\mathrm{d}\tilde{S}\;\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\mathrm{d}h\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{d}\lambda\;\varepsilon_{\alpha\gamma}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\big(\delta_{\beta}^{\gamma}-h\hskip 1.0ptb_{\beta}^{\gamma}\hskip 1.0pt\big)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi^{\beta}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda},

(152)

where the identity
\varepsilon_{\alpha\gamma}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\gamma}\times\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
is used, with
\varepsilon_{\alpha\gamma}
as the Levi-Civita tensor. Similarly, the line element is

\mathrm{d}l\,\nu_{\alpha}=\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi^{\beta}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}.

(153)

Substituting these geometrical relations into Eq. (
149
), and requiring the force matching to hold for an arbitrary boundary curve, gives the active stress vectors
[
55
,
56
]
:

\boxed{\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\!\int_{0\,}^{\,\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}\left(\hskip 1.0ptg^{\alpha\beta}\!-h\,\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}\right)\!.}

(154)

where
\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}=2Hg^{\alpha\beta}-b^{\alpha\beta}
is cofactor of the curvature tensor, cf. Eq. (
520
). Thus, Eq. (
154
) is the curved-surface counterpart of the thickness-integrated excess stress used in the locally planar calculation above. It follows from the force-matching condition and the geometry of the surfaces displaced along the membrane normal, without yet making an approximation for the form of
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}
. We next evaluate the excess stress in Eq. (
154
) using the same separation between the normal scale
\Lambda
and the tangential scale
L
introduced in the locally planar calculation, with ratio
\epsilon_{\Lambda}=\Lambda/L\ll 1
.

The physical body-force density entering the bulk momentum balance is
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}/J(h)
, cf. Eq. (
107
). The leading normal momentum balance within the curved active layer is therefore

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\partial_{h}p_{A}+\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)}{J(h)}=0,}

(155)

where the neglected viscous terms are smaller by a relative factor of order
\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}
, as in the locally planar calculation. Since the finite-
\Lambda
and sharp-interface descriptions coincide at the outer edge of the active layer,
p_{A}(\Lambda)=0
, and Eq. (
155
) gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{A}(h)=-\int_{h}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}s\;\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(s)}{J(s)}.}

(156)

The tangential–tangential components of the excess viscous stress are again smaller than the pressure contribution by a relative factor of order
\epsilon_{\Lambda}^{2}
. Hence, to the order required here,
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}=-p_{A}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}
. Substituting this result into Eq. (
154
) gives the in-plane part of the active stress vector as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=-\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;p_{A}(h)\left(g^{\alpha\beta}-h\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}\right).}

(157)

Using Eq. (
156
) and interchanging the order of integration, this becomes

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)}{J(h)}\left[h\,g^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{h^{2}}{2}\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}\right].}

(158)

The remaining expansion is purely geometrical. Since
h/L\leq\epsilon_{\Lambda}\ll 1
throughout the active layer, we use
J^{-1}(h)\simeq 1+2hH
together with
\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}=2Hg^{\alpha\beta}-b^{\alpha\beta}
. The geometrical factor in Eq. (
158
) then becomes
