{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{h\,g^{\alpha\beta}-{h^{2}}\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}/2}{J(h)}\simeq h\,g^{\alpha\beta}+h^{2}(Hg^{\alpha\beta}+b^{\alpha\beta}/2).}

(159)

The neglected terms are of order
(h/L)^{2}
and involve the third and higher moments of the active force profile. Retaining only the first and second moments defined in Eq. (
117
), Eq. (
158
) therefore reduces to

T^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}\hskip-1.0pt=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\,\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)\left[h\,g^{\alpha\beta}+h^{2}\!\left(Hg^{\alpha\beta}+b^{\alpha\beta}/2\right)\right].

(160)

Thus, the active in-plane stress tensor is

\boxed{T^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\mathbb{P}\,g^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Q}\left(2Hg^{\alpha\beta}+b^{\alpha\beta}\right).}

(161)

For a locally flat membrane, where the curvature tensor vanishes,
b^{\alpha\beta}=0
and hence
H=0
, Eq. (
161
) reduces to the locally planar result in Eq. (
140
). The additional term proportional to
\mathbb{Q}
therefore arises from the local membrane curvature.

Figure 8:
Schematic of an infinitesimal volume
\delta\tilde{V}=\delta h\,\delta\tilde{S}
of the ambient fluid, which is positioned away from the membrane between the heights
h
and
h+\delta h
, along the unit vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
normal to the membrane
\mathcal{S}
. The area element
\delta\tilde{S}
shown at height
h
differs from its associated membrane area
\delta S
because of the local curvature. They are related by
\delta\tilde{S}=J(h)\delta S
, where
J(h)=1-2hH+h^{2}K
, so that the physical volume of the segment is
\delta\tilde{V}=J(h)\delta S\,\delta h
. The force associated with this segment is
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)\delta S\,\delta h\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, and the corresponding three-dimensional body-force density is therefore
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}/J(h)
.

The same matching argument can be applied to the torque transmitted through the active layer. Requiring the integrated torque to be identical in the finite-
\Lambda
and sharp-interface descriptions gives

\int_{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}}\!\tilde{\!\boldsymbol{R}}\times\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}\right)\mathrm{d}\tilde{S}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}}\!\!\nu_{\alpha}\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\times\boldsymbol{M}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptstyle A}^{\alpha}+\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{T}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-2.5pt\scriptstyle A}\right)\mathrm{d}l.

(162)

By the same geometrical construction as for Eq. (
154
), the corresponding active director traction is
[
55
,
56
]
:

\boldsymbol{M}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptstyle A}^{\alpha}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\scriptstyle A}\right)\left(g^{\alpha\beta}-h\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}\right).

(163)

Using the pressure profile in Eq. (
156
) and the definition
M^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}=-\boldsymbol{M}^{\alpha}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptstyle A}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\beta}
, we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}=-\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)}{J(h)}\left[\frac{h^{2}}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{h^{3}}{3}\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}\right].}

(164)

The leading contribution in Eq. (
164
) is proportional to
h^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}
, whose integral over the active layer gives
\mathbb{Q}
. The curvature-dependent corrections carry one additional power of
h
and contribute through the third and higher normal moments. Truncating consistently at the second moment therefore gives

\boxed{M^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Q}\,g^{\alpha\beta}.}

(165)

In addition, due to the conservation of angular momentum, cf. Eq. (
45
), the active out-of-plane shear stress is thus found to be

N^{\alpha}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptstyle A}=-\nabla_{\!\beta}M^{\beta\alpha}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptstyle A}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\!\beta}\mathbb{Q}.

(166)

The symmetric in-plane stress tensor due to activity, which is defined by
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=T^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}-b^{\beta}_{\gamma}M^{\gamma\alpha}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}
, reduces to

\boxed{\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}=\mathbb{P}\,g^{\alpha\beta}+\mathbb{Q}\left(Hg^{\alpha\beta}+b^{\alpha\beta}\right).}

(167)

This readily shows how the active force distribution can contribute to the total in-plane stress of the membrane, through its nonzero first and second moments.

It remains to determine the corresponding slip velocity on the curved membrane. To the order at which the first two normal moments are retained, the tangential momentum balance within the thin active layer has the same local form as in Eq. (
136
), with the planar gradient replaced by the surface covariant gradient. Curvature corrections to this relation contain an additional factor of
h/L
and contribute only through higher moments. Thus, the tangential excess traction satisfies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{h}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\!\alpha}p_{A}.}

(168)

Since
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}(\Lambda)=\boldsymbol{0}
, integration across the active layer gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}(h)=-\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\int_{h}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}s\;p_{A}(s).}

(169)

Likewise, the leading relation between tangential traction and the normal gradient of the excess tangential velocity remains
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\!A}=\eta\partial_{h}\boldsymbol{u}_{\!A}
. Hence, using the same boundary matching as in Eq. (
145
), the slip velocity is

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=-\frac{1}{\eta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,p_{A}(h).}

(170)

Using Eq. (
156
), the pressure moment appearing here can be written as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;h\,p_{A}(h)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\!\mathrm{d}h\;\frac{h^{2}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptstyle A}(h)}{J(h)}.}

(171)

Expanding
J^{-1}(h)
in
h/L
, the leading term is
-\mathbb{Q}/2
, while the first curvature-dependent correction involves the third moment and is beyond the order retained here. Hence we recover the sharp-interface slip velocity

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boxed{\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=\frac{1}{2\eta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\!\alpha}\mathbb{Q}.}}

(172)

Thus, the locally planar and curved calculations give the same leading slip velocity, with the ordinary tangential gradient replaced by the covariant surface gradient. Together, Eqs. (
167
), (
165
), and (
172
) give the active contributions retained in the sharp-interface description through the first two moments of the active force profile.

A.8
Membrane equations of motion

The free-energy density
F
in Eq. (
62
) can be written using the adiabatically reduced quantities
V_{\mathrm{m}}
and
C_{\mathrm{m}}
derived in Appendix A.5 as

F={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}}+2\kappa H{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\mathrm{m}}}+2\kappa\hskip 0.25ptH^{2}.

(173)

Here,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}
represents the composition-renormalized surface tension, defined by

\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}=\Sigma+V_{\mathrm{m}},

(174)

where
V_{\mathrm{m}}
is given in Eq. (
87
). We also define the difference in the dimensionless chemical-potential of component
k
relative to the passive membrane background,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E_{k}\equiv\mathcal{E}_{k}-\mathcal{E}_{0}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\frac{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}}\left[\mathcal{E}^{k}_{n}-\mathcal{E}_{0}+\ln\frac{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}}\right].

(175)

Using
1-\Phi_{0}=\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}
, the composition-dependent spontaneous curvature can be equivalently written as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\mathrm{m}}}\!=\mathcal{C}_{0}+\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\hskip 1.0ptC_{k}
,
where
C_{k}
denotes the difference in curvature coupling of component
k
relative to the passive membrane background,

C_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\equiv\mathcal{C}_{k}-\mathcal{C}_{0}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\aleph_{k}}\frac{\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{n}\hskip-1.0pt-\mathcal{C}_{0}\right)}{\mathbb{R}^{k}_{n}}.

(176)

By using the free-energy in Eq. (
173
), the in-plane stress tensor
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{0}
and the bending moment tensor
M^{\alpha\beta}_{0}
are determined from Eq. (
67
) and (
68
), respectively. Furthermore, the overall in-plane (symmetrized) stress tensor
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}=\sigma_{0}^{\alpha\beta}+\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\hskip-1.5pt\scriptstyle A}
is found to be

\sigma^{\alpha\beta}\hskip-1.5pt=\hskip-0.5pt\left(F+\mathbb{P}+H\mathbb{Q}\right)\hskip-0.5ptg^{\alpha\beta}\hskip-1.0pt+\left(\hskip-1.5pt\mathbb{Q}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\right)b^{\alpha\beta}\hskip-1.0pt,

(177)

whereas the bending moment tensor
M^{\alpha\beta}
is given by

M^{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\hskip-1.0pt\mathbb{Q}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial H}\right)g^{\alpha\beta}.

(178)

This allows us to obtain the equations of motion, as derived in Eq. (
46
) and (
47
), along the normal and tangential direction of the membrane, respectively.

As a result, the force balance equation along tangential direction is found to be

\displaystyle\quad\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}

\displaystyle+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}}+2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25ptH\hskip 0.85pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\mathrm{m}}}

\displaystyle+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\mathbb{P}+H\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\mathbb{Q}+2\,\mathbb{Q}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}H\,,

(179)

by substituting Eq. (
173
) into (
177
) and (
178
), and then expanding out the terms in Eq. (
47
). Moreover, by using Eq. (
87
), the definitions in Eq. (
117
), as well as Eqs. (
174
–
176
), this can be further rewritten as

\displaystyle\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\Sigma+\sum_{k}\!\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+H\!\left(2\kappa C_{k}+\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right)\right]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\Phi_{k}

\displaystyle+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\sum_{k}\!\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{0}}\right){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\Phi_{k}+2\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}H.

(180)

Similarly, the force-balance along the normal direction to the membrane can be found via Eq. (
46
) as follows:

\displaystyle\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=f+2H\!\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}}+\mathbb{P}\right)-2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\!\left[\Delta H+2H\!\left(H^{2}\hskip-1.0pt-K\right)\hskip-1.0pt\right]

\displaystyle-\hskip-1.0pt\kappa\hskip-1.0pt\left(\Delta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\mathrm{m}}}\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip-1.0pt2K{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\mathrm{m}}}\right)+\frac{\Delta\mathbb{Q}}{2}+\mathbb{Q}\left(4H^{2}\hskip-2.5pt-\hskip-1.0ptK\right)\!,

(181)

which can be subsequently expanded as

\displaystyle\!\!\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}

\displaystyle=f-2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\!\left[\Delta H+2H\left(H^{2}\hskip-1.0pt-K\right)\right]+\hskip-1.0pt2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25ptK\hskip 0.5pt\mathcal{C}_{0}\,

\displaystyle+\hskip-1.0pt2H{\left[\Sigma+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{0}+\ln\Phi_{0}\right)\right]}

\displaystyle+{2H}\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\!\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\!\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\Phi_{k}}{\Phi_{0}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\,\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\!\left[2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25ptK\hskip 0.5ptC_{k}+\left(4H^{2}\!-\!K\right)\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{k}\Delta\Phi_{k}\!\left(\kappa\hskip 0.25ptC_{k}\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip-1.0pt{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}/{2}\hskip 0.75pt\right)\hskip-1.0pt\!,

(182)

using Eq. (
87
) and Eqs. (
174
–
176
).
The terms proportional to
2H
in Eqs. (
181
) and (
182
) identify the activity-renormalized surface tension
\Sigma_{A}
:

\boxed{{\Sigma_{A}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}+\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\Sigma_{\mathrm{m}}+\mathbb{P}.}}

(183)

The direct mechanical contribution to
\Sigma_{A}
is the total first moment
\mathbb{P}
. For the simple point-force dipolar model in Eq. (
118
), with the sign set by viscous drag,
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}>0
for fission and
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}<0
for fusion. The fission first-moment contribution therefore raises the activity-renormalized surface tension, whereas the fusion contribution lowers it. More generally, these signs depend on the cycle-averaged force profiles and are not fixed by whether the underlying process is fission or fusion.

Similarly, we identify an activity-renormalized spontaneous curvature that includes both the composition-dependent contribution
C_{\mathrm{m}}
and the direct mechanical contributions from the second force moments
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, namely

\boxed{C_{A}={C_{\mathrm{m}}-\frac{\mathbb{Q}}{2\kappa}}=\mathcal{C}_{0}+\!\sum_{k}\hskip-1.0pt\Phi_{k}\!\left(C_{k}-\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2\kappa}\right).}

(184)

For the same simple point-force dipolar model, with forcing set by viscous drag,
\mathbb{Q}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}^{1}>0
for fission and
\mathbb{Q}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}^{2}<0
for fusion. The second-moment contribution therefore decreases
C_{A}
for fission and increases it for fusion. More generally, the direction of this shift is determined by the sign of the corresponding active second moment.

A.9
Coupling to the ambient fluid

On either side of the organelle, its closed membrane surface
\mathcal{M}
is in contact with an ambient fluid. In general, this surrounding fluid provides the dominant dissipative mechanism for the relaxation of shape deformations on large length scales
[
94
]
, corresponding here to length scales larger than the coarse-graining length in our model of the membrane system (that is,
\sim 50
–
100\;
nm). Accordingly, on the length scales resolved by our coarse-grained model, the membrane is hydrodynamically coupled to the bulk fluid through interfacial conditions on both the interior and exterior faces of the membrane
[
95
]
. The normal component of the fluid velocity couples to shape deformations of the membrane, whereas tangential fluid motion drives in-plane membrane flows.

Here, we assume that the inner and outer bulk fluids are locally incompressible and obey the Stokes equations. This is motivated by the small Reynolds number in our system (
\mathrm{Re}\lessapprox 10^{-4}
), with the advective inertial terms being negligible, compared with the viscous forces. Thus, the fluid motion is governed by

\boxed{\eta\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\cdot\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=0,}

(185)

where the Laplacian
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\!=\!\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}
, with
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
as the usual three-dimensional gradient operator, and
\eta
is the shear viscosity of the bulk fluid, taken to be the same inside and outside the organelle. Here,
\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}(\boldsymbol{r})
and
p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}(\boldsymbol{r})
are the fluid velocities and pressures at position
\boldsymbol{r}
, with the subscripts “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{-}
” and “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{+}
” indicating respectively whether the fluid is located inside and outside of the membrane compartment. The last relation in Eq. (
185
) is the incompressibility condition of the bulk fluid, whereas the former equation is the momentum conservation relationship, which follows from the stress balance condition:

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{0},

(186)

where the Stokesian stresses, which are denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
for the respective inner and outer flows, are given by

\boxed{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=-p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\,\boldsymbol{I}+\eta\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right)+\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right)^{\!\mathsf{T}}\hskip 0.5pt\right]\hskip-0.5pt\!.}

(187)

As discussed in Appendix A.7, the active body-force density acts within a finite layer on the cytosolic side of the membrane. In the sharp-interface description used here, this finite active layer is no longer resolved explicitly, and the inner and outer bulk fluids therefore obey the homogeneous Stokes equations in Eq. (
186
). The force and torque transmitted through the active layer are instead retained through the active in-plane stress
\sigma^{\alpha\beta}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
and bending moment
M^{\alpha\beta}_{\!\scriptstyle A}
, while flow within the layer produces the slip velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
. The in-plane stress depends on both the first and second moments of the active force profile, whereas the bending moment and slip velocity depend only on the second moment.

The hydrodynamic force per unit area
\boldsymbol{f}
exerted by the ambient fluids on the membrane is given by the jump in bulk-fluid traction, cf. Eq. (
35
). Hence,

\boxed{\boldsymbol{f}=\left(\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right)\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\,\equiv\,\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{-}},\;\mathrm{at}\;\,\boldsymbol{r}=\!\boldsymbol{R},}

(188)

where
\boldsymbol{R}
is the position vector of the membrane surface,
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
is the normal unit vector to the membrane, and we define the traction vector acting across the surface as

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}.

(189)

In the local coordinate system of the membrane, we can decompose Eq. (
188
) into the normal component

f=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\Big[\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\!\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\!\boldsymbol{R}\right)-\,\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\!\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\!\boldsymbol{R}\right)\Big],

(190)

as well as along the in-plane membrane components

f^{\alpha}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\cdot\Big[\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\!\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\!\boldsymbol{R}\right)-\,\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\!\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\!\boldsymbol{R}\right)\Big].

(191)

The coupling between the membrane and the bulk fluids is specified by the interfacial velocity condition,

\boxed{\boldsymbol{v}\,+\,\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}\,{-\,{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}\,=\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{R}\right)\!,}

(192)

where
\boldsymbol{v}
is the membrane velocity. The tangential correction
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
is the slip velocity generated by flow within the finite active layer and is derived in Appendix A.7. The normal correction
-{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
arises from volume exchange at the membrane, where
{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
is the volume flux per unit area associated with passive osmosis and active volume exchange due to fission and fusion, as discussed in Appendix A.10.

Additionally, we consider that the fluid flow is at rest at infinity and bounded at the origin of the compartment:

\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to 0}\left|\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{-}}}(\boldsymbol{r})\right|<\infty},&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to 0}p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{-}}}(\boldsymbol{r})=P_{-}},\\[5.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to\infty}\left|\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{+}}}(\boldsymbol{r})\right|=0},&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to\infty}p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{+}}}(\boldsymbol{r})=P_{+}}.\end{cases}

(193)

where
P_{\pm}
are scalar pressure constants. The exterior pressure
P_{+}
sets the overall pressure reference, while the pressure difference
P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\equiv P_{+}-P_{-}
is determined by the global volume constraint in Eq. (
208
).

These boundary conditions, together with the interfacial velocity condition in Eq. (
192
) and the traction balance in Eq. (
188
), complete the specification of the inner and outer Stokes flows. On the length and time scales considered here, the membrane dynamics is overdamped by the viscous traction exerted by the ambient fluids. We therefore neglect the inertial term
\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}
in Eqs. (
182
) and (
180
) relative to the hydrodynamic traction
\boldsymbol{f}
, so that

\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=\boldsymbol{0}.

(194)

The membrane force balance thus reduces to a balance between the hydrodynamic traction and the passive and active membrane stresses.

A.10
Osmosis and volume dynamics

Organelle membranes are semipermeable, allowing solvent to cross the membrane more readily than solute, so that differences in solute concentration can drive osmotic transport
[
96
]
. For dilute solutions, where solute–solute interactions can be neglected, the solvent chemical potential is given by
[
97
]
:

\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}\!\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\right)=\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}_{0}\!\left(P_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\right)-k_{B}T\hskip 1.0pt\nu_{0}\hskip 1.0ptc_{\boldsymbol{\pm}},

(195)

where
c_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
are the solute number densities, while
\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}_{0}
is the bulk chemical potential associated with the pure solvent at a uniform pressure
P_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
and temperature
T
, with the subscripts “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{+}
” and “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{-}
” referring to quantities outside and inside of the organelle, respectively. The scalars
P_{\pm}
are the reference pressures defined by the boundary conditions in Eq. (
193
), while
\nu_{0}=\partial\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}_{0}/\partial P
is the molecular volume of the pure solvent at fixed
T
.

Osmotic equilibrium corresponds to a vanishing solvent chemical-potential difference across the membrane,

\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}\!\left(P_{\boldsymbol{+}}\right)=\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}\!\left(P_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right)\!.

(196)

Defining the hydrostatic pressure difference across the membrane as
P_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\equiv P_{\boldsymbol{+}}-P_{\boldsymbol{-}}
, and taking
\nu_{0}
to be approximately constant over this pressure difference, Eq. (
196
) gives the dilute-solution
van’t Hoff
relation
[
97
]
,

\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=k_{B}T\left(c_{\boldsymbol{+}}-c_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right),\qquad P_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}.

(197)

Away from osmotic equilibrium, the solvent chemical potentials on the two sides of the membrane differ, with

{\delta\hskip-0.5pt\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}\equiv\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}\!\left(P_{\boldsymbol{+}}\right)-\mu^{\scriptstyle\hskip-1.0pt\mathfrak{B}}\!\left(P_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right)=\nu_{0}\left(P_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right).}

(198)

We model passive solvent permeation by taking the corresponding flux to depend linearly on this chemical-potential difference. Since solvent permeation through the membrane typically occurs on timescales of order minutes
[
98
]
, the passive osmotic contribution to the volume dynamics is correspondingly slow.

Fission and fusion provide an active contribution to the exchange of lumenal volume. The corresponding local volume flux generated by the trafficking cycles is

\mathbb{V}=\!\sum_{k,n,m}\!\mathbb{V}^{k}_{nm}r^{k}_{nm}\phi^{k}_{m}=\sum_{k}\,\mathbb{J}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\sum_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}\mathbb{V}^{k}_{n+1,n},

(199)

where the second equality uses the adiabatic approximation of Appendix A.5. Using Eq. (
84
), we can write

\boxed{\mathbb{V}=\sum_{k}\,\Phi_{k}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}},}

(200)

where
\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
is the volume transfer rate associated with cycle
k
, defined by

\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}{\sum_{n=1}^{\aleph_{k}}\mathbb{V}^{k}_{n+1,n}}.

(201)

Its sign is negative for the fissogens (
k=1
) and positive for the fusogens (
k=2
):

\mathrm{sgn}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right)=(-1)^{k}.

(202)

The total volume flux per unit membrane area combines the active trafficking contribution with passive osmotic permeation. Denoting by
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
the scalar volume flux into the organelle and by
\boldsymbol{j}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle V}
the corresponding vector volume flux, we write

\boxed{{{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}=\mathbb{V}-w_{0}\hskip-0.5pt\left(\hskip 1.0pt\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-P_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right),\quad\boldsymbol{j}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle V}=-{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}.}}

(203)

Since
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
is the outward membrane normal,
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}>0
corresponds to volume transport into the organelle and thus increases the enclosed volume. The corresponding change in the enclosed volume is therefore

\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}t}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\,\mathrm{d}S.

(204)

A nonzero volume flux also modifies the kinematic coupling between the membrane and the surrounding fluids. In the absence of volume exchange, the normal fluid and membrane velocities coincide; however, with volume exchange, this instead becomes

{\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\big|_{\mathcal{M}}-\boldsymbol{v}\right)\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=-{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}.}

(205)

Thus, the interfacial fluid velocity acquires the normal correction
-{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. Including the tangential slip velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
generated by the active layer then we recover the full interfacial condition in Eq. (
192
).

Let
\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
denote the number of solute molecules enclosed by the organelle. Assuming that the interior solution is well mixed, its concentration is
c_{\boldsymbol{-}}=\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}/V
, and hence

\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=k_{B}T\,\bigg[c_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\frac{\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}}{V}\bigg].

(206)

In general, both
V
and
\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
may change through vesicular trafficking or lumenal cargo processing. Fission and fusion can exchange solvent and solute cargo, while cargo maturation may also alter the enclosed solute content. A more detailed description would require additional balance equations for the lumenal solute species, including both trafficking fluxes and internal reactions, which we do not consider here.

The hydrostatic pressure difference
P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
is determined by requiring consistency between volume exchange across the membrane and the resulting motion of the membrane surface. Equation (
204
) gives the rate at which volume is added to or removed from the organelle by transport, whereas the same change in enclosed volume can be expressed geometrically in terms of the normal membrane velocity. As derived in Appendix D.3,

\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}t}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}v\,\mathrm{d}S,

(207)

where
v
is the normal membrane velocity. Since both expressions represent the same change in the enclosed volume, equating them gives the volume constraint

\int_{\mathcal{M}}\!\mathrm{d}S\,\bigg[v+w_{0}\left(\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-P_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right)-\sum_{k}\Phi_{k}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\bigg]=0.

(208)

This gives us
P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
for a given membrane velocity, osmotic pressure, and active volume exchange. This is a global condition, as its integrand need not vanish pointwise.

A.11
Boundary integral formulation

A more general approach to solving the incompressible Stokes equations in Eq. (
185
) in the presence of an interface with a surface force
\boldsymbol{f}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
, cf. Eq. (
188
), involves a boundary integral representation
[
99
]
. A starting point is the use of the Lorentz reciprocal identity
[
100
]
, which states that any two flows
\boldsymbol{V}
and
\boldsymbol{V}^{\prime}
with their corresponding stress tensors
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\prime}
satisfy:

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\left[{V}^{\prime}_{i}\,\Omega_{ik}-{V}_{i}\,\Omega^{\prime}_{ik}\right]=0.

(209)

Here,
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
denotes the spatial position in the fluid domain.
We choose
\boldsymbol{V}^{\prime}
to be the flow generated by a point force
\boldsymbol{g}=g_{i}\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{i}
located at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
, with
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{i}
denoting the Cartesian basis vectors. We write the velocity field as
V^{\prime}_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})=G_{ij}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})g_{j}/(8\pi\eta)
, where
G_{ij}
is the velocity Green’s function for a point force at
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
. The corresponding stress is
\Omega^{\prime}_{ik}(\boldsymbol{x})=T_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})g_{j}/(8\pi)
, where
T_{ijk}
is the associated stress Green’s function
[
99
]
. Since the point force
\boldsymbol{g}
is arbitrary, we obtain

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\big[G_{ij}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\Omega_{ik}(\boldsymbol{x})-\eta{V}_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})T_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\big]\!=0.

(210)

Integrating Eq. (
210
) over a control volume bounded by a closed surface
\mathcal{S}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}
and applying the divergence theorem, with
\mathcal{L}_{jk}=G_{ij}\Omega_{ik}-\eta V_{i}T_{ijk}
, gives, when the source point
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
lies outside the control volume,

\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}}\mathcal{L}_{jk}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\,n_{k}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{x})=0.

(211)

When
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
lies inside the control volume, the point-force Green’s functions
G_{ij}
and
T_{ijk}
are singular at
\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
. We first exclude a small spherical neighborhood around
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
before applying the divergence theorem and then take its radius to zero, yielding
[
100
,
99
]

\int_{\mathcal{S}_{\scriptscriptstyle C}}\mathcal{L}_{jk}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\,n_{k}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{x})=-8\pi\eta V_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}).

(212)

As the source point approaches the boundary, the two terms in the boundary-integral representation behave differently. The term involving
G_{ij}
acts on the surface traction and is referred to as
the single-layer potential
; it remains continuous across the interface. The term involving
T_{ijk}
acts on the surface velocity and is referred to as
the double-layer potential
; its limiting value has a finite jump across the interface, with the remaining singular integral understood in the principal-value sense
[
99
]
.

We now apply these identities to the exterior and interior flows
\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\pm}
separated by the closed membrane interface
\mathcal{M}
. For the exterior flow, together with its decay at infinity, we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V_{+}}(\boldsymbol{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M\;}}\boldsymbol{V}^{\mathsf{T}}\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{x})\hskip 1.5pt\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{r})\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi\eta}\int_{\mathcal{M\;}}\!\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{x})\,\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{+}(\boldsymbol{x})\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{x}),

(213)

where the symmetry property
G_{ij}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})=G_{ji}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{x})
is used to switch the order of indices
[
99
]
.

The corresponding interior representation contains the same two types of terms. Since the fluid velocity at the membrane is the same when approached from either side, and the inner and outer viscosities are equal, the double-layer potentials associated with the stresslet cancel when the two representations are combined. The remaining single-layer contribution depends only on the traction jump
\boldsymbol{f}=(\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{+}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{-})\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
defined in Eq. (
188
), giving

\boxed{\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\pm}(\boldsymbol{r})=-\frac{1}{8\pi\eta}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{x})\,\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{x}).}

(214)

Thus, the fluid velocity throughout both domains, including its limiting value at the membrane, is determined directly by the interfacial traction jump
\boldsymbol{f}
.

For the equal-viscosity case considered here,
G_{ij}
is the same in both fluid domains and is given by the free-space Green’s function, the Oseen tensor
\mathscr{S}_{ij}

[
99
]
. Thus,
G_{ij}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}_{0})=\mathscr{S}_{ij}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})
, where
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
is the separation vector from the source point
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
to the field point
\boldsymbol{x}
, with

{\mathscr{S}_{ij}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{\,\left|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\right|^{\phantom{1}}\!}+\frac{\hat{x}_{i}\hat{x}_{j}}{\left|\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\right|^{3}}.}

(215)

The pressure fields follow from the corresponding free-space pressure Green’s function
\mathscr{P}(\hat{\boldsymbol{y}})=2\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}/|\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}|^{3}

[
99
]
, where
\hat{\boldsymbol{y}}=\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{x}
is the separation vector from the source point
\boldsymbol{x}
on the membrane to the field point
\boldsymbol{r}
. Taking the exterior pressure
P_{+}
as the reference, this representation gives the pressure on either side of the membrane,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}(\boldsymbol{r})=P_{+}-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathscr{P}(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{x}).}

(216)

Here,
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
correspond to
\boldsymbol{r}
lying outside and inside the membrane, respectively. Although both are written relative to
P_{+}
, the pressure difference
P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=P_{+}-P_{-}
enters through the hydrostatic contribution
-P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
to the traction jump
\boldsymbol{f}
. For a closed membrane, the integral
\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathscr{P}(\boldsymbol{r}-\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}S
vanishes outside the membrane and equals
-8\pi
inside. This contribution leaves the exterior pressure unchanged and shifts the interior pressure by
-P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
, thereby recovering the interior reference pressure
P_{-}=P_{+}-P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
. Thus, while
P_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
shifts the interior pressure, it does not generate a fluid velocity for a closed membrane; its value is fixed through the global volume constraint in Eq. (
208
).

Appendix B
Quasi-spherical Perturbation Analysis

B.1
Surface parameterization

The results derived so far are coordinate-invariant and valid for any closed surface
\mathcal{M}
, described by an embedding function
\boldsymbol{R}
(its position vector). We now assume that the membrane surface can be described as a small deformation of a sphere, which is given by

\boxed{\boldsymbol{R}=R\left[1+\varepsilon\,u\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\right]\boldsymbol{\hat{r}},}

(217)

in which we employ the spherical coordinates
(r,\hskip 1.0pt\theta,\hskip 1.0pt\varphi)
centered on the fixed, undeformed sphere of radius
R
. Here,
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
is the radial unit vector, which is given by

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}=\left(\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\,\sin\theta\sin\varphi,\,\cos\theta\hskip 0.5pt\right)^{\textsf{T}}\!\hskip-1.0pt,

(218)

where the inclination angle
\theta\in{[0,\pi]}
, and the azimuth angle
\varphi\in[0,2\pi)
. Furthermore, the unit tangent vectors along the spherical angular directions
\theta
and
\varphi
are denoted by
\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}
and
\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}
, respectively. By using Eq. (
488
), the explicit forms of these two unit vectors are found to be

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\left(-\sin\varphi,\,\cos\varphi,\,0\hskip 1.0pt\right)^{\textsf{T}}\!,\qquad\mathrm{and}

(219)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}\,

\displaystyle=\left(\cos\theta\cos\varphi,\,\cos\theta\sin\varphi,\,-\sin\theta\hskip 0.5pt\right)^{\textsf{T}}\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(220)

Herein,
\varepsilon\ll 1
is a small dimensionless parameter which can ultimately be absorbed into the arbitrary angular function
u\left(\theta,\varphi\right)
. It is introduced only to keep track of the perturbative order of the quantities subsequently computed from the shape parameterization in Eq. (
217
).

We assume that
u(\theta,\varphi)
is a well-behaved function that can be further expanded in a series of surface spherical harmonics, denoted by the subscript
\ell
; namely, we have

\boldsymbol{R}=R\hskip 1.0pt\Big[1+\varepsilon\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}u_{\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\hskip-1.0pt\Big]\boldsymbol{\hat{r}},

(221)

where the surface harmonics
u_{\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)
can be written as

u_{\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)=\sum^{\ell}\limits_{m=-\ell}u_{\ell,m}\;{Y}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\!,

(222)

where
u_{\ell}(\theta,\varphi)
denotes the complete spherical-harmonic component of degree
\ell
, while
u_{\ell,m}
is the amplitude associated with the individual spherical harmonic
{Y}_{\ell,m}

[
101
]
. Note that the zeroth harmonic
u_{0}
results in a uniform rescaling of the radius over which we perturb. The first harmonics correspond to a translation of the whole sphere at linear order, while the second harmonics describe an ellipsoidal deformation of the sphere.

B.2
Lamb’s general solution of Stokes equations

In spherical coordinates, H. Lamb
[
57
]
gave a general solution for the Stokes equations in the absence of body forces within the bulk fluid. To derive this solution, we first employ the vector identity of the Laplacian
\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\!\boldsymbol{V}\right)-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\!\boldsymbol{V}\right)
, and then take the divergence of the first vector equation in Eq. (
185
), which gives

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}\right)=\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\!\boldsymbol{V}\right)=\frac{1}{\eta}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}p,

(223)

omitting for brevity the subscript “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\pm}
”. This implies that the pressure field
p
must be a harmonic function,

\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}p=0,

(224)

by the incompressibility condition
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\!\boldsymbol{V}=0
. We expand the pressure in a series of solid spherical harmonics:

p(r,\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\,p_{\ell}(r,\theta,\varphi),

(225)

where for
\ell\geq 0
, the regular solid spherical harmonic
p_{\ell}
of degree
\ell
can be explicitly written as

p_{\hskip 0.45pt\ell}(r,\theta,\varphi)=\sum^{\ell}\limits_{m=-\ell}p_{\hskip 0.45pt\ell,m}\,r^{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt{Y}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\!,

(226)

where
p_{\hskip 0.45pt\ell,m}
is the spherical harmonic amplitude corresponding to the angular mode numbers
m
and
\ell
.

This expansion forms the basis of Lamb’s method
[
57
]
, which allows us to find a general solution for the bulk fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{V}
in terms of the pressure harmonics (as the inhomogeneous part), and two other solid spherical harmonics representing the homogeneous solutions, which we denote by
\Upsilon_{\ell}
and
\Xi_{\,\ell}
. Thus, the solution reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}(r,\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\hskip 0.5pt=\hskip 0.25pt-\infty\\
\ell\hskip 0.75pt\neq\hskip 0.5pt-1\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\!\!\frac{\left(\ell+3\right)r^{2}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}p_{\ell}-2\ell\,r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\,p_{\ell}}{2\eta\left(\ell+1\right)\!\left(2\ell+3\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\!\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\Upsilon_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\!\times\!\left(r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\,\Xi_{\,\ell}\right)\right]\!.

(227)

These three harmonic functions can be determined once the velocity field is prescribed on a spherical surface, say, at
r=R
. For membrane vesicles or droplets, however, the coupling between membrane deformations and surrounding viscous flows requires boundary conditions on a deformable, time-dependent surface
[
102
,
103
]
. A common approximation is to impose these boundary conditions on a sphere of an appropriately chosen mean radius. Here, we develop a perturbation scheme to determine the solid harmonics
p_{\ell}
,
\Upsilon_{\ell}
and
\Xi_{\,\ell}
in Eq. (
227
) when the boundary conditions are instead prescribed on the deformed spherical surface parameterized by Eq. (
217
).

Instead of directly working with the individual components of the velocity field
\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}\hskip-0.5pt
, the boundary-value problem is more suitably addressed by three scalar quantities: the radial velocity,
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}
, the radial component of the fluid vorticity,
(r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})\hskip-0.5pt\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{V}
, and the quantity that we refer to as the Lamb term,
\mathfrak{L}=(r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{V})-r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}
.

The radial component of the velocity can be readily calculated from Eq. (
227
); namely,

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}=\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\ell\,r\hskip 1.0ptp_{\ell}}{2\eta\left(2\ell+3\right)}+\frac{\ell\,\Upsilon_{\ell}}{r}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt,

(228)

where the following identity has been used
[
57
]
:

r\frac{\partial h_{\ell}}{\partial r}=\ell\hskip 1.0pth_{\ell},

(229)

with
h_{\ell}
being any solid spherical harmonic of order
\ell
. Similarly, the radial component of the curl of the velocity field
\boldsymbol{V}
can be determined as follows:

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{V}=\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\Xi_{\,\ell}}{r}.

(230)

To obtain an expression for the Lamb term
\mathfrak{L}
, we begin by differentiating Eq. (
228
) with respect to
r
, and then by using again the identity in Eq. (
229
), we find that

\frac{\partial\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip-0.5pt\cdot\!\boldsymbol{V}\right)}{\partial r}=\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)p_{\ell}}{2\eta\left(2\ell+3\right)}+\frac{\ell\left(\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0pt\Upsilon_{\ell}}{r^{2}}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(231)

Due to the incompressibility condition, the above equation also allows us to compute the Lamb term
\mathfrak{L}
in terms of the solid harmonic functions, that is,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}\,=\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)r\hskip 1.0ptp_{\ell}}{2\eta\left(2\ell+3\right)}+\frac{\ell\left(\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0pt\Upsilon_{\ell}}{r}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(232)

By evaluating Eqs. (
228
), (
230
), and (
232
) at
r=R
, and by employing the identity
\left[\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]_{r=R}=h_{\ell}\left({r}/{R}\hskip 0.5pt\right)^{-\ell}
, for any solid spherical harmonic of order
\ell
, we obtain

\left[\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}\right]_{r=R}\,=\!\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\hskip-0.5pt\left[\frac{\ell\,p_{\ell}\hskip 1.0ptR^{\ell+1}}{2\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt3\right)\hskip-0.5ptr^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}+\frac{\ell\,\Upsilon_{\ell}}{R^{1-\ell}\,r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt,

(233)

the radial part of the vorticity

\left[\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{V}\right]_{r=R}\,=\!\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-0.5ptR^{\ell-1}\,\Xi_{\,\ell}}{r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}},

(234)

and, lastly, the Lamb divergence term

\left[\mathfrak{L}\hskip 0.25pt\right]_{r=R}=\!\sum_{\ell=-\infty}^{\infty}\!\left[\frac{\ell\hskip-1.0pt\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-1.0ptp_{\ell}\hskip 1.0ptR^{\ell+1}}{2\eta\left(2\ell+3\right)\hskip-0.5ptr^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt\frac{\ell\hskip-0.5pt\left(\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0pt\Upsilon_{\ell}}{R^{1-\ell}\,r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(235)

We denote the prescribed velocity field on the spherical surface
r=R
by
\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right)
. The radial velocity and radial vorticity at this surface are then

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}\right]_{r=R}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right),

(236)

\displaystyle\left[(r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{V}\right]_{r=R}

\displaystyle=(R\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right).

(237)

Similarly, the Lamb term evaluated at
r=R
reduces to

\left[\mathfrak{L}\hskip 0.25pt\right]_{r=R}=-R\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right)\hskip-1.0pt.

(238)

The velocity
\!\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi)
is a vector field defined on a spherical surface of radius
R
. Hence, following Lamb’s approach, this vector field may be decomposed in a series of surface spherical harmonics; specifically,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right)=\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\,\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\hskip-1.0pt,

(239)

\displaystyle-r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right)=\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\,\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\hskip-1.0pt,

(240)

\displaystyle(r\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})\hskip-0.5pt\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathrm{m}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(R,\hskip 0.5pt\theta,\varphi\right)=\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\,\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\hskip-1.0pt,

(241)

where
\mathcal{X}_{\ell}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}
are the complete surface spherical-harmonic components of degree
\ell
, defined analogously to Eq. (
222
). Note that the summation runs only over the positive integers, as
\mathcal{X}_{0}\hskip-1.0pt=\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{Y}_{0}\hskip-1.0pt=\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{Z}_{0}\hskip-1.0pt=0
, identically. Accordingly, the remaining relations can be indexed by
\ell\geq 1

[
57
]
. By means of the orthogonality condition of the solid spherical harmonics, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\ell}=\;

\displaystyle p_{-(\ell+1)}\frac{\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\ell+1}}{2\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip-1.0pt1\right)\hskip-0.75ptR^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}-\Upsilon_{-(\ell+1)}\frac{\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\ell+1}}{R^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+2}}

\displaystyle+\,p_{\ell}\,\frac{\ell\hskip 1.0ptR^{\ell+1}}{2\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt3\right)\hskip-0.75ptr^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}\,+\;\Upsilon_{\ell}\,\frac{\ell\hskip 1.0ptR^{\ell-1}}{r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}},

(242)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}=\;

\displaystyle p_{-(\ell+1)}\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\ell+1}}{2\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\hskip-1.0pt-\hskip-1.0pt1\right)\hskip-0.5ptR^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}-\Upsilon_{-(\ell+1)}\frac{\left(\ell+1\right)\!\left(\ell+2\right)r^{\ell+1}}{R^{\hskip 0.75pt\ell+2}}

\displaystyle+\,p_{\ell}\,\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)R^{\ell+1}}{2\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt3\right)\hskip-0.75ptr^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}\,+\;\Upsilon_{\ell}\,\frac{\ell\left(\ell-1\right)R^{\ell-1}}{r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}},

(243)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\ell}=\;

\displaystyle\hskip 1.0pt\Xi_{-(\ell+1)}\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-0.75ptr^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}{R^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}+\hskip 1.0pt\Xi_{\,\ell}\,\frac{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-0.75ptR^{\ell}}{r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}},

(244)

where
\ell\geq 1
. The negative-index harmonics are obtained formally by replacing
\ell
with
-\ell-1
.

Since the fluid is required to be at rest at infinity, for the outer flow (
i.e.
\!\boldsymbol{V}\!=\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\boldsymbol{+}}
), we must exclude the strictly positive harmonics,

p_{\ell}\,=\,\Upsilon_{\ell}\,=\,\Xi_{\ell}\,=0,\quad\mathrm{for}\quad\ell\geq 1,

(245)

as the corresponding terms diverge as
r\to\infty
. Therefore, the resulting equations can be simultaneously solved for the three unknown harmonic functions:
p_{-(\ell+1)}
,
\Upsilon_{-(\ell+1)}
, and
\Xi_{-(\ell+1)}
. This gives

\displaystyle p_{-(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta\left(2\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\left[\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}+\left(\ell+2\right)\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\right]\hskip-1.0pt,

(246)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{-(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{R^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell+2}}{2\hskip-0.75pt\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\left[\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}+\ell\hskip 0.25pt\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\hskip-1.0pt,

(247)

\displaystyle\Xi_{-(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{R^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell+1}}{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-0.5ptr^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\,\mathcal{Z}_{\ell},

(248)

for
\ell\geq 1
, and we also set
p_{-1}\!=\!\Upsilon_{-1}\!=\Xi_{-1}\!=0
, as there are no explicit sources or sinks for the ambient fluid. This completely determines both the velocity and pressure fields outside the closed membrane.

Similarly, for the inner flow (
i.e.
\!\boldsymbol{V}\!=\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\boldsymbol{-}}
), bounded by the membrane, the negative harmonics must be set to zero, as these terms diverge at the origin (
r\to 0
):

p_{-(\ell+1)}=\Upsilon_{-(\ell+1)}=\,\Xi_{-(\ell+1)}\,=0,\;\;\mathrm{for}\;\;\ell\geq 0.

(249)

Thus, we find that

\displaystyle p_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta\left(2\ell+3\right)\hskip-0.75ptr^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{\ell\hskip 0.5ptR^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\left[\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}-\left(\ell-1\right)\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\hskip-1.0pt,

(250)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{2\hskip 0.5pt\ell\hskip 0.5ptR^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell-1}}\left[\left(\ell+1\right)\mathcal{X}_{\ell}-\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\hskip-1.0pt,

(251)

\displaystyle\Xi_{\hskip 1.0pt\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{\ell\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-0.5ptR^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}\,\mathcal{Z}_{\ell},

(252)

for integer modes
\ell\geq 1
. These relations determine the velocity and pressure fields of the inner fluid.

B.3
Flows around a slightly deformed sphere

Here, we assume that the velocity and pressure fields of the ambient fluid can be written as power series in the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon
, that is,

\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\,\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\,\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip 0.5ptp^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}},\vskip-1.0pt

(253)

respectively, where the subscripts “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{+}
” and “
\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{-}
” denote the regions outside and inside the closed membrane, respectively. Due to the linearity of the Stokes equations, substituting the above expansions into Eq. (
185
) shows that each perturbation field must also satisfy the Stokes equations,

\eta\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{2}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}p^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\cdot\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=0.

(254)

Similarly, by equating the terms corresponding to each order in
\varepsilon
, the boundary conditions in Eq. (
193
) yield

\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to 0}\left|\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{-}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}(\boldsymbol{r})\right|<\infty},&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to 0}p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{-}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}(\boldsymbol{r})=P_{-}^{(\mathtt{i})}},\\[5.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to\infty}\left|\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{+}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}(\boldsymbol{r})\right|=0},&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\lim_{|\boldsymbol{r}|\to\infty}p_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{+}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}(\boldsymbol{r})=P_{+}^{(\mathtt{i})}}.\end{cases}

(255)

Likewise, the boundary condition in Eq. (
192
) at
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{R}
can be expressed as follows:

\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}\hskip-2.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{R}\right)\!,

(256)

where we define the composite velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}
as the prescribed interfacial velocity that matches the surrounding fluid flow. In addition, the membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\boldsymbol{R}
, the slip velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}
, the scalar volume flux
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}
, and the surface normal
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
may each be expanded in the perturbation parameter; namely,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{i})}\!,\qquad\boldsymbol{v}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}\!,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{i})},\qquad\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{i})}.}

(257)

Hence, at each order in the perturbation parameter,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{i})}+\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}-\sum_{\mathtt{q}=0}^{\mathtt{i}}j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{q})}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{i}-\mathtt{q})}.}

(258)

By Taylor expanding the bulk-fluid perturbation fields in Eq. (
256
) about the fixed radius
r=R
, we find that

\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}\hskip-2.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{R}\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{\mathtt{j}=\hskip 0.65pt0}\frac{\left(\hskip 0.5ptr-R\hskip 0.5pt\right)^{\mathtt{j}}}{\mathtt{j}!}\left[\frac{\partial^{(\mathtt{j})}}{\partial r^{(\mathtt{j})}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{i})}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}.

(259)

where, on the membrane,
r-R=\varepsilon R\,u(\theta,\varphi)
. By substituting (
259
) into Eq. (
256
), we have that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{i})}=}\sum_{\mathtt{j}=0}^{\mathtt{i}}\frac{\left(R\,u\right)^{\mathtt{j}}}{\mathtt{j}!}\left[\frac{\partial^{(\mathtt{j})}}{\partial r^{(\mathtt{j})}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{i}-\mathtt{j})}\right]_{r=R},

(260)

which is derived by collecting and equating terms of the same order in
\varepsilon
. Satisfying the velocity boundary condition on the deformed membrane requires matching the perturbation fields on the fixed reference sphere
r=R
. As each bulk-fluid perturbation also satisfies the Stokes equations, Lamb’s method can be applied order by order. The zeroth- and first-order terms in Eq. (
260
) are

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{0})}}

\displaystyle=\left[\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\right]_{r=R},

(261)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{1})}}

\displaystyle=\left[\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\right]_{r=R}+\left(Ru\right)\left[\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{0})}}{\partial r}\right]_{r=R}.

(262)

Higher-order boundary conditions contain terms with progressively higher powers of
u(\theta,\varphi)
.

From Eq. (
95
), the surface divergence of the membrane velocity field is related to the active membrane-area flux
\mathbb{M}
, which may likewise be expanded as
\mathbb{M}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\,\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{i})}
, so that Eq. (
95
) becomes

\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{i})}=\left(\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}\right)^{(\mathtt{i})}\!.

(263)

Moreover, the perturbation expansion in Eq. (
253
) gives the following stress tensors:

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=-p^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{I}+\eta\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right]+\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right]^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathsf{T}}\right\}\hskip-0.75pt\hskip-1.0pt,

(264)

and the corresponding stress vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
acting across the surface of the membrane are

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=\sum^{\mathtt{i}}_{\mathtt{j}=\hskip 0.65pt0}\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\mathtt{j})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{i}-\mathtt{j})},

(265)

which follows from the series expansion in
\varepsilon
of the stress vector
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\big[\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip 1.5pt\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\big]
, together with the expansion of the surface normal introduced above. In particular, the zeroth- and first-order terms of Eq. (
265
) are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})},\quad\mathrm{and}

(266)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}+\,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}.

(267)

Similarly, the body force
\boldsymbol{f}
on the membrane can be expanded in a power series in
\varepsilon
, that is,
\boldsymbol{f}=\sum^{\infty}_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}\,\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip 0.25pt\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{i})}
. Hence, Taylor expanding the stress boundary condition in Eq. (
188
) about the fixed sphere gives

\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{i})}\hskip-1.0pt=\sum^{\mathtt{i}}_{\mathtt{j}=\hskip 0.65pt0}\,\frac{\left(R\,u\hskip 1.0pt\right)^{\mathtt{j}}}{\mathtt{j}!}\!\left[\frac{\partial^{(\mathtt{j})}}{\partial r^{(\mathtt{j})}}\,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{i}-\mathtt{j})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\hskip-0.5pt\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R},

(268)

where we define the stress-vector jump

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}^{(\mathtt{i})}=\,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\!-\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{-}}^{(\mathtt{i})}.

(269)

Eq. (
268
) is obtained analogously to Eq. (
259
), with the stress vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{i})}_{{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}(\boldsymbol{r}=\!\boldsymbol{R})
expanded about a fixed sphere of radius
R
. This gives the explicit forms of the zeroth- and first-order terms of Eq. (
268
):

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!

\displaystyle=\left[\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-0.5pt\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R},\quad\mathrm{and}

(270)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{1})}

\displaystyle=\left[\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-0.5pt\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}+\left(R\,u\right)\!\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\,\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}^{(\mathtt{0})}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}.

(271)

Starting from the leading-order perturbation field, the higher-order fields can then be solved successively by matching the corresponding boundary conditions.

B.3.1
Zeroth order perturbation

At leading order in
\varepsilon
, the position vector
\boldsymbol{R}
of the membrane is given by
\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt=R\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
. Thus, the membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\hskip 0.25ptt}\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}
may be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=\!\frac{\partial\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\!}{\partial t}\!+\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip 0.25pt,\alpha}\!=\!\,v^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!+\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\hskip-2.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!,

(272)

where the first equality follows from the material derivative, while the second gives the decomposition into normal and tangential components. Since
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt=\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
,
\boldsymbol{e}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\theta}\!=R\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}
, and
\boldsymbol{e}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\varphi}\!=R\sin\theta\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}
, we have
v^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
, and thus

\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\theta}\hskip 0.6pt\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}\,+\,\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\varphi}\hskip 0.6pt\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}},

(273)

where we additionally define

\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\theta}=R\left[v^{\theta}\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\!\!,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\varphi}=R\sin\theta\left[v^{\varphi}\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\!.

(274)

Thus, the zeroth-order membrane velocity is purely tangential to the fixed sphere of radius
R
.

In the homogeneous base state, the zeroth-order second active force moment
\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{0})}
is spatially uniform, so the corresponding slip velocity vanishes,
\boldsymbol{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0}
. The normal component of the zeroth-order interfacial velocity defined in Eq. (
256
) is therefore

{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=-j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}.}

(275)

Since the zeroth-order inner flow is incompressible, its net normal flux through the closed membrane must vanish. Moreover,
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}
is spatially uniform in the homogeneous base state, and hence

{0=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{d}S^{(\mathtt{0})}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{\!{\boldsymbol{-}}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=-4\pi R^{2}j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}.}

(276)

Thus, the zeroth-order volume flux vanishes,

j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}+w_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left[P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right]\!=0,

(277)

where
\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}
is the zeroth-order active contribution to the volume flux, while
\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
and
P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
are the zeroth-order osmotic pressure and hydrostatic pressure difference, respectively.

It follows that the zeroth-order interfacial velocity has no radial component. Using Lamb’s method as described in the previous section, we therefore have

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\mathfrak{m}}={\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}}\,\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=0,

(278)

and the orthogonality of the surface spherical harmonics implies
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=0
for every
\ell\geq 1
. The zeroth-order Lamb term evaluated on the reference sphere is

\mathfrak{L}^{(\mathtt{0})}=-R\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=-R\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptscriptstyle S}+2\hskip 0.5ptj_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}-R\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{0})}.

(279)

Here,
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{0})}
is the zeroth-order active membrane-area flux, which vanishes in the homogeneous steady state, as shown in Appendix B.4. Since
\boldsymbol{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
, it follows that

\mathfrak{L}^{(\mathtt{0})}={\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}}\,\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=0,

(280)

which implies that each of the zeroth-order surface harmonics
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=0
for all
\ell\geq 1
.

Consequently, together with
p_{-1}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\Upsilon_{-1}^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
, the corresponding pressure and potential harmonics for the inner and outer flows vanish,

\begin{cases}p^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=0,&\ell\geq 1,\\[4.0pt]
p^{(\mathtt{0})}_{-(\ell+1)}=\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{0})}_{-(\ell+1)}=0,&\ell\geq 0.\end{cases}

(281)

The first and second lines correspond to the inner and outer flows, respectively.

As the normal
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt=\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
, the zeroth-order traction vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
acting across the membrane are given by

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!=-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptp^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}+\eta\hskip 1.0ptr\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\!\hskip-1.0pt\left[\!\frac{\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}}{r}\hskip-1.0pt\right]\!+\frac{\eta}{r}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}\!\left[r\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(282)

Since the nonconstant pressure harmonics vanish, the corresponding pressure fields reduce to

\displaystyle p^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}

\displaystyle=P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}p_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{0})}=P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}},

(283)

\displaystyle p^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}

\displaystyle=P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}p_{-(\ell+1)}^{(\mathtt{0})}=P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}.

(284)

Here,
P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
are the zeroth-order constant pressures outside and inside of the organelle, respectively. These constants do not contribute to the velocity fields
\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}
, since their gradients vanish, cf. Eq. (
227
).

The remaining zeroth-order solid spherical harmonics
\Xi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}
and
\Xi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{-(\ell+1)}
describe the toroidal components of the inner and outer flows, respectively. Since the homogeneous membrane fields generate no tangential force at this order, cf. Eq. (
180
), taking the radial component of the curl of the stress-vector jump obtained from Eq. (
282
) gives

{\mathcal{Z}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}=0,\qquad\ell\geq 1.}

(285)

It then follows from Eqs. (
252
) and (
248
) that both
\Xi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\ell}
and
\Xi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{-(\ell+1)}
vanish identically.

As a result, the zeroth-order velocity fields
\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\hskip-1.0pt=\boldsymbol{0}
everywhere within the bulk fluid, including at
r=R
. The interfacial condition, together with
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then gives
\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0}
. By means of Eq. (
282
), we find that the zeroth-order stress vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptP^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}
, whereas the stress tensors are given by

\boxed{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}=-P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip 0.5pt{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\boldsymbol{I}.}

(286)

Hence, the zeroth-order stress boundary condition in Eq. (
268
) reduces to

\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{0})}=-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\left[P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\!-P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right]=-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 0.75ptP^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta},

(287)

where
P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}
denotes the zeroth-order hydrostatic pressure difference across the membrane. Thus, by decomposing the hydrodynamic force
\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{0})}
onto the local membrane basis, we have

\boxed{f^{(\mathtt{0})}=-P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}^{(\mathtt{0})}\left[f^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=\boldsymbol{0},}

(288)

where
f^{(\mathtt{0})}
and
\left[f^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}
are the zeroth-order normal and tangential components of the hydrodynamic force, respectively, and
\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{0})}
denotes the tangential hydrodynamic-force vector.

B.3.2
First order perturbation

At first order in the perturbation, the position vector of the membrane is given by
\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0pt=R\hskip 1.25ptu\!\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
, and the corresponding membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\!\frac{\partial\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\!}{\partial t}\!+\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip 0.25pt,\alpha}\!=\!\,v^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!+\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\hskip-2.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!,

(289)

which follows from
v^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
and
\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
. As before, we can rewrite the above expression as

\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{r}\hskip 0.6pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}+\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\theta}\hskip 0.6pt\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}\,+\,\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\varphi}\hskip 0.6pt\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}},

(290)

where we use the corresponding unit tangent vectors to define the tangential velocity components

\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\theta}=R\left[v^{\theta}\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{\!(\mathtt{1})}\!\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\varphi}=R\sin\theta\left[v^{\varphi}\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\!,

(291)

while the radial velocity of
\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}
is found to be

\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{r}=R\,\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=R\,\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial u_{\ell}}{\partial t}.

(292)

Since the zeroth-order fluid velocities
\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, the boundary condition in Eq. (
262
) reduces to

\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}+\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{1})}\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}=\left[\boldsymbol{V}_{{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\right]_{r=R}.

(293)

which allows us to apply Lamb’s method to obtain both the inner and outer fluid flows. By employing Eq. (
239
), the degree-
\ell
surface harmonics for
\ell\geq 1
are

\boxed{\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=R\,\frac{\partial u_{\ell}}{\partial t}-\mathbb{V}_{\hskip-1.0pt\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})},\qquad\ell\geq 1,}

(294)

where
u_{\ell}
and
\mathbb{V}_{\hskip-1.0pt\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}
are the degree-
\ell
components of the shape field and the first-order active volume flux, respectively. For
\ell\geq 1
, the passive osmotic contribution to
j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{1})}
vanishes, so that
j_{{\scriptscriptstyle V},\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\mathbb{V}_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}
.

From Eq. (
263
), we have
\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}
, and since
R\,\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\mathfrak{m}}=r\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, Eq. (
240
) gives

\boxed{\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=-R\,\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}+2\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-1.0pt\ell}+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2\eta R}\,\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}.}

(295)

where
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
is the degree-
\ell
component of the first-order active membrane-area flux. The remaining terms follow from

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\text{div}\,\!\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}+\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}\right)+2H^{(\mathtt{0})}j_{\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{1})},}

(296)

with
H^{(\mathtt{0})}=-1/R
and
j_{{\scriptscriptstyle V},\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
for
\ell\geq 1
. The first-order slip velocity is

\boxed{\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}=\frac{1}{2\eta}[\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}]^{(\mathtt{0})}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}.}

(297)

Hence,
\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}=\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}/(2\eta)
. Using

R^{2}\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}{Y}_{\ell,m}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)=-\ell(\ell+1){Y}_{\ell,m}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right),

(298)

gives the final term in Eq. (
295
), where
\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
is the degree-
\ell
component of the first-order contribution to the second active force moment
\mathbb{Q}
.

As both
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}
are zero, their associated equations reduce to

\boxed{R\hskip 1.0pt\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial t}=\frac{1}{2}R\hskip 1.5pt\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}\!=\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}\!+w_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left[P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right]\!,}

(299)

where
P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
and
\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
are the first-order hydrostatic pressure difference and osmotic pressure, respectively.

Therefore, the surface harmonics
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
allows us to obtain both the functions
p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
and
\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
which are associated with the interior fluid flow
\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{-}}}
, that is,

\displaystyle p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta\left(2\ell+3\right)\hskip-0.75ptr^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{\ell\hskip 0.5ptR^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}-\left(\ell-1\right)\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\hskip-2.0pt,

(300)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{2\hskip 0.5pt\ell\hskip 0.5ptR^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell-1}}\left[\left(\ell+1\right)\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}-\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\hskip-2.0pt,

(301)

with
\ell\geq 1
, and the functions
p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}
and
\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}
that correspond to the outer fluid flow
\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{+}}}
, namely

\displaystyle p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta\left(2\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}}{\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\left[\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}+\left(\ell+2\right)\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\right]\hskip-2.0pt,

(302)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{R^{\hskip 0.25pt\ell+2}}{2\hskip-0.75pt\left(\ell+1\right)r^{\hskip 0.5pt\ell+1}}\left[\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}+\ell\hskip 0.25pt\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\hskip-2.0pt,

(303)

where
\ell\geq 1
. On the other hand,
\hskip 1.0pt\Xi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}
and

\hskip 1.0pt\Xi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}
can be found as before from the stress boundary condition.

The traction vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!
at first order in the perturbation expansion may be expressed in the form

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!=\eta\hskip 1.0ptr\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\!\hskip-1.0pt\left[\!\frac{\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}}{r}\hskip-1.0pt\right]\!+\frac{\eta}{r}\,\boldsymbol{\nabla}\!\left[r\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\!\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!{\boldsymbol{\pm}}}\right]\!\hskip-1.5pt-\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0ptP^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptp^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!,

(304)

where the normal vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
is found to be

\qquad\quad\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=-\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}\,-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}\,=-r\boldsymbol{\nabla}u,

(305)

where the last equality follows from the definition of the three-dimensional gradient operator
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
written in spherical coordinates. As before, the pressure inside the closed membrane is given by

p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}=P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}+\sum\limits_{\ell=1}\,p_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!,

(306)

whilst the outside pressure is

\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;\,p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}=P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}+\sum\limits_{\ell=1}\,p_{-(\ell+1)}^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip 1.0pt,

(307)

where the scalars
P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!
are the spatially uniform (
\ell=0
