) contributions to the first-order pressure fields.

Since
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\times\boldsymbol{\nabla}u
, the radial components of the curl of the stress vectors
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{\pm}}\!
corresponding to the inner and outer flows are given by

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip-1.0pt\cdot\hskip-1.5pt\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-2.0pt\times\hskip-1.5pt\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}=\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\frac{\eta\hskip 1.0pt\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)\,\Xi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip 0.5pt\ell}}{R},

(308)

and

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip-1.0pt\cdot\hskip-2.0pt\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hskip-2.0pt\times\hskip-1.5pt\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}=-\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\frac{\eta\hskip 1.0pt\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)\,\Xi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}}{R},

(309)

respectively. Taking their difference and using Eqs. (
252
) and (
248
) gives

\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right]_{r=R}=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{\eta(2\ell+1)}{R}\,\mathcal{Z}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}.

(310)

At first order, the tangential membrane force is a surface gradient and therefore has no toroidal component; equivalently, its radial curl vanishes. Hence, by orthogonality,

\mathcal{Z}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=0,\qquad\ell\geq 1.

(311)

It then follows from Eqs. (
252
) and (
248
) that both
\Xi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip 0.25pt\ell}
and
\Xi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}
vanish identically.

By employing Eqs. (
287
) and (
304
), the normal component of the hydrodynamic force
\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{1})}
is given by

f^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\cdot\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{1})}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\cdot\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\hskip-1.0pt\left[\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R},

(312)

where the identity
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\,\cdot\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=0
and Eq. (
271
) are used to derive the last equation. To find
f^{(\mathtt{1})}\!
, we need to compute the radial part of the stress vectors. On the inner side of the closed membrane, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\hskip-1.0pt\left[\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}=-P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\!\!

\displaystyle\,+\,\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\frac{\left(\ell^{2}-\ell-3\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\ell}}{\left(2\ell+3\right)\hskip-1.0ptr^{\ell}}\hskip 1.0ptp^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 1.0pt\left.+\,\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\eta\hskip 1.0pt\ell\hskip-0.5pt\left(\ell-1\right)\hskip-0.5ptR^{\ell-2}}{r^{\ell}}\hskip 1.0pt\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt,

(313)

whereas, on the outer side of the membrane, we get

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\hskip-1.0pt\left[\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}=

\displaystyle-P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\,\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\frac{\left(\ell^{2}+3\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0ptr^{\ell+1}}{\left(2\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\ell+1}}\hskip 1.0ptp^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}\!\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\left.-\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(\ell+2\right)\!\left(\ell+1\right)\hskip-0.5ptr^{\ell+1}}{R^{\ell+2}}\hskip 1.0pt\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}\,\right]\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(314)

Hence, the normal hydrodynamic force
f^{(\mathtt{1})}
in Eq. (
312
) can be written in terms of the surface harmonics
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
, by substituting Eq. (
300
) and (
301
) into Eq. (
313
), and Eq. (
302
) and (
303
) into Eq. (
314
), which yields

\boxed{f^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=-P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}+\sum^{\infty}_{\ell=1}\,f^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell},\vskip-2.0pt}

(315)

where
P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}-P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}
is the first-order hydrostatic pressure difference across the membrane. The degree-
\ell
components
f^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
are found to be

\boxed{f^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\!=\frac{\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\!+\!1\right)\hskip-1.5pt\left\{\hskip-0.5pt3\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\!-\hskip-1.0pt\left[\hskip 0.5pt2\ell\hskip-0.5pt\left(\ell\!+\!1\right)\!-3\hskip 0.5pt\right]\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\right\}}{\ell\left(\ell\!+\!1\right)\hskip-1.0ptR}.}

(316)

The tangential contribution of the hydrodynamic force
\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{1})}
, at first order in
\varepsilon
, has the components

\left[f^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}=\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{1})}\cdot\left[\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}+\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{0})}\cdot\left[\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})},

(317)

where
\boldsymbol{f}^{(\mathtt{0})}=-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 0.75ptP^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
from Eq. (
287
). Using the orthogonality of the normal and tangent vectors at first order, the corresponding tangential body force is

\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}+\left[\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right)\right]_{r=R}.

(318)

The term within the square brackets is the tangential part of the first-order traction-vector jump. The corresponding tangential traction vectors for the inner and outer flows are

\displaystyle\!\!\left[\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\hskip-1.0pt\!-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right)\hskip-1.0pt\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}\!

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\!\left[\frac{\ell\left(\ell+2\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\ell}}{\left(\ell+1\right)\!\left(2\ell+3\right)r^{\ell}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 1.0ptp_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-65.0pt\left.+\frac{2\eta\left(\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\ell-2}}{r^{\ell}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\hskip 0.5pt\right]\!+P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 0.5ptu,

(319)

\displaystyle\hskip-108.0pt\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle\!\!\left[\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\hskip-1.0pt\!-\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\right)\hskip-1.0pt\right]_{\hskip-1.0ptr=R}\!

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\!\left[\frac{\left(\ell^{\hskip 0.5pt2}-1\right)\hskip-1.5ptr^{\ell+1}}{\ell\left(2\ell-1\right)\hskip-1.0ptR^{\ell+1}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 1.0ptp_{-(\ell+1)}^{(\mathtt{1})}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-75.0pt\left.-\frac{2\eta\left(\ell+2\right)\hskip-0.5ptr^{\ell+1}}{R^{\ell+2}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}\hskip 0.5pt\right]\!+P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{+}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 0.5ptu,

(320)

respectively, where
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
denotes the surface gradient on the unit sphere,

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)=\frac{\partial\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)}{\partial\theta}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\frac{\partial\hskip-1.0pt\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)}{\partial\varphi}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}.

(321)

The above expressions in Eq. (
319
) and (
320
) are derived by employing the identities:

\displaystyle\;\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}h_{\ell}=\frac{\ell\hskip 1.0pth_{\ell}\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}{r}+\frac{\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell}}{r},\quad\mathrm{and}

(322)

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell}\hskip 0.5pt\right]_{r=R}=\left[\frac{r}{R}\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{-\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\hskip 0.5pth_{\ell},

(323)

where
h_{\ell}
is a solid spherical harmonic of degree
\ell
. Hence, by substituting Eq. (
300
) and (
301
) into Eq. (
319
), and Eq. (
302
) and (
303
) into Eq. (
320
), we find

\boxed{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\hskip-1.0pt=-\hskip-0.65pt\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\hskip-2.0pt\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\!\hskip-1.0pt\left[\frac{\eta\hskip-1.0pt\left(2\ell\hskip-0.5pt+\hskip-0.5pt1\right)}{\ell\hskip-1.0pt\left(\ell\hskip-0.5pt+\hskip-0.5pt1\right)\!R}\!\hskip-0.5pt\left(\hskip-1.0pt\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\!\!\hskip-0.5pt+\hskip-0.5pt2\hskip 0.25pt\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\right)\!\right]\!,}

(324)

which shows that the first-order tangential body force is a surface gradient and has no toroidal component.

B.4
Dynamics of the excess areal fractions

Here, the steady-state areal fractions corresponding to Eq. (
97
) are denoted by
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
, and they must satisfy

\boxed{\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\sum_{k}\,\bar{\Phi}_{k}\,\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0.}

(325)

On the other hand, the steady-state areal fraction
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
that is associated with Eq. (
96
) is obtained via the areal-fraction constraint,
\textnormal{{i.e.\ }}1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}=\bar{\Phi}_{1}+\bar{\Phi}_{2}
. By using this constraint,
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
can be expressed as follows:

\boxed{\bar{\Phi}_{k}=\frac{\displaystyle{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk^{\prime}}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}}{\displaystyle{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk^{\prime}}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}-{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}}\left(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right)\!,\quad\mathrm{with}\,\;k\hskip-1.0pt\neq k^{\prime},}

(326)

where both the indices
k,\,k^{\prime}\!\in\{1,2\}
. In deriving this homogeneous steady-state, we assume that the shape is also fixed, corresponding to the undeformed sphere of radius
R
at zeroth order in the perturbation expansion.

The system is subsequently perturbed about this nonequilibrium steady-state by small variations in the membrane areal fractions; namely, we consider that

\Phi_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)=\bar{\Phi}_{0}-\varepsilon\,\Psi_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)\,+\;\mathcal{O}[\varepsilon^{2}],

(327)

where
\varepsilon
is the perturbation parameter and
\Psi_{0}
is the corresponding perturbation field of the background areal fraction. This linear perturbation also leads to a linear response in the other areal fractions,

\Phi_{k}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)=\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\varepsilon\,\Psi_{k}\hskip-1.5pt\left(\xi^{\alpha}\!,\hskip 0.5ptt\right)\,+\;\mathcal{O}[\varepsilon^{2}],

(328)

where
\Psi_{k}
is the excess areal fraction of active membrane components associated with trafficking cycle
k
. Due to the constraint in Eq. (
31
),
\Psi_{k}
must satisfy

\Psi_{1}+\Psi_{2}=\Psi_{0}.

(329)

Moreover, Eq. (
328
) implies the following perturbation expansion of the active membrane-area flux
\mathbb{M}
from Eq. (
92
):

\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{0})}+\varepsilon\,\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}\,+\;\mathcal{O}[\varepsilon^{2}],

(330)

which is truncated to linear order in
\varepsilon
. Hence, the first-order contribution is

\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k}\,\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}.\vskip-5.0pt

(331)

Thus, the dynamical equation for
\Psi_{k}
associated with the first-order perturbation of Eq. (
102
) is given by

\dot{\Psi}_{k}\!=\!2\hskip 1.0pt\Omega_{k}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\Delta H\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!-\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!+\hskip 1.0pt\gamma_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\!\!\left[\hskip-0.2pt\frac{\Psi_{0}}{\,\bar{\Phi}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}_{0}\!\!}+\frac{\Psi_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}\right]\!\!,

(332)

where
\left[\Delta H\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0pt=\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0pt+\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{1})}H^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt
is the first-order contribution to the Laplacian of the mean curvature
H
. From the shape parameterization in Eq. (
217
), we have that
H^{(\mathtt{0})}=-1/R
, and thus
\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{1})}H^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt=0
. Also, we find

H^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.5pt=\hskip-0.5pt\frac{u\!\left(\theta,\hskip-0.5pt\varphi\right)}{R}+\frac{{\partial_{\theta}}\!\left[\hskip 1.0pt\sin\theta\hskip 2.0pt\partial_{\theta}\hskip 1.0ptu\!\left(\theta,\hskip-0.5pt\varphi\right)\right]}{2R\sin\theta}+\frac{\partial^{2}_{\varphi}\hskip 1.0ptu\!\left(\theta,\hskip-0.5pt\varphi\right)}{2R\sin^{2}\hskip-0.75pt\theta},

(333)

where
\partial^{2}_{\alpha}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)=\partial_{\alpha}\!\left[\hskip 1.0pt\partial_{\alpha}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)\hskip 0.5pt\right]
is the second derivative with respect to the local surface coordinates. As the Laplacian operator
\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\!
in spherical coordinates is given by

\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\!\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)=\frac{{\partial_{\theta}}\!\left[\hskip 1.0pt\sin\theta\hskip 2.0pt\partial_{\theta}\hskip 0.5pt\!\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)\right]}{R^{2}\sin\theta}+\frac{\partial^{2}_{\varphi}\hskip 0.5pt\!\left(\boldsymbol{\cdot}\right)}{R^{2}\sin^{2}\hskip-0.5pt\theta},

(334)

we can express the term
\left[\Delta H\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0pt
as follows:

\left[\Delta H\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\frac{\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}u}{R}+\frac{R\hskip 1.0pt\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\!\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}u}{2}.

(335)

Since
\sum_{k}\Psi_{k}=\Psi_{0}
, the last term in Eq. (
332
) can be written in terms of the variables
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
,
i.e.

\frac{\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\Psi_{k}}{\;\;\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}}+\frac{\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\Psi_{0}}{\;\;\bar{\Phi}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}_{0}}=\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\Psi_{k}\!\left[\hskip-1.0pt\frac{1}{\,\bar{\Phi}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}_{0}\!\!}\!+\!\frac{1}{\,\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}\!}\hskip-1.0pt\right]+\frac{\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\Psi_{k^{\prime}}\!}{\,\bar{\Phi}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}_{0}\!\!\!},

with
k^{\prime}\hskip-1.0pt\neq k
. Also, the total derivative of
\Psi_{k}
reduces to

\dot{\Psi}_{k}=\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}+\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\Psi_{k\hskip 0.5pt,\alpha}+\left[v^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\bar{\Phi}_{k\hskip 0.5pt,\alpha}\!=\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t},

(336)

as the zeroth-order membrane velocity
\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0}
and the base-state areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
are spatially uniform. Thus, Eq. (
332
) can be expressed in a matrix form as follows:

\boxed{\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\Psi}}{\partial\hskip 0.25ptt}=-\mathsf{M}\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\Psi}+\mathsf{D}\hskip 1.0pt\Delta^{\!(\mathtt{0})}\boldsymbol{\Psi}+2\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\Omega}\left[\Delta H\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})},}

(337)

by defining the vectors
\boldsymbol{\Psi}=\left(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\hskip-1.5pt
,
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\left(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\hskip-1.5pt
, where the superscript
\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}
indicates a vector transpose. On the other hand,
\mathsf{M}
is a matrix which is defined by

\mathsf{M}=\!\begin{bmatrix}\,\bar{\Phi}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}&\bar{\Phi}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}\,\\[5.0pt]
\,\bar{\Phi}_{2}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}&\bar{\Phi}_{2}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}\,\end{bmatrix}\!\hskip-1.0pt,

(338)

whilst the diffusion matrix
\mathsf{D}
is given by

\mathsf{D}=\!\begin{bmatrix}\,\gamma_{1}\!\left(\hskip-0.25pt\left.1\middle/\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right.+\left.1\middle/\bar{\Phi}_{1}\right.\hskip-1.0pt\right)&\,\left.\gamma_{1}\hskip 0.25pt\middle/\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right.\\[5.0pt]
\,\left.\gamma_{2}\hskip 0.25pt\middle/\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right.&\,\gamma_{2}\!\left(\hskip-0.25pt\left.1\middle/\hskip 0.25pt\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right.+\left.1\middle/\bar{\Phi}_{2}\right.\hskip-1.0pt\right)\end{bmatrix}\!\hskip-1.0pt.

(339)

By employing the quasi–spherical parametrization in Eq. (
217
), and assuming that
\Psi_{k}(\theta,\varphi)
are well-behaved functions of the angular variables, we could then expand, as done previously for
u(\theta,\varphi)
, the excess areal fractions
\Psi_{k}
in a series of surface spherical harmonics, that is,

\Psi_{k}(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\,\Psi_{k,\ell}(\theta,\varphi).

(340)

Here, the surface harmonics can also be rewritten as

\Psi_{k,\ell}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\theta,\varphi\right)=\sum^{\ell}\limits_{m=-\ell}\!\Psi_{k,\ell,m}\,{Y}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\theta,\varphi\right)\!,\vskip-2.0pt

(341)

where
\Psi_{k,\ell,m}
is the amplitude associated with the individual spherical harmonic
{Y}_{\ell,m}
.

Hence, the governing equation in (
337
) can be re-expressed as follows:

\boxed{\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt\ell}}{\partial\hskip 0.25ptt}=-\mathsf{M}\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt\ell}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{R^{2}}\mathsf{D}\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt\ell}+\frac{\ell(\ell+2)(\ell^{2}\!-1)}{R^{3}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}\hskip 1.0ptu_{\ell},}

(342)

where we define the vectors
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt\ell}\hskip-1.0pt=\hskip-1.0pt\left(\Psi_{1,\ell},\Psi_{2,\ell}\hskip 1.0pt\right)^{\!\mathsf{T}}\hskip-1.5pt
, and use Eq. (
298
) to evaluate the Laplacian terms. In particular,

\left[\Delta H\hskip 0.5pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{\ell\left(\ell+2\right)\!(\ell^{2}-1)\hskip 1.0ptu_{\ell}}{2R^{3}},

(343)

which shows that the zeroth and first harmonics of the Laplacian of the mean curvature vanish identically.

B.5
Area and volume dynamics

The hydrostatic pressure difference
P_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
between the two sides of the membrane depends on the osmotic pressure, as well as the active volume exchange associated with the trafficking cycles, as prescribed by Eq. (
208
). At zeroth order in the perturbation expansion, this becomes

\int\limits_{\mathcal{M}\;\;}\!\!\mathrm{d}S^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip 1.0pt\bigg\{\hskip-1.0ptv^{(\mathtt{0})}\!+w_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!-P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right]\!-\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip-1.0pt\bigg\}\hskip-1.0pt=0.

(344)

Note that the area element
\mathrm{d}S
also depends on the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon
; namely, we have that

\mathrm{d}S^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=\left[\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0pt\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\mathrm{d}\varphi\hskip 1.5pt\mathrm{d}\theta\,=R^{2}\sin\theta\;\mathrm{d}\varphi\hskip 1.5pt\mathrm{d}\theta,

(345)

for the shape parameterization in Eq. (
217
). The zeroth-order active volume flux is given by

\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}.

(346)

Moreover, the osmotic pressure is found to be

\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!=\,k_{B}T\hskip 1.0pt\bigg[\hskip-0.5ptc_{\boldsymbol{+}}-\frac{\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}}{V^{(\mathtt{0})}}\bigg]=\,k_{B}T\left[c_{\boldsymbol{+}}-c^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right]\!,

(347)

where
\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
is the number of solutes inside the organelle, the volume
V^{(\mathtt{0})}=\frac{4\pi}{3}R^{3}
, and
c^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}={\mathfrak{n}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}}/{\,V^{(\mathtt{0})}}\hskip 1.0pt
.

As the normal velocity
v^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
, the integral equation in (
344
) gives us an expression for the zeroth-order hydrostatic pressure difference,

\boxed{P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!=\hskip 1.0pt\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-w_{0}^{-1}\,\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{0})},}

(348)

which in terms of Eq. (
346
) and (
347
) now reads

P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!=\,k_{B}T\left[c_{\boldsymbol{+}}-c^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\right]\hskip-1.0pt-w_{0}^{-1}\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}.

(349)

This is equivalent to the zeroth-order normal volume flux vanishing,
j_{\hskip-1.0pt\scriptscriptstyle V}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=0
, cf. Eq. (
277
). The active contribution in Eq. (
349
) may therefore be interpreted as an effective shift of the osmotic pressure difference.

If the characteristic time associated with passive leakage is much longer than those associated with the active volume-transfer rates
\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
(that is,
w_{0}\to 0
), then maintaining a finite zeroth-order pressure difference requires
\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
. In this limit, Eq. (
349
) reduces to the
van’t Hoff relation
, cf. Eq. (
197
), so that
P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
. Together with Eq. (
325
), this condition implies that the active rates
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
cannot be independent if a homogeneous steady state with
\bar{\Phi}_{k}\neq 0
is to exist, which gives the linear relationship
{\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}/{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}={\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}/{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}
as
w_{0}\to 0
.

The osmotic pressure at first order in
\varepsilon
can be determined by expanding the volume in Eq. (
206
), namely

\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=c^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\,k_{B}T\,\frac{V^{(\mathtt{1})}}{V^{(\mathtt{0})}}\hskip 1.0pt,

(350)

where the first-order perturbation of the enclosed volume can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle V^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\frac{1}{3}\iint\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\hskip 1.5pt\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle\,\left\{\left[\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0pt\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\cdot\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}+\left[\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0pt\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\cdot\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{1})}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad+\,\left[\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!\cdot\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}\right\}\hskip-1.0pt,

(351)

where the Jacobian term at first order in
\varepsilon
is given by

\left[\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0pt\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\hskip 1.0pt2\hskip 0.5ptR^{2}\hskip 1.0ptu(\theta,\varphi)\hskip 0.5pt\sin\theta.

(352)

Since the lowest perturbation fields of the position vector are given by
\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=R\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
and
\boldsymbol{R}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=R\,u(\theta,\varphi)\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
, and the normal vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{0})}
is the radial unit vector
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
, the excess volume
V^{(\mathtt{1})}\!
can be further simplified to

V^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=R^{3}\!\iint\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\hskip 1.5pt\mathrm{d}\theta\;u(\theta,\varphi)\hskip 1.0pt\sin\theta=4\pi R^{3}\hskip 1.0ptu_{0},

(353)

by also using that
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
is orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
, cf. Eq. (
305
). The latter equality in Eq. (
353
) follows by expanding
u(\theta,\varphi)
in terms of the spherical harmonics
u_{\ell}(\theta,\varphi)
and using their orthogonality. Since
Y_{0,0}=1/\sqrt{4\pi}
and
u_{0}=u_{0,0}Y_{0,0}
, only the zeroth harmonic contributes to the surface integral. Thus, the osmotic pressure at first order can be rewritten as

\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=3\hskip 1.0ptu_{0}\hskip 0.75ptc^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\boldsymbol{-}}\hskip 0.25ptk_{B}T.

(354)

The first-order active volume flux is obtained by substituting Eq. (
328
) into Eq. (
200
), which yields

\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k}\,\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}.

(355)

The last two equations give the first-order form of the integral condition in Eq. (
208
),

\int\limits_{\mathcal{M}\;\;}\!\!\mathrm{d}S^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip 1.0pt\bigg\{\hskip-1.0ptv^{(\mathtt{1})}\!+w_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!-P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\right]\!-\mathbb{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-1.0pt\bigg\}\hskip-1.0pt=0,\vskip-3.0pt

(356)

where the term pre-multiplying the area element
\mathrm{d}S^{(\mathtt{1})}
vanishes identically due to the result in Eq. (
348
). As the normal membrane velocity
v^{(\mathtt{1})}\hskip-2.0pt=\hat{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{r}\hskip-2.0pt=R\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(\theta,\varphi)
, from Eq. (
292
), we get

P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}+w_{0}^{-1}\!\left(R\hskip 1.25pt\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial t}-\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,0}\,\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right)\!.

(357)

This result follows from the orthogonality of the spherical harmonics, since only the degree-zero components of
u(\theta,\varphi)
and
\Psi_{k}(\theta,\varphi)
contribute to the surface integral. Eq. (
357
) agrees with Eq. (
299
), obtained independently from Lamb’s solution, which also gives

\boxed{\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial t}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,0}\,\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\,.}

(358)

This relation connects the uniform shape change to the degree-zero active membrane-area flux. Thus, we can rewrite the first-order hydrostatic pressure difference as

\boxed{P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\hskip-0.25pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!=\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-w_{0}^{-1}\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,0}\left(\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-\frac{1}{2}R\hskip 1.25pt\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right)\!.}

(359)

The dynamics of the excess areal fractions
\Psi_{k}
in terms of the surface spherical harmonics is given by Eq. (
342
). For
\ell=0
, this reduces to
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi_{k,0}=-\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}
, which gives

\frac{\partial\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}}{\partial t}=-\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{0})}\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}=0.

(360)

Thus, the degree-zero active membrane-area flux is constant in time and set by the initial perturbation, so that

\boxed{\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\left(t\right)=\tilde{\mathbb{M}}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\quad\!\mathrm{and}\quad\!u_{0}(t)=\tilde{u}_{0}+\frac{t}{2}\,\tilde{\mathbb{M}}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})},}

(361)

where
\tilde{u}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{M}}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!
are the initial values at
t=0
for their corresponding functions. Note that the second equation is simply obtained by integrating Eq. (
358
). Thus,
u_{0}
changes linearly with time if
\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\neq 0
. To avoid this secular change of the uniform mode, membrane-area addition and removal must balance globally, so that
\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!=0
.

Since the volume of the homogeneous steady-state is given by
V^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=\frac{4\pi}{3}R^{3}
, we find from Eq. (
353
) that

\boxed{\frac{\partial V^{(\mathtt{1})}\!}{\partial t}=\frac{3}{2}\,\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}V^{(\mathtt{0})}.}

(362)

Similarly, the total area at zeroth order is

\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{0})}=4\pi R^{2}
, and its first-order
contribution is given by

\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{1})}=2R^{2}\!\iint\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\hskip 1.5pt\mathrm{d}\theta\;u(\theta,\varphi)\hskip 1.0pt\sin\theta=8\pi R^{2}\hskip 1.0ptu_{0},

(363)

where the former equality follows from Eq. (
352
), and the latter is obtained by employing the orthogonality condition of the surface spherical harmonics. Hence, the rate of change of the total area at first order is

\boxed{\frac{\partial\hskip-0.5pt\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{1})}}{\partial t}=\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{0})}.}

(364)

Combining these results, the rate of change of
u_{0}
is related to variations in both the area and the volume:

\frac{\partial u_{0}}{\partial t}=\frac{1}{2\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{0})}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{1})}}{\partial t}=\frac{1}{3V^{(\mathtt{0})}}\frac{\partial V^{(\mathtt{1})}}{\partial t}.

(365)

If
\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}=0
, the first-order corrections to both the area and volume remain constant in time and are set by the initial perturbation
\tilde{u}_{0}
. When
\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}\neq 0
, both instead vary linearly with time at this order, increasing or decreasing according to the sign of
\mathbb{M}_{0}^{(\mathtt{1})}
.

B.6
Membrane shape equations

The surface tension
\Sigma(\xi^{\alpha})
acts as a Lagrange multiplier enforcing the local membrane-area balance in Eq. (
95
). Accordingly, we expand the surface tension order by order in the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon
,

\Sigma(\theta,\varphi)=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\hskip 0.5pt\Sigma^{(\mathtt{i})}(\theta,\varphi).

(366)

At each order,
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{i})}
is determined self-consistently from the tangential membrane traction balance together with the local membrane-area balance.

B.6.1
At zeroth order

Figure 9:
The reduced surface tension
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
as a function of the homogeneous background areal fraction
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
for several fixed values of the activity parameter
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
.

At zeroth order, the tangential force balance together with Eq. (
288
) gives

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla_{\!\alpha}}\Sigma^{(\mathtt{0})}=0.

(367)

Thus, the zeroth-order surface tension is spatially uniform. The normal force balance in Eq. (
182
) becomes

\displaystyle\!\!f^{(\mathtt{0})}\!

\displaystyle=\hskip-1.0pt\sum_{k}\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{R}\!\hskip-1.0pt\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt\frac{k_{B}T\!}{b_{0}}\!\left(\!E_{k}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt\ln\hskip-0.5pt\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\!\!\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}\!\!\!\!}\right)\hskip-2.5pt-\hskip-0.5pt\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\hskip 0.5ptC_{k}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.5pt3\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2R}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{R}\hskip-1.0pt\left[\Sigma^{(\mathtt{0})}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}(\mathcal{E}_{0}+\ln\bar{\Phi}_{0})-\frac{\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R}\right]\!\hskip-0.5pt.

(368)

Here, the zeroth-order normal fluid traction is given by
f^{(\mathtt{0})}=-P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
. Using Eq. (
348
), Eq. (
368
) gives

\displaystyle\!\hskip-1.0pt\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!\hskip-1.0pt

\displaystyle+\hskip-0.5pt\sum_{k}\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{R}\!\hskip-1.0pt\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt\frac{k_{B}T\!}{b_{0}}\!\left(\!E_{k}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.0pt\ln\hskip-0.5pt\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\!\!\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}}\!\!\!\!}\right)\hskip-2.5pt-\hskip-0.5pt\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\hskip 0.5ptC_{k}\hskip-1.0pt+\hskip-1.5pt3\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2R}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-15.0pt+\frac{2}{R}\hskip-1.0pt\left(\bar{\Sigma}^{(\mathtt{0})}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\ln\bar{\Phi}_{0}-\frac{\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R}\right)\!=\sum_{k}\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{w_{0}},

(369)

where we define
\bar{\Sigma}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!=\Sigma^{(\mathtt{0})}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and
\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
is the osmotic pressure, whose expression is found in Eq. (
347
).

Figure 10:
Diagram showing the regions in
(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}})
parameter space where Eq. (
370
) admits two, one, or no real solutions for
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
. The boundary
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is given by
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=\ln\!\left(1+e^{-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}}\right)
, while
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is defined by
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
. In region
A
, two distinct real solutions exist, as illustrated by the inset at
(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}})=(1,-0.8)
, while regions
B
and
C
each contain a single solution, with the inset at
(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}})=(-0.5,1)
illustrating the case in region
C
. The insets show the left-hand side (in blue) and the right-hand side (in orange) of Eq. (
370
) as functions of
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
, with the red intersections marking the corresponding solutions.

The homogeneous steady-state areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
are given by Eq. (
326
), and thus
\bar{\Phi}_{k}\hskip-1.0pt\propto\!\left(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right)\hskip-1.0pt
. Hence, Eq. (
369
) can be written as the relation

\boxed{\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}+\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\hskip 1.0pt(1\!-\hskip-1.0pt\bar{\Phi}_{0})=-\bar{\Phi}_{0}\ln\bar{\Phi}_{0}-(1\!-\hskip-1.0pt\bar{\Phi}_{0})\ln(1\!-\hskip-1.0pt\bar{\Phi}_{0})}

(370)

where the parameters
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
are given by

\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=\frac{b_{0}}{k_{B}T}\left(\bar{\Sigma}^{(\mathtt{0})}+\frac{R}{2}\hskip 0.5pt\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\frac{\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R}\right)\!,

(371)

and
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}=\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}_{1}+\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}_{2}
, with

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk^{\prime}}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|}{|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|+|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk^{\prime}}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|}\left[\,E_{k}+\ln\hskip-1.0pt\left(\frac{|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk^{\prime}}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|}{|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|+|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk^{\prime}}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\;+\hskip-1.0pt\frac{b_{0}}{k_{B}T}\!\left(\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\hskip-1.0pt-\frac{R\hskip 1.0pt\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\hskip-1.0pt}{2w_{0}}-\frac{2\hskip 0.5pt\kappa\hskip 0.25pt\hskip 0.5pt{C}_{k}+3\hskip 0.5pt\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2R}\right)\!\hskip-0.5pt\Bigg]\!,

(372)

where
k^{\prime}\neq k
. The right-hand side of Eq. (
370
) is positive for
\bar{\Phi}_{0}\in(0,1)
. At thermodynamic equilibrium, the active-component areal fractions vanish,
\bar{\Phi}_{k}=0
, and hence
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=1
. In this limit, Eq. (
370
) gives
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=0
, recovering the
Young–Laplace
relation:

\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!=2H^{(\mathtt{0})}\bar{\Sigma}^{(\mathtt{0})}\!+2\kappa\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{C}_{0}K^{(\mathtt{0})},

(373)

At equilibrium, the hydrostatic pressure difference equals the osmotic pressure,
P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}\!=\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
, where
H^{(\mathtt{0})}
and
K^{(\mathtt{0})}
are the zeroth-order mean and Gaussian curvatures, respectively. Also,
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}=\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}_{2}=0
at equilibrium, since the cycle rates
\mathbb{R}^{\hskip 0.0ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
vanish.

We first consider the simpler case in which
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
is prescribed. Eq. (
370
) then directly determines the corresponding reduced surface tension
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
. The dependence of the reduced surface tension
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
on
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
is shown in Fig.
9
, with
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}\to 0
as
\bar{\Phi}_{0}\to 1
in the equilibrium limit.

We next consider the case in which the zeroth-order surface tension is fixed and
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
is allowed to adjust. Eq. (
370
) must then be solved for
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
and may admit more than one solution depending on
(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}})
. In Fig.
10
, we show the corresponding solution regions in
\left(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\right)
. Since the right-hand side is strictly concave and the left-hand side is linear, the upper boundary
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is defined by their tangency at
\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{\star}=1/(1+e^{-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}})
. Substitution into Eq. (
370
) gives

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\left\{\left(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\right)\,\middle|\,\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=\ln\left(1+e^{-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}}\right)\right\}
. This gives the largest value of
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
for which a solution exists:

\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}\leq\ln\hskip-1.0pt\left(1+e^{-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}}\hskip 0.75pt\right),

(374)

for any real
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
. The lower bound is determined by the endpoint values

\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
at
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=0

and
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}=0
at
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=1
:

\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}\geq\min(0,-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}).

(375)

Two distinct solutions exist in region
A
when
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
lies above both endpoint values but below the tangency value:

A=\left\{\left(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\right)\,\middle|\,\max\left(0,-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\right)<\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}<\ln\!\left(1\!+\!e^{-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}}\right)\right\}.

(376)

On
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, the two roots merge at
\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{\star}
. Regions
B
and
C
each contain a single solution,

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\right)\,\middle|\,0<\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}<-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}},\;\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}<0\right\},

(377)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\right)\,\middle|\,-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}<\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}<0,\;\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}>0\right\}.

(378)

The two branches are shown in Fig.
11
. The branch extending through
A\cup B
follows the larger root in
A
and approaches
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=1
at equilibrium, whereas the branch extending through
A\cup C
follows the smaller root and does not. Thus, the former is the physical homogeneous branch, which exists for

\boxed{0\leq\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}\leq\ln\hskip-1.0pt\left(1+e^{-\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}}\hskip 0.75pt\right)\!,}

(379)

for any real value of the activity parameter
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
. Within this domain, Eq. (
370
) determines a unique physical value of
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
, which in turn determines the homogeneous areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
via Eq. (
326
).

B.6.2
At first order

Linearizing the tangential membrane force balance in Eq. (
180
) about the homogeneous spherical base state gives

\left[f^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}=-\nabla^{\alpha}\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}-\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\nabla^{\alpha}\!\left(2H^{(\mathtt{1})}\right)-\sum_{k}\left(\nabla^{\alpha}\Psi_{k}\right)\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R}\right].

(380)

Since
\left[f^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{0})}=0
, the first-order tangential contribution to the hydrodynamic force is

\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}^{(\mathtt{0})}\left[f^{\alpha}\right]^{(\mathtt{1})}
. Hence,

\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\left\{-\frac{\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}}{R}+\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\frac{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}}{R^{2}}-\sum_{k}\frac{\Psi_{k,\ell}}{R}\!\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R}\right]\!\right\}

(381)

where
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}
and
H^{(\mathtt{1})}
are expanded in the basis of surface spherical harmonics. The result in Eq. (
381
) shows that the body force
\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
can be expressed as a surface gradient of a scalar field defined on the membrane. Likewise,
\boldsymbol{f}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
can be written in terms of the ambient fluid stresses acting across the membrane surface, as derived in Eq. (
324
) from Lamb’s solution. Since this expression is also a surface gradient, the orthogonality of spherical harmonics allows the corresponding surface modes to be equated. Thus, for each
\ell\geq 1
, the tangential balance gives

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\frac{\eta(2\ell+1)}{\ell(\ell+1)}\left[\frac{\partial u_{\ell}}{\partial t}-\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\left(2\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-\frac{3\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{\eta R^{2}}\right)\right]-\frac{\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}}{R}

\displaystyle+u_{\ell}\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\frac{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R^{2}}-\sum_{k}\frac{\Psi_{k,\ell}}{R}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R}\right]=0,

(382)

where we have used the explicit forms
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=R\frac{\partial u_{\ell}}{\partial t}-\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\left[2\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-R\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2\eta R}\right]
.

Figure 11:

(a) Density plot of the background areal fraction
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
as a function of the parameters
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
, showing the physical branch of Eq. (
370
) on
A\cup B
, with the regions
A
,
B
, and
C
defined in Fig.
10
. In region
A
, this branch follows the larger, right-most of the two solutions. The black points mark representative parameter choices:
(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}})=(1,-1.5)
corresponds to a single solution, while
(0.6,-0.3)
and
(0.1,1)
each correspond to two solutions, with the corresponding roots illustrated in the insets as in Fig.
10
.
(b) Density plot of
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
in terms of
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}
, showing the second branch on
A\cup C
. In region
A
, this branch follows the smaller, left-most of the two solutions. The black points at
(\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0},\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}})=(0.6,-0.3)
and
(0.1,1)
correspond to the same representative parameter choices as in panel (a), with the corresponding roots illustrated in the insets. This root is unphysical as it does not respect the requirement that
\bar{\Phi}_{0}\to 1
as we approach thermodynamic equilibrium,
i.e.
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\textstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptstyle\varsigma$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle\varsigma$}}}_{0}\to 0
and
\mathchoice{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\displaystyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\textstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptstyle\wp$}}}{\mathord{\scalebox{1.20}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wp$}}}\to 0
.

The equation of motion along the normal direction at first order in
\varepsilon
follows from Eq. (
182
). Accordingly, the normal body force on the membrane,
f^{(\mathtt{1})}
, can be computed as

\displaystyle f^{(\mathtt{1})}=\frac{2\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}}{R}-2H^{(\mathtt{1})}\left\{\left(\bar{\Sigma}^{(\mathtt{0})}-\frac{2\kappa\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\ln\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right)+\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k}\frac{2\Psi_{k}}{R}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2R}\right]+\sum_{k}\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}\Psi_{k}\left(\kappa C_{k}-\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2}\right)+2\kappa\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}.

(383)

Here, we use that
H^{2}-K=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, which gives the Gaussian curvature at first order as
K^{(\mathtt{1})}=2H^{(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}
. Moreover, since
H^{(\mathtt{0})}
is spatially uniform, the first-order expansion of the curvature-Laplacian term is
[\Delta H]^{(\mathtt{1})}=\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}
. The term in the curly brackets can be simplified using Eq. (
368
), giving

\displaystyle f^{(\mathtt{1})}=2H^{(\mathtt{1})}\left[\frac{R}{2}P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}+\frac{\kappa\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R}+\sum_{k}\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}(2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})}{2R}\right]+\sum_{k}\frac{2\Psi_{k}}{R}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2R}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k}\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}\Psi_{k}\left(\kappa C_{k}-\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2}\right)+2\kappa\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}+\frac{2\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}}{R},

(384)

where
P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}
is the hydrostatic pressure difference at zeroth order in
\varepsilon
. Expanding in the basis of spherical harmonics, with
H^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=-(\ell-1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}/(2R)
and
\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}Y_{\ell}=-\ell(\ell+1)Y_{\ell}/R^{2}
, gives
\Delta^{(\mathtt{0})}H^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}=\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}/(2R^{3})
. Thus,

\displaystyle f^{(\mathtt{1})}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Bigg\{\frac{2\Sigma_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}}{R}+\frac{\kappa\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}}{R^{3}}-(\ell-1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}\left[\frac{P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}}{2}+\frac{\kappa\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R^{2}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}(2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})}{2R^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k}\frac{2\Psi_{k,\ell}}{R}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2R}\right]-\sum_{k}\frac{\ell(\ell+1)\Psi_{k,\ell}(2\kappa C_{k}-\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})}{2R^{2}}\Bigg\}.

(385)

To avoid secular evolution of the isotropic mode, cf. Eqs. (
362
) and (
364
), we require that the
\ell=0
contribution to the active membrane-area flux vanish:
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,0}\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
. Hence, Eq. (
342
) for
\ell=0
becomes
\dot{\Psi}_{1,0}=\dot{\Psi}_{2,0}=0
. This implies that the excess areal fractions
\Psi_{k,0}
are constants independent of time, set by the initial perturbation. Since we perturb about the homogeneous steady-state areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
, we choose
\Psi_{1,0}=\Psi_{2,0}=0
, so that the perturbations contain no uniform shift in the areal fractions. Likewise, we choose
u_{0}=0
, cf. Eq. (
299
). Using Eq. (
315
) and Eq. (
359
), we find that the zeroth mode of Eq. (
385
) reduces to
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}=0
, as
P^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=\Pi^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=0
. Hence, for this choice of perturbations, the sum in Eq. (
385
) may start from the first spherical-harmonic mode (
\ell=1
). More generally, if the
\ell=0
perturbations are retained,
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}
is linearly related to the initial value of
u_{0}
and one independent compositional zero-mode amplitude, since
\Psi_{1,0}
and
\Psi_{2,0}
are constrained by
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{0}=0
.

Equating the degree-
\ell
components of Eq. (
385
) with the fluid-side normal traction in Eq. (
316
), we obtain for each mode
\ell\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{\eta(2\ell+1)}{\ell(\ell+1)}\left\{[3-2\ell(\ell+1)]\frac{\partial u_{\ell}}{\partial t}+\frac{3+2\ell(\ell+1)}{R}\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-3\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\left(\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{2\eta R^{2}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle-\frac{2\Sigma_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}}{R}+(\ell-1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}\left[\frac{P^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}}{2}+\frac{\kappa\mathcal{C}_{0}}{R^{2}}+\sum_{k}\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}(2\kappa C_{k}+3\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})}{2R^{2}}\right]-\frac{\kappa\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}}{R^{3}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{k}\frac{2\Psi_{k,\ell}}{R}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)-\frac{2[\ell(\ell+1)+2]\kappa C_{k}-(\ell-2)(\ell+3)\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{4R}\right]=0.

(386)

Solving the tangential balance in Eq. (
382
) for
\Sigma_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}
and substituting the result into the normal balance gives the evolution equation for the degree-
\ell
shape component
u_{\ell}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\ell}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\left[\frac{R\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+[2\ell(\ell+1)-3]\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2\eta R}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{R[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}\right]-\sum_{k}\frac{\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)(\ell+2)(\Psi_{k,\ell}+u_{\ell}\bar{\Phi}_{k})(\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-2\kappa C_{k})}{2\eta R^{2}(2\ell+1)[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\kappa u_{\ell}\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)(\ell+2)[R\mathcal{C}_{0}-\ell(\ell+1)]}{\eta R^{3}(2\ell+1)[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}+\frac{\ell(\ell-1)(\ell+1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}}{2\eta(2\ell+1)[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}\left[\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\frac{1}{w_{0}}\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right].

(387)

Substituting the resulting shape dynamics back into the tangential balance gives the corresponding degree-
\ell
component of the surface tension,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\!\left[(8\ell^{3}+15\ell^{2}+\ell-3)\Psi_{k,\ell}+(\ell-1)(\ell+2)(2\ell-1)(2\ell+3)u_{\ell}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\right]}{2R[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}-\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\!\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)\!\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k}\frac{\kappa C_{k}\left\{[5\ell(\ell+1)-4]\Psi_{k,\ell}+(\ell-1)(\ell+2)u_{\ell}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\right\}}{R[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}-\sum_{k}\frac{\eta(2\ell-1)(2\ell+1)(2\ell+3)\Psi_{k,\ell}(R\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-2\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})}{\ell(\ell+1)[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\kappa u_{\ell}(\ell-1)(\ell+2)[R\mathcal{C}_{0}-\ell(\ell+1)]}{R^{2}[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}+\frac{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)Ru_{\ell}}{2[2\ell(\ell+1)-1]}\left[\Pi^{(\mathtt{0})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}-\frac{1}{w_{0}}\sum_{k}\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right].

(388)

The surface-tension component
\Sigma_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}
obtained above is the degree-
\ell
contribution to
\Sigma
, which acts as a Lagrange multiplier enforcing local membrane-area balance. To connect this result with the activity-renormalized surface tension
\Sigma_{A}
defined in Eq. (
183
), we expand
\Sigma_{A}
to first order, giving

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{A,\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\Sigma_{\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}+\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,\ell}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\frac{k_{B}T}{b_{0}}\left(E_{k}+\ln\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)\right].}

(389)

Substituting Eq. (
388
) shows that the explicit first-moment and thermodynamic contributions cancel from
\Sigma_{A,\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}
through the self-consistent adjustment of the surface-tension Lagrange multiplier. Physically, the first active force moment contributes directly to the homogeneous activity-renormalized surface tension in Eq. (
183
), whereas its spatially varying first-order contribution, proportional to
\Psi_{k,\ell}\,\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, is compensated by the local adjustment of
\Sigma
required by membrane-area balance. Thus,
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
remains part of the homogeneous state but does not contribute independently to the linear nonuniform response, which also explains its absence from the linear shape equation.

The
\ell=1
mode is of particular interest because it corresponds to translation of the compartment and is associated with the drift instability discussed below. For this mode, Eqs. (
388
) and (
389
) reduce to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Sigma_{A,1}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,1}\!\left[\frac{7\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+4\kappa C_{k}}{2R}-\frac{5\eta}{2}\!\left(R\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}-2\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\right)\right]\!.}

(390)

For the two-point force model, an extensile force dipole during fission and a contractile one during fusion give
\mathrm{sgn}(\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})=(-1)^{k+1}
, while
\mathrm{sgn}(\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}})=(-1)^{k}
, so
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
have opposite signs for each trafficking cycle. Using Eqs. (
389
) and (
388
), their composition-dependent contributions are seen to enter with opposite prefactors for every
\ell\geq 1
and therefore reinforce one another. For
\Psi_{k,\ell}>0
, both contributions raise the activity-renormalized surface tension for fission and lower it for fusion. This change has the same direction as the direct first-moment contribution discussed in Appendix A following Eq. (
183
), but its origin is different: in the linear nonuniform response it arises from the second active force moment and membrane-area transfer rather than from an explicit first-moment contribution.

B.7
Linear stability analysis

The dynamics of the excess areal fractions
\Psi_{k,\ell}
is given by Eq. (
342
), and the shape dynamics by Eq. (
387
). Together, these equations form a closed linear system whose stability about the homogeneous steady state can be analyzed for each spherical-harmonic degree
\ell\geq 1
by writing it in matrix form:

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big[\begin{matrix}u_{\ell}&\Psi_{1,\ell}&\Psi_{2,\ell}\end{matrix}\,\big]^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathsf{S}_{\ell}\big[\begin{matrix}u_{\ell}&\Psi_{1,\ell}&\Psi_{2,\ell}\end{matrix}\,\big]^{\mathsf{T}}\!,

(391)

where
\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}
denotes a transpose, and
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}
is the corresponding stability matrix.
For
\ell=1
, Eq. (
387
) reduces to

\frac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial t}=\sum_{k}\Psi_{k,1}\!\left[\frac{R\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}+\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{3R}-\frac{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}{3\eta R^{2}}\right],

(392)

whereas the membrane compositional equation, written in the vector form

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\ell}=\left(\Psi_{1,\ell},\Psi_{2,\ell}\right)^{\!\mathsf{T}}\hskip-1.5pt
,
reduces to

\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt1}}{\partial\hskip 0.25ptt}=-\mathsf{M}\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt1}-\frac{2}{R^{2}}\,\mathsf{D}\hskip 0.5pt\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\hskip-0.5pt1}.

(393)

A

Dimensionless fission rate

0.1
–
500

\zeta

Ratio of fusion and fission rates

0.25
–
4

\bar{\Phi}_{0}

Background areal fraction

3/5

\gamma

Surface diffusion coefficient

0.002
–
0.1~\mu\mathrm{m}^{2}/\mathrm{s}

\mathcal{K}

Dimensionless bending rigidity

0.2
–
200

|Q_{k}|

Dimensionless second moment

5
–
35000

|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|

Membrane-area transfer rate

0.01
–
1.2~\mathrm{s}^{-1}

|\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|

Active force first moment

0.01
–
0.2~\mathrm{mN}/\mathrm{m}

|\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|

Active force second moment

0.5
–
7~\mathrm{pN}

Table 2:

Estimated ranges of physical and dimensionless parameters. To estimate
\gamma
, we associate the effective surface diffusivity
D
with the slower compositional diffusion mode, corresponding to the smaller eigenvalue of the diffusion matrix
\mathsf{D}
in Eq. (
339
). For
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=\gamma
and
\zeta=1
, the slower eigenvalue is
D=2\gamma/(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})
. The membrane-area transfer rate is estimated as
|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|\sim\nu_{k}r^{2}/(R^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{k})
, where
\nu_{k}
is the frequency of vesicle events associated with a cisterna. Taking
\nu_{k}\sim 1/T_{a}
and
\bar{\Phi}_{k}\sim 1/5
gives the estimated range of
|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|
, from which
A=R^{2}|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|/\gamma
follows. We take
\zeta
to be of order unity,
allowing both weaker and stronger fusion relative to fission. The estimates of
\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
are obtained
by considering a characteristic mechanical energy scale
E_{a}\sim 5
–
50\,k_{B}T
acting over a normal distance of order the vesicle radius
r\approx 30
–
40
nm, giving
|\mathbb{P}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.35pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|\sim E_{a}/r^{2}
and
|\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|\sim E_{a}/r
. The dimensionless second moment and bending rigidity follow from
|Q_{k}|=|\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|/(\gamma\eta)
and

\mathcal{K}=\kappa/(R\gamma\eta)
, respectively. The energy scale
E_{a}
represents the combined energetic cost of elastic membrane deformation and hydrodynamic dissipation associated with displacement of the ambient fluid.

We denote the eigenvalues of
\mathsf{S}_{\ell}
by
\lambda_{\ell}
, which satisfy
\det(-\lambda_{\ell}\hskip 0.5pt\mathsf{I}+\mathsf{S}_{\ell})=0
, where
\mathsf{I}
is the identity matrix. For
\ell=1
, one eigenvalue is identically zero, corresponding to the translational mode associated with the first spherical harmonics. The remaining two eigenvalues are determined by the compositional dynamics and are independent of the first-row elements of
\mathsf{S}_{1}
.

Although we could obtain the eigenvalues in exact form, it is more instructive to work with a reduced set of dimensionless parameters. We choose the surface diffusivities
\gamma_{k}
to be the same for both the fissogens and fusogens, namely
\gamma_{k}=\gamma
. We also nondimensionalize the membrane-area transfer rates
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
using the characteristic diffusion time
R^{2}/\gamma
:

{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}=-\frac{\gamma A}{R^{2}},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}=\zeta\,\frac{\gamma A}{R^{2}},

(394)

where
A
is a dimensionless fission rate, while
\zeta
is the ratio of the magnitude of the fusion and fission membrane-area transfer rates; namely,
\zeta=-{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}/{\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}
. Estimates of
A
and
\zeta
are shown in Table II.

To estimate the membrane-area transfer rate
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
, we consider a cisterna with an effective area
4\pi R^{2}
and nearly uniform composition. From Eq. (
92
), the corresponding area-transfer rate for cycle
k
is
4\pi R^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{k}|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|
. Since each fission or fusion event transfers a membrane area
4\pi r^{2}
, events occurring at a frequency
\nu_{k}
produce an area-transfer rate
4\pi r^{2}\nu_{k}
. Equating these rates gives
|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|\sim\nu_{k}r^{2}/(R^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{k})
. For the fission cycle, we take
\nu_{1}\sim 1/T_{a}
, assuming that the local event frequency is comparable to the Golgi-wide estimate in Table I, although turnover may be faster or slower at individual cisternae. Combining this estimate with the definition of
A
in Eq. (
394
), we obtain
A\sim r^{2}/(\gamma\bar{\Phi}_{1}T_{a})
.

The homogeneous areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
from Eq. (
326
) can be rewritten as

\bar{\Phi}_{1}=\zeta\,\frac{1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}}{1+\zeta},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\bar{\Phi}_{2}=\frac{1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}}{1+\zeta},

(395)

where
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
is the homogeneous background areal fraction. This shows that the areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
are independent of
A
. For simplicity, we also neglect the curvature couplings and volume-transfer rates by setting
C_{k}=0
and
\mathbb{V}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip-0.85pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}=0
. We therefore focus on membrane-area exchange and active force transmission. Among the active force moments, only the second moments
\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}
appear explicitly in the shape equation in Eq. (
387
), so we define

{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}=\gamma\eta\,Q_{1},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad{\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}}=\gamma\eta\,Q_{2},

(396)

rescaling them by the characteristic force
\gamma\eta
, with
Q_{k}
dimensionless. The signs of the second active force moments are not fixed in general, so
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
may take either sign independently. We therefore consider two representative cases. First, we take
Q_{2}=-Q_{1}=Q
, corresponding to second moments of equal magnitude and opposite sign. For the illustrative two-point force model in Appendix A,
Q<0
corresponds to an extensile force dipole for fission and a contractile one for fusion, whereas
Q>0
reverses these force dipoles. Second, we take
Q_{1}=Q_{2}=Q
, corresponding to second moments of equal magnitude and the same sign. Estimates of the magnitude of
Q
are shown in Table II.
