Using this rescaling of parameters, the nonzero eigenvalues of
\mathsf{S}_{1}
are found to be (with growth rates measured in units of
\gamma/R^{2}
):

\lambda^{\pm}_{1}=\frac{-2\zeta-(1+\zeta^{2})\bar{\Phi}_{0}\pm\sqrt{2\zeta^{2}(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}\left[2+A(\zeta-1)\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right]+(1-\zeta^{2})^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}}{\zeta\bar{\Phi}_{0}(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})}.

(397)

This shows that
\operatorname{Re}\lambda^{-}_{1}<0
for all parameter values, whereas
\lambda^{+}_{1}
can acquire a positive real part. The onset of this instability occurs when
\lambda^{+}_{1}
crosses zero, giving

A\geq A^{\star}_{\ell=1}=\frac{2(1+\zeta)^{2}}{\zeta(\zeta-1)(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}},\;\;\mathrm{with}\;\;\zeta>1,

(398)

where
A^{\star}_{\ell=1}(\zeta)
defines the instability boundary at which the composition fields become spontaneously unstable. By using Eq. (
392
), we can see that this also leads to an instability in the shape. For the first spherical harmonics, the instability of these modes (
\ell=1
) corresponds to a spontaneous drift of the membrane compartment.

Herein, the drift velocity
\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}
of the center of mass of the organelle is defined by

\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}},

(399)

where
\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}
is its center-of-mass position, given by

\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}=\frac{1}{V}\iiint\mathrm{d}V\,\boldsymbol{r},

(400)

where the integration is over the enclosed volume
V
, and
\boldsymbol{r}
is the position vector. We can express the center-of-mass position
\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}
, as well as
\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}
, as power series in the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon
; that is,

\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{i})}\!

and

\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}=\sum_{\mathtt{i}=\hskip 0.65pt0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{\mathtt{i}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{i})}\!
. At zeroth order in
\varepsilon
, we find

\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{0})}=\boldsymbol{0}.

(401)

At first order in
\varepsilon
, the center-of-mass position is given by
\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\frac{3R}{4\pi}\iint\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\theta\sin\theta\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\,u(\theta,\varphi)
, and therefore the center-of-mass velocity is found to be

\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\frac{3R}{4\pi}\iint\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\theta\sin\theta\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u(\theta,\varphi).

(402)

Expanding
u(\theta,\varphi)
in spherical harmonics, we note that the components of the radial unit vector
\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
are themselves combinations of
\ell=1
spherical harmonics. By orthogonality, only the
\ell=1
component
u_{1}
contributes to the integral in Eq. (
402
). For the
m=0
component, we have

\iint\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\theta\sin\theta\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\,Y_{1,0}(\theta,\varphi)=\sqrt{{4\pi}/{3}}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{z}
.

Figure 12:

Temporal evolution of the spherical-harmonic amplitudes for mode
\ell=1
. Panels (a) and (b) show the amplitude of the membrane-area flux
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
and the amplitude of the shape rate
\dot{u}_{\ell=1}
, together with the amplitude of the activity-renormalized surface tension
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{A,\ell=1}
. Panels (c) and (d) show the amplitudes
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
of the surface-harmonic modes entering Lamb’s solution, rescaled by
R/\gamma
, together with the amplitudes of the normal and tangential tractions
\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!n,\,\ell=1}
and
\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!s,\,\ell=1}
, rescaled by
R^{2}/(\eta\gamma)
. The membrane-area flux and shape rate are rescaled by
R^{2}/\gamma
, while the activity-renormalized surface tension is rescaled by
R/(\eta\gamma)
. Time is in units of
R^{2}/\gamma
. Here,
\zeta=2.5
,
A=180
,
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
, and
\mathcal{K}=10
, with
u_{\ell=1}(0)=0
and
\Psi_{k,\ell=1}(0)=0.01
for both
k
. With
Q_{2}=-Q_{1}=Q
, we use
Q=125
in panels (a) and (c) for anterograde motion, directed away from the region of net membrane addition, and
Q=75
in panels (b) and (d) for retrograde motion, directed towards it.

Thus, for the
m=0
component of the
\ell=1
mode, the center-of-mass velocity can be written as

\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\boldsymbol{e}_{z}\hskip 1.0ptR\,\frac{\partial u_{1,0}}{\partial t}\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}.

(403)

Here,
m=0
corresponds to translation along the
z
-axis, while combinations of the
m=\pm 1
components correspond to translations along the
x
- and
y
-directions.

As a result, Eq. (
403
) and its generalizations show that
\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!\!\mathrm{cm}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
depends linearly on the rate of change of
u_{1}
, which in turn is coupled through Eq. (
392
) to the compositional fields
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{1}
. These fields become linearly unstable if the condition in Eq. (
398
) is satisfied, resulting in spontaneous drift of the organelle.

The condition for linear stability is given by
A<A^{\star}_{\ell=1}
if
\zeta\!>\!1
, and for every
A\!>\!0
if
\zeta\in(0,1)
. In the latter domain, we also find a region in which the solutions show damped oscillations. The boundary of this regime is found by setting the radicand in Eq. (
397
) to zero; within the oscillatory regime it is negative, giving a complex-conjugate pair of eigenvalues with negative real part. With
\zeta\in(0,1)
, we find

A\geq A^{\textsl{o}}_{\ell=1}=\frac{4\zeta^{2}\left(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right)^{2}+\left(1-\zeta^{2}\right)^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}{2\bar{\Phi}_{0}\left(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}\right)^{2}\left(1-\zeta\right)\zeta^{2}}.

(404)

For higher spherical harmonics (
\ell>1
), we also choose to neglect the curvature couplings to the compositional fields by setting
\Omega_{k}=0
. Thus, the dynamics of
\Psi_{k,\ell}
is decoupled from the shape, and the two eigenvalues associated with the compositional fields, denoted by
\lambda_{\ell}^{\pm}
, are independent of the shape dynamics. We find that
\operatorname{Re}\lambda^{-}_{\ell}<0
for all
\ell>1
, while
\operatorname{Re}\lambda^{+}_{\ell}>0
gives the instability condition for the higher spherical-harmonic modes:

\boxed{A\geq A^{\star}_{\ell}=\frac{\ell(\ell+1)(1+\zeta)^{2}}{\zeta(\zeta-1)(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}},\;\;\mathrm{with}\;\;\zeta>1,}

(405)

where
A^{\star}_{\ell}(\zeta)
marks the onset of the
\ell
-th compositional instability. As for the
\ell=1
mode, the boundary of the stable damped-oscillation regime is obtained by setting the eigenvalue discriminant to zero, giving

A\geq A^{\textsl{o}}_{\ell}=\frac{\ell(\ell+1)\!\left[4\zeta^{2}(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}+(1-\zeta^{2})^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}\right]}{4\bar{\Phi}_{0}(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}(1-\zeta)\zeta^{2}},

(406)

with
\zeta\in(0,1)
. Note that this instability in the composition also drives in turn unstable growth of the corresponding shape modes through Eq. (
387
).

Figure 13:

Temporal evolution of the spherical-harmonic amplitudes for mode
\ell=2
. Panels (a) and (b) show the amplitude of the membrane-area flux
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
and the amplitude of the shape deformation
u_{\ell=2}
, together with the amplitude of the activity-renormalized surface tension
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{A,\ell=2}
. Panels (c) and (d) show the amplitudes
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
, together with the normal and tangential traction amplitudes
\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!n,\,\ell=2}
and
\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!s,\,\ell=2}
. The membrane-area flux is rescaled by
R^{2}/\gamma
, the shape amplitude
u_{\ell=2}
is scaled by
10^{3}
for clarity, the activity-renormalized surface tension by
R/(\eta\gamma)
, the surface harmonics are rescaled by
R/\gamma
, and the traction amplitudes by
R^{2}/(\eta\gamma)
. Time is in units of
R^{2}/\gamma
. Here,
\zeta=2.5
,
A=180
,
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
, and
\mathcal{K}=10
, with
u_{\ell=2}(0)=0
and
\Psi_{k,\ell=2}(0)=0.01
for both
k
. For these initial conditions,
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
remains positive, so the long-time deformation is prolate when
u_{\ell=2}
and
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
have the same sign and oblate when their signs are opposite. With
Q_{2}=-Q_{1}=Q
, we use
Q=125
in panels (a) and (c) for the oblate case and
Q=25
in panels (b) and (d) for the prolate case.

Another instability can arise directly from the shape equation, where the corresponding eigenvalues

\lambda^{u}_{\ell}\!=\!-\frac{\ell^{2}(\ell\!+\!1)^{2}(\ell\!-\!1)(\ell\!+\!2)}{(4\ell^{3}+6\ell^{2}-1)}\!\left[\mathcal{K}\!+\!\displaystyle\frac{(Q_{2}+\zeta Q_{1}\hskip-1.0pt)\left(1\!-\bar{\Phi}_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\right)}{2\ell(\ell+1)(1+\zeta)}\right]

(407)

where we assume an isotonic solution, with the osmotic pressure
\Pi_{\hskip-0.5pt\scriptscriptstyle\Delta}=0
, and
\mathcal{K}=\kappa/(\gamma\eta R)
is a rescaled bending rigidity, non-dimensionalized by the energy scale
\gamma\eta R
. Hence, a band of unstable modes can be obtained (for which
\lambda^{u}_{\ell}>0
) when the following condition holds:

Q_{2}+\zeta Q_{1}<-\frac{2\ell(\ell+1)(1+\zeta)\mathcal{K}}{1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}}.

(408)

Figure 14:

Panels (a) and (b) show the long-time retrograde (yellow) and anterograde (blue) drift regimes in the
A
–
\zeta
plane for fixed
Q=150
and
Q=75
, respectively. The colored curves mark the boundary
Q=Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}(A,\zeta)
separating the two regimes, whose dependence on
A
and
\zeta
is shown in (c). Panels (d) and (e) show the corresponding prolate (green) and oblate (red) deformation regimes for fixed
Q=40
and
Q=25
, respectively. The colored curves mark the boundary
Q=Q^{\star}_{\ell=2}(A,\zeta)
, shown as a function of
A
and
\zeta
in (f). These deformation regimes correspond to the asymptotic linear response to the chosen initial perturbations, which produce a positive quadrupolar membrane-area flux amplitude. The solid black curve marks the
\ell=1
instability threshold, while the dashed black curve in panels (d)–(f) marks the
\ell=2
instability threshold. Black points indicate
(\zeta,A)=(2.5,180)
, as used in Figs.
12
and
13
. Here,
Q_{2}=-Q_{1}=Q
,
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
, and
\mathcal{K}=10
.

If we consider the equal-magnitude, opposite-sign case
Q_{1}=-Q
and
Q_{2}=Q
, where the signed parameter
Q
may take any real value, then
Q<0
gives the second-moment signs of the illustrative two-point force model discussed in Appendix A.7, for which the fission second moment is positive and the fusion second moment is negative, while
Q>0
corresponds to the opposite orientation of the two second moments. The condition for shape instability can be further simplified as follows:

\left\{\!\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle Q<-\frac{2\ell(\ell+1)(1+\zeta)\mathcal{K}}{(1-\zeta)(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})},&\mathrm{if}\;\;\zeta<1,\\[14.0pt]
\displaystyle Q>\frac{2\ell(\ell+1)(1+\zeta)\mathcal{K}}{(\zeta-1)(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})},&\mathrm{if}\;\;\zeta>1.\end{array}\right.

(409)

The corresponding phase regions are plotted in Fig.
2
(c). If we consider the other case, with equal magnitudes and the same sign (
Q_{1}=Q_{2}=Q
), we find that

Q<-\frac{2\ell(\ell+1)\mathcal{K}}{1-\bar{\Phi}_{0}}

(410)

corresponds to the unstable region, which is independent of the parameter
\zeta
. However, in what follows we consider only the case
Q_{1}=-Q
and
Q_{2}=Q
.

B.8
Solutions to membrane shape and composition

The governing equations at first order, Eq. (
391
), can be solved exactly for prescribed initial conditions to obtain the time evolution of the shape and composition amplitudes
u_{\ell}(t)
and
\Psi_{k,\ell}(t)
. In the unstable regime, the component along an unstable eigenmode grows exponentially. These solutions also determine the surface tension from Eq. (
388
) and the activity-renormalized surface tension from Eq. (
389
). We first consider the
\ell=1
mode, governed by Eqs. (
392
) and (
393
), with
u_{\ell=1}(0)=0
and an initial perturbation in the composition fields. The quantities of interest are the shape rate
\dot{u}_{\ell=1}
, which determines the center-of-mass velocity through Eq. (
403
), the membrane-area flux amplitude
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
, and the activity-renormalized surface-tension amplitude
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{A,\ell=1}
, as shown in Fig.
12
. We choose
A
and
\zeta
in the unstable regime defined by Eq. (
398
).

Since this instability condition is independent of the second-moment parameter
Q
, the membrane-area flux grows exponentially at a rate independent of
Q
. The translational response, however, depends on
Q
, and the direction of drift follows from the sign of
\dot{u}_{\ell=1}
relative to the membrane-area flux amplitude. We call the motion retrograde when
\dot{u}_{\ell=1}
and
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
have the same sign, corresponding to motion towards the region of net membrane addition, and anterograde when they have opposite signs, corresponding to motion away from this region. Thus, varying
Q
can reverse the direction of drift relative to the membrane-area flux.

The corresponding surface harmonics
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=1}
determine the surrounding Stokesian flows and are shown together with the hydrodynamic traction amplitudes in Fig.
12
(c) and (d). The tangential and normal traction amplitudes, obtained from Eqs. (
324
) and (
316
), are given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!s,\,\ell}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta(2\ell+1)}{\ell(\ell+1)R}\left[\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}+2\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\right],\qquad\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!n,\,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta(2\ell+1)}{\ell(\ell+1)R}\left[3\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}-(2\ell^{2}+2\ell-3)\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}\right].

(411)

The long-time anterograde and retrograde drift regimes are shown in Figs.
14
(a) and (b) as
\zeta
and
A
vary within the unstable domain defined by Eq. (
398
). The colored curves mark the boundary
Q=Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}(A,\zeta)
, separating retrograde motion for
Q<Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}
from anterograde motion for
Q>Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}
. This boundary is given by

Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}=\frac{2A(\zeta+1)}{2+\bar{\Phi}_{0}\left(\zeta+\zeta^{-1}\right)+\sqrt{2A\bar{\Phi}_{0}(1-\bar{\Phi}_{0})^{2}(\zeta-1)+\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}\left(\zeta+\zeta^{-1}\right)^{2}-8\bar{\Phi}_{0}+4}}.

(412)

In Fig.
14
(c), we show the dependence of
Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}
on
\zeta
and
A
in the unstable region. In the large-
\zeta
limit,
Q^{\star}_{\ell=1}
approaches
A/\bar{\Phi}_{0}>0
, so anterograde drift requires
Q>0
in this limit. The boundary is determined by requiring the leading contribution of the unstable linear mode to the center-of-mass velocity to vanish. Nonlinear mode coupling, however, modifies the finite-amplitude composition and can shift the anterograde–retrograde boundary relative to the linear prediction; see Appendix C.

We next consider the
\ell=2
mode, with an initially undeformed shape,
u_{\ell=2}(0)=0
, and prescribed perturbations of the composition fields
\Psi_{k,\ell=2}
. The resulting evolution of
u_{\ell=2}
is shown in Fig.
13
for two values of
Q
with distinct long-time responses. For these initial perturbations, the quadrupolar membrane-area flux amplitude remains positive, so a prolate deformation corresponds to
u_{2}>0
, whereas an oblate deformation has
u_{2}<0
. Reversing the initial quadrupolar composition perturbations reverses both the flux amplitude and
u_{2}
, interchanging the prolate and oblate shapes while preserving their relative sign.

These solutions also determine the amplitude of the activity-renormalized surface-tension
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{A,\ell=2}
and the surface harmonics
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell=2}
, from which the tangential and normal traction amplitudes
\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!s,\,\ell=2}
and
\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!n,\,\ell=2}
are obtained, as shown in Fig.
13
. The long-time prolate and oblate deformation regimes are shown in Figs.
14
(d) and (e) as
\zeta
and
A
vary within the
\ell=2
unstable domain. The colored curves mark the boundary
Q=Q^{\star}_{\ell=2}(A,\zeta)
separating the two regimes, whose dependence on
\zeta
and
A
is shown in panel (f). For the chosen initial perturbations, the oblate response occurs only for
Q>0
, consistent with
Q^{\star}_{\ell=2}(A,\zeta)>0
.

B.9
Solutions to the surrounding fluid flows

The surface harmonics
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
, determined by
u_{\ell}
and
\Psi_{k,\ell}
, give the surrounding fluid flows through Lamb’s solution in Eq. (
227
). To obtain the explicit spatial flows, we restore the azimuthal index
m
and use the individual amplitudes
\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}
. The inner-fluid velocity for each mode
\ell>0
, with
|m|\leq\ell
, is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!\!-,\ell,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}Y_{\ell,m}\hskip 1.0ptr^{\ell-1}}{2R^{\ell-1}}\left[\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\!\left(1+\ell-(\ell-1)\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt-\left.\!\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\!1\!-\!\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\hskip-1.0pt\right]-\frac{\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\ell,m}\,r^{\ell-1}}{2\ell R^{\ell-1}}\left[\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\!1\!-\!\frac{(\ell\!+\!3)\hskip 1.0ptr^{2}}{(\ell\!+\!1)\hskip 1.0ptR^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\left.-\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\left(1\!+\!\ell-\frac{(\ell-1)(\ell+3)\hskip 1.0ptr^{2}}{(\ell+1)\hskip 1.0ptR^{2}}\right)\right]\!,

(413)

where
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\ell,m}=r\boldsymbol{\nabla}Y_{\ell,m}(\theta,\varphi)
is the tangential vector spherical harmonic. The inner-fluid velocity follows by substituting
p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
and
\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}
from Eqs. (
300
) and (
301
), respectively, into Lamb’s solution, Eq. (
227
). Similarly, the outer-fluid velocity is obtained using
p^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}
and
\Upsilon^{(\mathtt{1})}_{-(\ell+1)}
from Eqs. (
302
) and (
303
), respectively, giving

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!\!+,\ell,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}Y_{\ell,m}R^{\ell+2}}{2\hskip 1.0ptr^{\ell+2}}\left[\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\!-\ell+(\ell+2)\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt-\left.\!\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\!1\!-\!\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\hskip-1.0pt\right]+\frac{\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\ell,m}\hskip 1.0ptR^{\ell+2}}{2(\ell+1)\hskip 1.0ptr^{\ell+2}}\left[\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\!\left(\!1\!-\!\frac{(\ell\!-\!2)\hskip 1.0ptr^{2}}{\ell\hskip 1.0ptR^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt\left.+\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\left(\ell-\frac{(\ell+2)(\ell-2)\hskip 1.0ptr^{2}}{\ell\hskip 1.0ptR^{2}}\right)\right]\!.

(414)

The fluid velocity
\boldsymbol{v}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{(\mathtt{1})}
at the membrane surface (
r=R
) for each surface harmonic mode
\ell
and
m
is found to be

{\boldsymbol{v}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\mathfrak{m},\ell,m}=\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptY_{\ell,m}+\frac{2\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell,m}}{\ell(\ell+1)}\,\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\ell,m}.}

(415)

The axisymmetric (
m=0
) inner and outer fluid flows are illustrated in Fig.
15
(b) for the
\ell=1
mode and in Fig.
15
(e) for the
\ell=2
mode, corresponding to the retrograde drift and prolate deformation regimes, respectively. The complementary anterograde
\ell=1
and oblate
\ell=2
responses are shown in Fig.
3
.

Figure 15:

Illustration of the retrograde motion and prolate deformation regimes for
\zeta=2.5
,
A=180
,
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
, and
\mathcal{K}=10
, at
t=0.3\,R^{2}/\gamma
. We take
Q_{2}=-Q_{1}=Q
, with
Q=75
in panels (a)–(c) and
Q=25
in panels (d)–(f), corresponding to the black points in Fig.
14
(b) and (e), respectively. The fields are axisymmetric (
m=0
) and shown in the
x
-
z
plane through the sphere center, with
Y_{\ell,m}
denoting the spherical harmonic of degree
\ell
and order
m
. Panels (a) and (d) show the membrane-area flux profiles

\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}(\theta)=\sum_{k}\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Psi_{k,\ell,0}Y_{\ell,0}(\theta)
,
for
\ell=1
and
2
, respectively,
with arrows indicating membrane addition or removal. Panels (b) and (e) show the corresponding inner and outer fluid flows. In each case, the density plot and its color bar show the velocity magnitude
V^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\ell}(r,\theta)
, the gray streamlines show the flow direction, and the interfacial color and its color bar show the activity-renormalized surface tension
\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{A,\ell}(\theta)=\Sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{A,\ell,0}Y_{\ell,0}(\theta)
. Panels (c) and (f) show the corresponding outcomes: retrograde motion towards the region of net membrane addition for
\ell=1
, and a prolate deformation along the
z
-axis for
\ell=2
. The membrane-area flux is rescaled by
R^{2}/\gamma
, the fluid velocity by
R/\gamma
, and the activity-renormalized surface tension by
R/(\eta\gamma)
.

From Eq. (
414
), the leading far-field terms of the complete outer fluid velocity are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!\!+}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|m|\leq 1}\frac{3\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,m}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,m}}{4(r/R)}\,(2\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptY_{1,m}+\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{1,m})

\displaystyle+\sum_{|m|\leq 2}\frac{4\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{2,m}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{2,m}}{2(r/R)^{2}}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptY_{2,m}+\mathcal{O}[r^{-3}].

(416)

Using Eqs. (
294
) and (
295
) for
\ell=1
, together with the membrane shape equation in Eq. (
392
), we find that the
1/r
term vanishes identically, since

3\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,m}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,m}=0,\qquad|m|\leq 1.

(417)

Thus, the far-field flow of the outer fluid becomes

\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!\!+}=\sum_{|m|\leq 2}\frac{4\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{2,m}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{2,m}}{2(r/R)^{2}}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptY_{2,m}+\mathcal{O}[r^{-3}].

(418)

The leading
\ell=1
contribution is contained in the
\mathcal{O}(r^{-3})
term. Retaining it explicitly gives

\boldsymbol{V}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!\!+,\,\ell=1}=\sum_{|m|\leq 1}\frac{\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,m}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,m}}{4(r/R)^{3}}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{1,m}-2\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptY_{1,m}).

(419)

Hence, the force-free
\ell=1
flow decays as
1/r^{3}
, whereas the leading
\ell=2
flow decays as
1/r^{2}
. The amplitude of this leading
\ell=2
far-field flow is controlled by
4\mathcal{X}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{2}+\mathcal{Y}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{2}
and can change sign as the balance between membrane-area exchange, the second active force moment, and the shape response is varied.

The net hydrodynamic force on the organelle can also be obtained directly by integrating the traction over the membrane surface. The normal contribution is

\boldsymbol{F}_{\!n}=\iint\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,R^{2}\sin\theta\,\sum_{\ell,m}\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!n,\,\ell,m}\,\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}\hskip 1.0ptY_{\ell,m}(\theta,\varphi).

(420)

Applying Eq. (
411
) to each azimuthal component, we find that only the
\ell=1
modes contribute to the integral. For the axisymmetric
m=0
component,

\boldsymbol{F}_{\!n}\!=10\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{3}}\boldsymbol{e}_{z}\left(\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,0}-\eta R^{2}\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,0}\right)+\cdots

(421)

where
\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,0}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Psi_{k,1,0}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,0}=\sum_{k}\mathbb{Q}^{\hskip 0.5ptk}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\Psi_{k,1,0}
. The remaining terms correspond to
m=\pm 1
and give the equivalent force components along the other spatial directions. Similarly, the tangential contribution to the net hydrodynamic force is

\boldsymbol{F}_{\!s}=\iint\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,R^{2}\sin\theta\,\sum_{\ell,m}\sigma^{(\mathtt{1})}_{\!s,\,\ell,m}\,\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{\ell,m}(\theta,\varphi),

(422)

which gives

\boldsymbol{F}_{\!s}\!=-10\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{3}}\boldsymbol{e}_{z}\left(\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,0}-\eta R^{2}\mathbb{M}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{1,0}\right)+\cdots

(423)

with the
m=\pm 1
components canceling in the same way. Hence,
\boldsymbol{F}_{\!n}\!+\boldsymbol{F}_{\!s}\!=\boldsymbol{0}
, showing that the organelle behaves as a force-free swimmer, moving either retrograde or anterograde relative to the membrane-area flux.

Appendix C
Nonlinear Perturbative Analysis

The linear perturbation analysis indicates that the homogeneous steady state of the membrane compartment becomes unstable, with the compositional instability driving shape distortions (for modes
\ell>1
) and spontaneous drift (for
\ell=1
). We therefore ask whether nonlinearities in the membrane composition can saturate these instabilities and give rise to stable stationary or steadily drifting states.

C.1
Weakly nonlinear compositional dynamics

The compositional instability brings the system into a nonlinear regime, where higher-order terms in the perturbations are no longer negligible.
As in Appendix B, we expand the fissogen and fusogen areal fractions and the passive background fraction about the homogeneous spherical state as
\Phi_{k}=\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k}
and
\Phi_{0}=\bar{\Phi}_{0}-\Psi_{0}
, suppressing the explicit bookkeeping parameter
\varepsilon
throughout Appendix C. Here, the homogeneous areal fractions
\bar{\Phi}_{k}
are given in Eq. (
326
), while
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
is the homogeneous areal fraction of the passive background membrane. The fields
\Psi_{k}
denote the perturbations of the fissogen and fusogen areal fractions, while the passive background perturbation is not independent, since
\Psi_{0}=\Psi_{1}+\Psi_{2}
, cf. Eq. (
329
). The composition perturbations
\Psi_{k}
and
\Psi_{0}
, as well as the shape perturbation
u
, are understood to be small, with order tracked by the total degree in these fields; for instance, linear, quadratic, and cubic terms contain one, two, and three powers of the perturbations, respectively.

As shown in Appendix B, at linear order the
\ell=1
component of the shape perturbation represents rigid translation of the membrane compartment in the laboratory frame. Beyond linear order, translating a deformed membrane mixes spherical-harmonic modes in its radial representation. To separate this motion from intrinsic shape deformations, we work in a frame comoving with the organelle. Denoting its center-of-mass position by
\boldsymbol{X}(t)
, the membrane position in the laboratory frame is

\boldsymbol{R}_{\mathrm{lab}}(\theta,\varphi,t)=\boldsymbol{X}(t)+R\big[1+u(\theta,\varphi,t)\big]\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}.

(424)

The shape deformation field
u
is defined relative to the moving center, with rigid translation represented by
\boldsymbol{X}(t)
rather than by an
\ell=1
shape component. This comoving description removes the rigid translation of the organelle as a whole, but membrane material can still move tangentially relative to the moving center. Such tangential motion changes the angular positions of membrane constituents and therefore advects the composition fields even in the comoving frame.

We make use of the same nondimensionalization as in Appendix B, with length scaled by
R
and time by
R^{2}/\gamma
. We also take the fissogens and fusogens to have the same surface diffusivity,
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=\gamma
, and neglect the curvature–composition couplings and active volume fluxes. With
A
and
\zeta
defined in Eq. (
394
), the dimensionless active membrane-area flux is given by

\frac{R^{2}}{\gamma}\mathbb{M}=A\big(\zeta\Phi_{2}-\Phi_{1}\big)=\bar{\mu}+\mu(\theta,\varphi,t).

(425)

Here,
\bar{\mu}=A(\zeta\bar{\Phi}_{2}-\bar{\Phi}_{1})
is dimensionless and represents the homogeneous steady-state contribution to the active membrane-area flux, which vanishes,
\bar{\mu}=0
, upon using the areal fractions in Eq. (
326
). The dimensionless contribution from the composition perturbations is

\mu(\theta,\varphi,t)=A\big(\zeta\Psi_{2}-\Psi_{1}\big).

(426)

We denote the dimensionless first-order tangential membrane velocity relative to the moving center by
\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}
. The material derivative of the composition perturbations is then

\dot{\Psi}_{k}\simeq\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}+\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\cdot\nabla_{\!\Omega}\Psi_{k},

(427)

where

\nabla_{\!\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}\,\partial_{\theta}+\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}\,(\sin\theta)^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}

is the surface gradient on the unit sphere, with
\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}
and
\boldsymbol{\hat{\varphi}}
the corresponding unit tangent vectors. The partial time derivative is evaluated at fixed angular coordinates in the comoving frame.

Substituting these expansions into the governing equation for the areal fractions, Eq. (
102
), and using the material derivative in Eq. (
427
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}={}

\displaystyle-\mu\left(\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k}\right)+\Delta\ln\!\left(\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}-\Psi_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\cdot\nabla_{\!\Omega}\Psi_{k},

(428)

where
\Delta
is the dimensionless Laplace–Beltrami operator on the deformed membrane surface. The three terms on the right-hand side describe active membrane-area exchange, surface diffusion, and tangential advection, respectively. We retain the nonlinear surface-diffusion contributions to the composition equations through cubic order, while the separate mechanical shape equation and tangential membrane velocity are retained at linear order. For the logarithmic term, neglecting terms of fourth and higher order gives

\ln\!\left(\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}-\Psi_{0}}\right)\simeq\ln\!\left(\frac{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}\right)+\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{k}+\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{2})}_{k}+\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{3})}_{k}.

(429)

The first term is a constant and does not contribute under the Laplace–Beltrami operator, while the remaining terms are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Psi_{0}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}+\frac{\Psi_{k}}{\bar{\Phi}_{k}},

(430)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{2})}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Psi_{0}^{2}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-\frac{\Psi_{k}^{2}}{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right),

(431)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{3})}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{\Psi_{0}^{3}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{3}}+\frac{\Psi_{k}^{3}}{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{3}}\right).

(432)

Because the spatially varying part of the logarithm begins at linear order in the composition perturbations, the Laplace–Beltrami operator must be expanded through quadratic order in
u
to retain the corresponding cubic surface-diffusion terms. For an arbitrary scalar field
f
on the membrane, this gives

\Delta f\simeq\Delta_{\Omega}f-2u\Delta_{\Omega}f+\mathcal{D}_{2}[u]f,

(433)

where
\Delta_{\Omega}=\nabla_{\!\Omega}\!\cdot\nabla_{\!\Omega}
is the Laplace–Beltrami operator on the unit reference sphere. Terms of third and higher order in
u
are neglected, and the quadratic correction is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}[u]f={}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!\Omega}\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\!\Omega}u|^{2}\,\nabla_{\!\Omega}f-\nabla_{\!\Omega}u\left(\nabla_{\!\Omega}u\!\cdot\nabla_{\!\Omega}f\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(3u^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\!\Omega}u|^{2}\right)\Delta_{\Omega}f.

(434)

Substituting the logarithmic and Laplace–Beltrami expansions into Eq. (
428
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}={}\!

\displaystyle-\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mu+\Delta_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{k}

\displaystyle+\left[-\Psi_{k}\mu+\Delta_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{2})}_{k}-2u\Delta_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{k}-\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}\!\cdot\nabla_{\!\Omega}\Psi_{k}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\Delta_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{3})}_{k}-2u\Delta_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{2})}_{k}+\mathcal{D}_{2}[u]\mathcal{L}^{(\mathtt{1})}_{k}\right].

(435)

The three lines on the right-hand side contain the retained linear, quadratic, and cubic contributions to the composition dynamics, respectively. Since
\mu
is linear in the composition perturbations, active membrane-area exchange contributes only at linear and quadratic order. The cubic terms retained here arise from nonlinear surface diffusion and its coupling to the shape perturbation.

C.2
Galerkin projection and truncation method

We restrict to axisymmetric perturbations about the
z
-axis and retain only the
m=0
spherical harmonics. For the harmonic basis, we write
Y_{\ell}\equiv Y_{\ell,0}
, with

Y_{\ell}(\theta)=\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}}\,P_{\ell}(\cos\theta),\quad\int Y_{\ell}Y_{\ell^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega=\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}.

(436)

Here,
\mathrm{d}\Omega=\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\varphi
is the solid-angle element and
P_{\ell}
is the Legendre polynomial of degree
\ell
.

Likewise, we suppress the azimuthal index in the composition and shape amplitudes and write

\Psi_{k,\ell}\equiv\Psi_{k,\ell,0},\qquad u_{\ell}\equiv u_{\ell,0}.

(437)

Retaining the axisymmetric modes with
\ell=0,1,2
, the perturbations of the fissogen and fusogen areal fractions and the intrinsic shape perturbation are

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\theta,t)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1,0}(t)Y_{0}+\Psi_{1,1}(t)Y_{1}+\Psi_{1,2}(t)Y_{2},

(438)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\theta,t)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{2,0}(t)Y_{0}+\Psi_{2,1}(t)Y_{1}+\Psi_{2,2}(t)Y_{2},

(439)

\displaystyle u(\theta,t)

\displaystyle=u_{0}(t)Y_{0}+u_{2}(t)Y_{2}.

(440)

The composition amplitudes
\Psi_{1,\ell}
and
\Psi_{2,\ell}
describe the fissogen and fusogen areal-fraction perturbations at harmonic degree
\ell
. The monopolar (
\ell=0
) mode gives a spatially uniform perturbation of each areal fraction, the dipolar (
\ell=1
) modes describe the fore–aft asymmetry associated with translation, and the quadrupolar (
\ell=2
) modes describe the leading fore–aft-symmetric variation.

The evolution of each retained composition amplitude is obtained by projecting Eq. (
435
) onto the corresponding spherical harmonic

\frac{\partial\Psi_{k,\ell}}{\partial t}=\int Y_{\ell}\,\frac{\partial\Psi_{k}}{\partial t}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega.

(441)

where
\mathrm{d}\Omega=\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\varphi
, which after integration over the azimuthal angle
\varphi
reduces to
2\pi\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta
for the axisymmetric fields considered here. For compactness, we denote the harmonic projection of a scalar field
f(\theta)
by

f_{\ell}\equiv\int Y_{\ell}\,f\,\mathrm{d}\Omega.

(442)

Unlike the linear equations, in which each harmonic degree evolves independently, the nonlinear terms couple different degrees. Only the modes
\ell=0,1,2
are retained as independent amplitudes. Nonlinear products can nevertheless generate higher harmonics as intermediate contributions, which must be retained until the product is projected back onto the modes
\ell=0,1,2
. For example,
Y_{1}Y_{2}
contains both
\ell=1
and
\ell=3
contributions, and at cubic order the latter can combine with another
Y_{1}
to generate an
\ell=2
contribution. These projections require integrals of products of three or four spherical harmonics over the unit sphere. For example, projecting
Y_{1}^{2}
onto
Y_{0}
gives
G_{0}=\int Y_{0}Y_{1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega
. The required angular coupling coefficients are summarized in Table
3
, with the fourth-order integrals expressed in terms of the five independent coefficients
G_{0}
,
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
H_{1}
, and
H_{2}
.

For the fields in Eq. (
438
), the active membrane-area flux defined in Eq. (
425
) takes the form

\mu(\theta,t)=\mu_{0}Y_{0}+\mu_{1}Y_{1}+\mu_{2}Y_{2},

(443)

with each harmonic amplitude given by

\mu_{\ell}=A\left(\zeta\Psi_{2,\ell}-\Psi_{1,\ell}\right).

(444)

Similarly, using the dimensionless active second moments
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
defined in Eq. (
396
), the total dimensionless second moment becomes

\frac{\mathbb{Q}}{\eta\gamma}=Q_{1}\Phi_{1}+Q_{2}\Phi_{2}=\bar{q}+q_{0}Y_{0}+q_{1}Y_{1}+q_{2}Y_{2},

(445)

where
\bar{q}=Q_{1}\bar{\Phi}_{1}+Q_{2}\bar{\Phi}_{2}
is the homogeneous contribution, with each perturbation harmonic amplitude given by

q_{\ell}=Q_{1}\Psi_{1,\ell}+Q_{2}\Psi_{2,\ell}.

(446)

Quadratic product

Cubic product

Y_{1}^{2}

Y_{1}Y_{2}

Y_{2}^{2}

Y_{0}^{3}

Y_{0}Y_{2}^{2}

Y_{1}^{2}Y_{2}
