\displaystyle G_{0}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}},\qquad G_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5\pi}},\qquad G_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{7\sqrt{\pi}},\qquad H_{1}=\frac{11}{28\pi},\qquad H_{2}=\frac{15}{28\pi}

Table 3:

Angular coupling coefficients generated by the nonlinear Galerkin projection. The row gives the spherical harmonic used for the projection and the column gives the quadratic or cubic harmonic product being projected. Each entry is the corresponding integral over the unit sphere. For example, the entry
G_{0}
in row
Y_{0}
and column
Y_{1}^{2}
denotes
\int Y_{0}Y_{1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega=G_{0}
, while the entry
H_{1}
in row
Y_{2}
and column
Y_{1}^{2}Y_{2}
denotes
\int Y_{1}^{2}Y_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega=H_{1}
. Zero entries follow from orthogonality or parity. The final row gives the values of the five independent nonzero coefficients.

In the comoving formulation, the linear
\ell=1
shape response of Appendix B is represented by the center-of-mass position
\boldsymbol{X}(t)
rather than by an intrinsic shape mode. We denote the corresponding dimensionless translational rate by
U_{1}
, while
U_{2}=\dot{u}_{2}
denotes the intrinsic quadrupolar shape rate. For the retained axisymmetric modes, the linear shape equation, Eq. (
387
), gives

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{1}-q_{1}}{3},

(447)

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{2}}{11}-\frac{27q_{2}}{55}-\frac{144\mathcal{K}+12\bar{q}}{55}\,u_{2}.

(448)

where
\mathcal{K}=\kappa/(R\eta\gamma)
is the dimensionless bending rigidity. The center-of-mass relation in Eq. (
403
), expressed in dimensionless form, gives

V_{\mathrm{cm}}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}\,U_{1}=\frac{\mu_{1}-q_{1}}{\sqrt{12\pi}}.

(449)

For the tangential flow retained here, the velocity in Eq. (
427
) is along the
\theta
direction and can be written in terms of a scalar potential as

\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\nabla_{\!\Omega}\chi^{(\mathtt{1})},\qquad\chi^{(\mathtt{1})}=B_{1}Y_{1}+{B_{2}}Y_{2}/3,

(450)

with
\nabla_{\!\Omega}=\boldsymbol{\hat{\theta}}\,\partial_{\theta}
for the axisymmetric fields. The coefficients
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are determined by the local membrane-area balance in Eq. (
95
), which gives

\nabla_{\!\Omega}\!\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}+2U_{2}Y_{2}=\mu_{1}Y_{1}+\mu_{2}Y_{2}.

(451)

Using the identity
\Delta_{\Omega}Y_{\ell}=-\ell(\ell+1)Y_{\ell}
in Eq. (
450
) gives
\nabla_{\!\Omega}\!\cdot\boldsymbol{w}^{(\mathtt{1})}=\Delta_{\Omega}\chi^{(\mathtt{1})}=-2B_{1}Y_{1}-2B_{2}Y_{2}
. Substitution into Eq. (
451
) and matching the coefficients of the
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
spherical harmonics gives

B_{1}=-{\mu_{1}}/{2},\qquad B_{2}=U_{2}-{\mu_{2}}/{2}.

(452)

To write the projected composition equations compactly, we denote the harmonic amplitudes of the linear logarithmic term
\mathcal{L}_{k}^{(\mathtt{1})}
in Eq. (
430
) by

L_{k\ell}\equiv\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}^{(\mathtt{1})}\right)_{\ell}=\frac{\Psi_{0,\ell}}{\bar{\Phi}_{0}}+\frac{\Psi_{k,\ell}}{\bar{\Phi}_{k}}.

(453)

where
\Psi_{0,\ell}=\Psi_{1,\ell}+\Psi_{2,\ell}
follows from Eq. (
329
). Thus, for the retained dipolar and quadrupolar modes, the projected composition dynamics can be separated into linear, quadratic, and cubic contributions as

\dot{\Psi}_{k,\ell}=F_{k,\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}+F_{k,\ell}^{(\mathtt{2})}+F_{k,\ell}^{(\mathtt{3})}.

(454)

The linear contributions recover the corresponding composition dynamics of Appendix B and are given by

\displaystyle F_{k,\ell}^{(\mathtt{1})}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mu_{\ell}-\ell(\ell+1)L_{k\ell},

(455)

The corresponding quadratic contributions, using the tangential-flow coefficients in Eq. (
452
), are given by

\displaystyle F_{k,1}^{(\mathtt{2})}={}

\displaystyle-G_{0}\!\left(\Psi_{k,0}\mu_{1}+\Psi_{k,1}\mu_{0}\right)+G_{1}\!\left[{\mu_{1}}\Psi_{k,2}/2-\left(U_{2}+{\mu_{2}}/{2}\right)\Psi_{k,1}\right]+4\left(G_{0}u_{0}+G_{1}u_{2}\right)L_{k1}

\displaystyle-2G_{0}\!\left({\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,1}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,1}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)-2G_{1}\!\left({\Psi_{0,1}\Psi_{0,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,1}\Psi_{k,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right),

(456)

\displaystyle F_{k,2}^{(\mathtt{2})}={}

\displaystyle-G_{0}\!\left(\Psi_{k,0}\mu_{2}+\Psi_{k,2}\mu_{0}\right)-{3}G_{1}\mu_{1}\Psi_{k,1}/2-G_{2}\!\left(U_{2}+{\mu_{2}}/{2}\right)\Psi_{k,2}+12\left(G_{0}u_{0}+G_{2}u_{2}\right)L_{k2}

\displaystyle-6G_{0}\!\left({\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)-3G_{1}\!\left({\Psi_{0,1}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,1}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)-3G_{2}\left({\Psi_{0,2}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,2}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right).

(457)

The cubic contribution to the dipolar mode is

\displaystyle F_{k,1}^{(\mathtt{3})}={}

\displaystyle-{2\Psi_{0,1}}\!\left(G_{0}^{2}\Psi_{0,0}^{2}+2G_{0}G_{1}\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,2}+H_{1}\Psi_{0,2}^{2}+{(G_{0}^{2}+G_{1}^{2})}\Psi_{0,1}^{2}/3\right)/{\,\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{3}}

\displaystyle-{2\Psi_{k,1}}\!\left(G_{0}^{2}\Psi_{k,0}^{2}+2G_{0}G_{1}\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,2}+H_{1}\Psi_{k,2}^{2}+(G_{0}^{2}+G_{1}^{2})\Psi_{k,1}^{2}/3\right)/{\,\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{3}}

\displaystyle+\left(4G_{0}G_{1}u_{0}+7H_{1}u_{2}+5H_{2}u_{2}\right)\left({\Psi_{0,1}\Psi_{0,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,1}\Psi_{k,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+4\left(G_{0}^{2}u_{0}+G_{0}G_{1}u_{2}\right)\left({\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,1}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,1}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-6\left(G_{0}^{2}u_{0}^{2}+2G_{0}G_{1}u_{0}u_{2}+H_{1}u_{2}^{2}-H_{2}u_{2}^{2}\right)L_{k1}.

(458)

The corresponding quadrupolar contribution is

\displaystyle F_{k,2}^{(\mathtt{3})}={}

\displaystyle 12G_{0}\left(G_{0}u_{0}+G_{2}u_{2}\right)\left({\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)+6\left[G_{0}G_{1}u_{0}+(H_{2}-H_{1})u_{2}\right]\left({\Psi_{0,1}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,1}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+2\left(3G_{0}G_{2}u_{0}+4H_{2}u_{2}\right)\left({\Psi_{0,2}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{2}}-{\Psi_{k,2}^{2}}/{\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{2}}\right)-18G_{0}u_{0}\left(G_{0}u_{0}+2G_{2}u_{2}\right)L_{k2}

\displaystyle-6\left(G_{0}^{2}\Psi_{0,0}^{2}\Psi_{0,2}+G_{0}G_{1}\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,1}^{2}+G_{0}G_{2}\Psi_{0,0}\Psi_{0,2}^{2}+H_{1}\Psi_{0,1}^{2}\Psi_{0,2}+{H_{2}}\Psi_{0,2}^{3}/3\right)/{\,\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{3}}

\displaystyle-6\left(G_{0}^{2}\Psi_{k,0}^{2}\Psi_{k,2}+G_{0}G_{1}\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,1}^{2}+G_{0}G_{2}\Psi_{k,0}\Psi_{k,2}^{2}+H_{1}\Psi_{k,1}^{2}\Psi_{k,2}+{H_{2}}\Psi_{k,2}^{3}/3\right)/\,\bar{\Phi}_{k}^{3}.

(459)

These expressions contain the cubic logarithmic contribution and the shape-dependent surface-diffusion corrections retained in Eq. (
435
). The nonlinear terms couple the dipolar and quadrupolar composition modes to one another and to the shape perturbation. Together with
\dot{u}_{2}=U_{2}
from Eq. (
448
), they give five differential equations for
\Psi_{1,1}
,
\Psi_{2,1}
,
\Psi_{1,2}
,
\Psi_{2,2}
, and
u_{2}
.
The monopolar (
\ell=0
) composition modes behave differently because
\Delta_{\Omega}Y_{0}=0
, so surface diffusion provides no restoring contribution. Their linear dynamics is
\dot{\Psi}_{k,0}=-\bar{\Phi}_{k}\mu_{0}
, with
\mu_{0}=A(\zeta\Psi_{2,0}-\Psi_{1,0})
, and the steady-state relation
\bar{\Phi}_{1}=\zeta\bar{\Phi}_{2}
gives
\dot{\mu}_{0}=0
. A nonzero
\mu_{0}
would therefore drive a secular linear change in the monopolar amplitudes. We consequently do not evolve
\Psi_{1,0}
and
\Psi_{2,0}
independently, but determine them together with
u_{0}
from the fixed-volume and component-conservation constraints derived in the following subsection.

C.3
Constraints and stability of drifting states

The remaining isotropic amplitudes
\Psi_{1,0}
,
\Psi_{2,0}
, and
u_{0}
are determined by the global constraints. For the membrane shape in Eq. (
424
), the area element through cubic order in the perturbations is

\mathrm{d}S\simeq R^{2}\!\left(1+2u+u^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\!\Omega}u|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\Omega.

(460)

We define the excess area as
\Delta\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{0})}
,
where
\mathcal{A}^{(\mathtt{0})}=4\pi R^{2}
is the area of the homogeneous spherical state. Using the shape expansion in Eq. (
438
), we obtain

\frac{\Delta\mathcal{A}}{R^{2}}\simeq 4\sqrt{\pi}\,u_{0}+u_{0}^{2}+4u_{2}^{2}.

(461)

The cubic contributions to the area element cancel identically. The excess area is determined by the isotropic and quadrupolar shape amplitudes and need not vanish at fixed enclosed volume. The volume is given by

V=\frac{R^{3}}{3}\int(1+u)^{3}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega.

(462)

For the retained shape modes, the angular integrals required to expand the volume through cubic order are

\displaystyle\int u\,\mathrm{d}\Omega=2\sqrt{\pi}\,u_{0},\quad\int u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega=u_{0}^{2}+u_{2}^{2},

(463)

\displaystyle\int u^{3}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega=G_{0}u_{0}^{3}+3G_{0}u_{0}u_{2}^{2}+G_{2}u_{2}^{3}.

(464)

Requiring the enclosed volume to remain equal to its homogeneous steady-state value,
V^{(\mathtt{0})}=4\pi R^{3}/3
, then gives the cubic fixed-volume constraint

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{V}={}

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\pi}\,u_{0}+u_{0}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}\left(G_{0}u_{0}^{3}+3G_{0}u_{0}u_{2}^{2}+G_{2}u_{2}^{3}\right)=0.

(465)

The total amounts of the two active membrane components are separately conserved. Since the total membrane density is constant, we absorb this constant factor into the conserved amount

N_{k}\equiv\int_{\mathcal{M}}\Phi_{k}\,\mathrm{d}S=4\pi R^{2}\bar{\Phi}_{k},

(466)

Substituting
\Phi_{k}=\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k}
and the area element in Eq. (
460
), and retaining terms through cubic order, gives

\int\left[(\bar{\Phi}_{k}+\Psi_{k})\!\left(1+2u+u^{2}+\frac{|\nabla_{\!\Omega}u|^{2}}{2}\right)\!-\bar{\Phi}_{k}\right]\mathrm{d}\Omega=0.

The dipolar amplitudes
\Psi_{1,1}
and
\Psi_{2,1}
do not contribute to the global conservation constraints for the fissogen and fusogen amounts. Since the retained shape contains only the even harmonics
Y_{0}
and
Y_{2}
, the area element is even under
\cos\theta\mapsto-\cos\theta
, whereas
Y_{1}
is odd. Any contribution proportional to a dipolar amplitude is odd and integrates to zero over the sphere. Evaluating the remaining angular integrals using Table
3
gives the conservation constraint for each active membrane component:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}={}

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}_{k}\,\frac{\Delta\mathcal{A}}{R^{2}}+\left[2\sqrt{\pi}+2u_{0}+G_{0}\left(u_{0}^{2}+4u_{2}^{2}\right)\right]\Psi_{k,0}

\displaystyle+\left(2u_{2}+2G_{0}u_{0}u_{2}+\frac{5}{2}G_{2}u_{2}^{2}\right)\Psi_{k,2}=0.

(467)

Together with the fixed-volume constraint in Eq. (
465
), these component-conservation constraints determine the isotropic amplitudes
\Psi_{1,0}
,
\Psi_{2,0}
, and
u_{0}
.

Figure 16:

Color maps of the critical active second moments (a)
Q_{1}^{\ast}
and (b)
Q_{2}^{\ast}
as functions of
\zeta
and
A
. At each point in the colored region, the critical pair
(Q_{1}^{\ast},Q_{2}^{\ast})
is obtained by solving the nonlinear fixed-point equations with
V_{\mathrm{cm}}=u_{2}=0
, excluding the homogeneous spherical solution. These conditions identify the intersection of the drift-reversal and prolate–oblate boundaries. The black curve marks the linear drift-instability threshold, below which the homogeneous sphere is stable and satisfies both conditions identically. Above the red curve, only unstable fixed points satisfying both conditions were found. The colors indicate the corresponding values of the critical moments.

In the weakly nonlinear reduction, the enclosed volume and the total amounts of the active components are conserved, while the mechanical shape dynamics are retained only at linear order. Consequently, the reduced transient dynamics does not enforce the full nonlinear membrane-area balance exactly. At each nonlinear fixed point, however, the intrinsic shape is stationary,
U_{2}=0
, so the rate of change of the total membrane area vanishes,
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}/\mathrm{d}t=0
. The corresponding excess area need not vanish and is given in terms of the isotropic and quadrupolar shape amplitudes by Eq. (
461
).

Combining the retained dynamical equations with the global constraints leads to a reduced dynamical system of differential-algebraic equations. We collect its five dynamical amplitudes and three constrained isotropic amplitudes as follows:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}=\left(\Psi_{1,1},\Psi_{2,1},\Psi_{1,2},\Psi_{2,2},u_{2}\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(468)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}=\left(\Psi_{1,0},\Psi_{2,0},u_{0}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}.

(469)

The reduced dynamical system can then be written as

\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z}),\quad\boldsymbol{C}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z})=\boldsymbol{0},

(470)

where the dynamical and constraint functions are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}=\left(F_{1,1},F_{2,1},F_{1,2},F_{2,2},U_{2}\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(471)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}=\left(\mathcal{C}_{V},\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}.

(472)

Thus, the dipolar and quadrupolar composition amplitudes
\Psi_{1,1}
,
\Psi_{2,1}
,
\Psi_{1,2}
, and
\Psi_{2,2}
, together with the shape amplitude
u_{2}
, evolve dynamically, whereas the monopolar amplitudes
\Psi_{1,0}
,
\Psi_{2,0}
, and
u_{0}
are determined by the fixed-volume constraint
\mathcal{C}_{V}=0
and the fissogen and fusogen conservation constraints
\mathcal{C}_{k}=0
for
k=1,2
.

A steadily drifting state is stationary in the comoving frame, with constant shape and composition amplitudes and a center-of-mass position
\boldsymbol{X}(t)
that advances at constant velocity. For the reduced dynamical system in Eq. (
470
), such a fixed point
(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})
satisfies

\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\boldsymbol{C}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})=\boldsymbol{0}.

(473)

The condition
U_{2}=0
makes the intrinsic quadrupolar shape stationary in the comoving frame. Since all amplitudes are constant at a fixed point, Eq. (
449
) also gives a constant center-of-mass velocity. Denoting quantities evaluated at the fixed point by a superscript
\ast
, the corresponding fixed point represents a drifting state when

V_{\mathrm{cm}}^{\ast}=\frac{\mu_{1}^{\ast}-q_{1}^{\ast}}{\sqrt{12\pi}}\neq 0.

(474)

To determine the linear stability of a fixed point, we consider small perturbations of the dynamical amplitudes
\boldsymbol{x}
. The constrained amplitudes
\boldsymbol{z}
must then adjust so that
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{0}
remains satisfied. We write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast}+\delta\boldsymbol{x},

(475)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast}+\delta\boldsymbol{z}.

(476)

Linearizing the differential equations and constraints about the fixed point gives

\displaystyle\delta\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=F_{x}\,\delta\boldsymbol{x}+F_{z}\,\delta\boldsymbol{z},

(477)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}

\displaystyle=C_{x}\,\delta\boldsymbol{x}+C_{z}\,\delta\boldsymbol{z},

(478)

where all derivatives are evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})
, with

\displaystyle F_{x}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{F}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}}\right|_{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})},

\displaystyle F_{z}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{F}}{\partial\boldsymbol{z}}\right|_{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})},

(479)

\displaystyle C_{x}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{C}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}}\right|_{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})},

\displaystyle C_{z}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{C}}{\partial\boldsymbol{z}}\right|_{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\ast},\boldsymbol{z}^{\ast})}.

(480)

If
C_{z}
is nonsingular, the linearized constraints determine the response of the constrained amplitudes to a perturbation
\delta\boldsymbol{x}
as

\delta\boldsymbol{z}=-C_{z}^{-1}C_{x}\,\delta\boldsymbol{x}.

(481)

Substituting this into the differential equations gives

\displaystyle\delta\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}=J_{\mathrm{con}}\,\delta\boldsymbol{x},\quad J_{\mathrm{con}}=F_{x}-F_{z}\,C_{z}^{-1}C_{x}.

(482)

Figure 17:

Panels (a)–(f) show, respectively, the center-of-mass velocity
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
, dipolar membrane-area flux amplitude
\mu_{1}
, drift order parameter
\Xi_{1}
, quadrupolar shape amplitude
u_{2}
, quadrupolar membrane-area flux amplitude
\mu_{2}
, and shape order parameter
\Xi_{2}
, as functions of
\zeta
at
A=280
,
Q_{1}=250
,
Q_{2}=-575
,
\mathcal{K}=10
, and
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
. Green, yellow, blue, and purple indicate the stationary sphere (SS), anterograde prolate (AP), anterograde oblate (AO), and retrograde oblate (RO) states, respectively. In panels (a) and (b), solid and colored dashed curves represent symmetry-related solutions with opposite signs of
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and
\mu_{1}
, while all other quantities remain unchanged. The AP–AO shape transition precedes the AO–RO drift reversal. For these moving states,
\mu_{2}<0
, giving
\Xi_{2}=-u_{2}
. The velocity
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and drift order parameter
\Xi_{1}
are scaled by
\gamma/R
, while the flux amplitudes
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are scaled by
\gamma/R^{2}
;
u_{2}
and
\Xi_{2}
are dimensionless.

The drifting fixed point is locally stable when all eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
of
J_{\mathrm{con}}
satisfy
\operatorname{Re}\lambda_{i}<0
. A positive real part identifies an unstable direction. Since
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
depends on the dipolar composition amplitudes, the effect of each perturbation direction on the drift velocity follows from

\delta V_{\mathrm{cm}}=\frac{(A\zeta-Q_{2})\,\delta\Psi_{2,1}-(A+Q_{1})\,\delta\Psi_{1,1}}{\sqrt{12\pi}}.

(483)

Thus, the eigenvectors of
J_{\mathrm{con}}
determine both the perturbation directions and their first-order effect on the drift velocity. Numerically, the action of
C_{z}^{-1}
is obtained by solving the corresponding linear system; a singular
C_{z}
prevents this reduction, while a small singular value indicates poor conditioning.

Figure 18:

Panels (a)–(f) show, respectively, the center-of-mass velocity
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
, dipolar membrane-area flux amplitude
\mu_{1}
, drift order parameter
\Xi_{1}
, quadrupolar shape amplitude
u_{2}
, quadrupolar membrane-area flux amplitude
\mu_{2}
, and shape order parameter
\Xi_{2}
, as functions of
\zeta
at
A=300
,
Q_{1}=-125
,
Q_{2}=125
,
\mathcal{K}=10
, and
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=3/5
. Light green, orange, yellow, and dark green indicate the stationary sphere (SS), retrograde prolate (RP), anterograde prolate (AP), and stationary prolate (SP) states, respectively. The RP–AP drift reversal occurs while the shape remains prolate (
u_{2}>0
). At the subsequent AP–SP transition,
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and
\mu_{1}
vanish, while the prolate deformation and quadrupolar membrane-area flux remain finite. In panels (a) and (b), solid and colored dashed curves represent symmetry-related drifting solutions with opposite signs of
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and
\mu_{1}
, while the other quantities remain unchanged. For the RP, AP, and SP states,
\mu_{2}<0
, giving
\Xi_{2}=-u_{2}
. The velocity
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and drift order parameter
\Xi_{1}
are scaled by
\gamma/R
, while the flux amplitudes
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are scaled by
\gamma/R^{2}
;
u_{2}
and
\Xi_{2}
are dimensionless.

C.4
Fixed points and phase boundaries

We numerically determine the nonlinear fixed points of Eq. (
473
) using a Newton–Raphson method, repeating the search from randomly sampled initial guesses at several amplitude scales and retaining only distinct solutions. Their stability is assessed using the constrained Jacobian in Eq. (
482
). Repeating this analysis across parameter space, we identify stable stationary and drifting states with spherical, prolate, or oblate shapes. To characterize the direction of drift and shape deformation relative to their respective membrane-area flux amplitudes, we define two signed order parameters:

\displaystyle\Xi_{1}

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{cm}}\operatorname{sgn}(\mu_{1}),

(484)

\displaystyle\Xi_{2}

\displaystyle=u_{2}\operatorname{sgn}(\mu_{2}).

(485)

Note that the drift order parameter
\Xi_{1}
is denoted simply by
\Xi
in the main text; see Fig.
4
(c). Negative and positive values of
\Xi_{1}
correspond to anterograde and retrograde motion, respectively, while
u_{2}>0
and
u_{2}<0
distinguish prolate and oblate shapes. By reflection symmetry, stable drifting solutions occur in pairs with opposite
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and
\mu_{1}
, but unchanged
u_{2}
and
\mu_{2}
. Consequently, both order parameters
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
remain unchanged, and the paired solutions belong to the same phase.

To find the precise locations of the drift-reversal and prolate–oblate boundaries, we seek parameter values for which the nonlinear system admits fixed points with
V_{\mathrm{cm}}^{\ast}=0
and
u_{2}^{\ast}=0
, respectively. Using Eqs. (
449
) and (
448
), we find that

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{cm}}^{\ast}=0

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mu_{1}^{\ast}=q_{1}^{\ast},

(486)

\displaystyle u_{2}^{\ast}=0

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mu_{2}^{\ast}=27q_{2}^{\ast}/5.

(487)

We next determine where the drift-reversal and prolate–oblate boundaries intersect as both active second moments
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
vary. At fixed
A
and
\zeta
, this intersection defines a critical pair
(Q_{1}^{\ast},Q_{2}^{\ast})
. Since
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
depend linearly on
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, the preceding relations allow us to express both moments in terms of the composition amplitudes. We then obtain the critical pair and the corresponding amplitudes self-consistently by solving the nonlinear fixed-point equations, Eq. (
473
), subject to the simultaneous conditions
V_{\mathrm{cm}}^{\ast}=0
and
u_{2}^{\ast}=0
.

We exclude the homogeneous spherical solution, which satisfies both zero conditions for arbitrary active second moments. The critical moments corresponding to stable nontrivial fixed points are shown in Fig.
16
. These solutions occupy the region between the black curve, marking the linear
\ell=1
instability threshold of the homogeneous sphere, and the red curve, beyond which only linearly unstable critical fixed points are found. Throughout this region,
Q_{2}^{\ast}<0
, while
Q_{1}^{\ast}
is predominantly positive at moderate
\zeta
and becomes negative at larger
\zeta
.

Each critical pair provides a reference point for exploring the nearby phase structure. By varying
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
slightly around
(Q_{1}^{\ast},Q_{2}^{\ast})
and evaluating stable fixed points on a local stencil, we identify four neighbouring phases combining anterograde or retrograde motion with prolate or oblate morphology. These phases occupy regions separated by the intersecting drift-reversal and prolate–oblate boundaries. In particular, the anterograde oblate (AO) phase forms a wedge between these boundaries, with an opening angle and orientation that vary with
A
and
\zeta
. This reveals conditions on the active second moments for obtaining stable AO motion, which we find predominantly for
Q_{1}>0
and
Q_{2}<0
. Since a phase diagram at fixed
(Q_{1},Q_{2})
represents only a two-dimensional cross section of the four-dimensional parameter space, a narrow AO region in such a diagram need not imply that the phase is similarly restricted in the full parameter space. The right-hand diagram in Fig.
4
(c), with
(Q_{1},Q_{2})=(250,-575)
, illustrates one such choice supporting stable AO states.

However, the active second moments need not be close to these critical values for the system to exhibit stable stationary or drifting states. Different choices of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
can produce other combinations of motion and shape. For example, the left-hand diagram in Fig.
4
(c), with
(Q_{1},Q_{2})=(-125,125)
, exhibits stationary sphere (SS), retrograde prolate (RP), anterograde prolate (AP), and stationary prolate (SP) phases, without an AO region in the parameter range shown.

To examine the transitions between these phases, we follow the nonlinear fixed-point branches as
\zeta
varies at fixed
A
,
Q_{1}
, and
Q_{2}
. The bifurcation curves in Fig.
17
reveal a region where both the SS state and finite-amplitude drifting states are stable near the linear drift-instability threshold, consistent with a subcritical onset of drift. Along the stable drifting branches, the AP–AO shape transition precedes the AO–RO drift reversal, so the organelle changes from prolate to oblate while moving anterogradely before reversing its direction of motion.

The bifurcation curves in Fig.
18
likewise show a discontinuous onset of drift, with a finite jump from SS to the retrograde prolate (RP) state. This is followed by an RP–AP drift reversal and a continuous transition to the stationary prolate (SP) phase. At the latter transition, both
V_{\mathrm{cm}}
and
\mu_{1}
vanish, while
u_{2}>0
and
\mu_{2}\neq 0
, so the prolate deformation and quadrupolar membrane-area flux persist even when translation ceases.

To estimate the center-of-mass speeds in physical units, we take
R=1~\mu\mathrm{m}
,
\gamma=0.002~\mu\mathrm{m}^{2}/\mathrm{s}
,
\eta=1~\mathrm{Pa}\cdot\mathrm{s}
, and
\kappa=2\times 10^{-20}~\mathrm{J}
, consistent with the ranges in Tables I and II. These values give
\mathcal{K}=10
and a characteristic velocity
\gamma/R=120~\mathrm{nm/min}
. In the right-hand phase diagram of Fig.
4
(c), the active second moments
Q_{1}=250
and
Q_{2}=-575
correspond to
0.5~\mathrm{pN}
and
-1.15~\mathrm{pN}
, respectively. The numerically sampled stable AO states are found to be approximately within
A=203
–
424
and
\zeta=1.18
–
1.33
, corresponding to membrane-area transfer rates
|\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt1}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}|\sim 0.41
–
0.85~\mathrm{s}^{-1}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\hskip 0.5pt2}_{\scalebox{0.9}{$\circlearrowleft$}}\sim 0.48
–
1.13~\mathrm{s}^{-1}
. For these states, the average dimensionless center-of-mass speed is approximately
1.1
, with a maximum of about
2
, corresponding to physical speeds of approximately
130~\mathrm{nm/min}
and
250~\mathrm{nm/min}
, respectively.

For comparison, experimental measurements of effective cis-to-trans cargo progression through the Golgi give velocities of order
10~\mathrm{nm/min}

[
67
]
, roughly an order of magnitude smaller than our predicted center-of-mass speeds. However, we expect cisternal motion to be hindered by interactions with the surrounding cytoskeleton and neighbouring compartments, which are not included in our model. These additional mechanical constraints could substantially reduce the resulting drift speed, potentially bringing it closer to the measured cargo progression rate. Such a comparison remains indirect, however, since cargo progression also reflects vesicular exchange and biochemical maturation rather than physical cisternal motion alone.

Appendix D
Mathematical Prerequisites

D.1
Differential Geometry of Surfaces

We briefly outline the mathematical language of differential geometry of two-dimensional surfaces, concentrating on the practical aspects of the subject rather than mathematical rigor. This brief review assumes that the reader is acquainted with some basic knowledge of vector and tensor calculus. It is noteworthy to mention that an extensive literature exists on differential geometry, with emphasis on both pure mathematics (e.g. Ref.
[
104
]
) and applications in physics (e.g. Refs.
[
84
]
and
[
105
]
).

D.1.1
Surface parametrization

Each point on a two-dimensional surface, say
\mathcal{S}
, embedded in the three-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is specified by a position vector
\boldsymbol{R}=\left(X,\,Y,\,Z\right)^{\!\mathsf{T}}\!
, where
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are the Cartesian coordinates, and
\mathsf{T}
is a transpose
[
88
]
. However, these coordinates are constrained by the geometry of the surface and are thus not all independent.

Figure 19:
Schematic diagram of a two-dimensional surface
\mathcal{S}
, whose boundary is denoted by
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Every point on the surface can be identified by a three-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{R}
(described by the Cartesian coordinates
X
,
Y
and
Z
). Also, for each vector
\boldsymbol{R}
, we define the tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
, corresponding to the internal coordinates
\xi^{1}
and
\xi^{2}
, respectively, as well as the unit vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
normal to the tangent plane.

Therefore, the embedding surface function
\boldsymbol{R}
can be described locally by a pair of two variables, denoted by
(\xi^{1},\xi^{2})
, which represent the internal
curvilinear
coordinates associated with a parametrization of the surface. At each point on the surface, a basis can be formed by constructing the tangent vectors and their corresponding normal vector, as shown in Fig.
19
. The tangent vector associated with the internal coordinate
\xi^{\alpha}
is defined by

\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\frac{\partial\hskip-0.5pt\boldsymbol{R}}{\partial\xi^{\alpha}}\equiv\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{R},

(488)

with index
\alpha=1,2
, whilst the unit vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
normal to the surface (
i.e.
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=0
and
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=1
) is given by

\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}\times\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}\right)/\left\|\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}\times\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}\right\|,

(489)

where
\times
is the three-dimensional cross product, and
\left\|\cdot\right\|
gives the norm of the vector. The vectors
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}
and
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}
are not orthonormal in general, namely the four elements of

g_{\alpha\beta}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta},

(490)

known as the
metric tensor
, when written in matrix form, typically give
[g_{\alpha\beta}]\neq\Big[\!\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\[-2.0pt]
0&1\end{array}\!\Big]
. Instead, we introduce the corresponding dual basis vectors,
\boldsymbol{e}^{1}
and
\boldsymbol{e}^{2}
, which satisfy

\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\beta}=\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta},

(491)

where
\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
is the Kronecker delta,
i.e.
\left[\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}\right]=\Big[\!\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\[-2.0pt]
0&1\end{array}\!\Big]
. Note that metric tensor is a symmetric tensor,
i.e.
g_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\beta\alpha}
. Also, the inverse of the metric tensor,
g^{\alpha\beta}
, satisfies

g^{\alpha\beta}g_{\beta\gamma}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\gamma},

(492)

where Einstein convention is employed,
i.e.
summation over repeated super- and sub-script indices is implied. It follows that
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
g^{\alpha\beta}
can be used to lower and raise indices, respectively; for example,

\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=g^{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta},\quad\text{and}\quad\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=g_{\alpha\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\beta},

(493)

The metric tensor captures the distance information on the surface, being an intrinsic geometrical property of that surface, and defines the scalar product on
\mathcal{S}
; namely, for two vectors
\boldsymbol{v}=v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{w}=w^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
, we have that
\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}=\left(v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\right)\cdot\left(w^{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}\right)=g_{\alpha\beta}\,v^{\alpha}\,w^{\beta}=v_{\alpha}w^{\alpha}=v^{\alpha}w_{\alpha}
.

D.1.2
Surface decomposition

Both triads
\{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1},\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2},\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\}
and
\{\boldsymbol{e}^{1},\boldsymbol{e}^{2},\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\}
can be used as bases to decompose any three-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{v}
into its normal and in-plane vectorial parts, namely
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}+\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
, where
\boldsymbol{v}_{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=v\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
, in which we define the normal vector component by
v=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, the co- and contra-variant vector components by
v_{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and
v^{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
, respectively. From (
492
) and (
493
), we find that the metric tensor and its inverse can be used to raise and lower the index of vector components,
i.e.
v^{\alpha}=g^{\alpha\beta}\,v_{\beta}
and
v_{\alpha}=g_{\alpha\beta}\,v^{\beta}
.

Furthermore, we define the surface identity tensor,

\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha},

(494)

where
\otimes
is the tensor product. This second-order tensor acts as projection operator onto the tangent plane of the surface, extracting the in-plane vector
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
from
\boldsymbol{v}
(that is,
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
). Similarly, we define the three-dimensional identity tensor
\boldsymbol{I}
as follows:

\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},

(495)

which satisfies that
\boldsymbol{I}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{v}
for any vector
\boldsymbol{v}
.

The surface identity can also be used to extract the tangential part of any second-order tensor
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, namely

\boldsymbol{T}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=T^{\alpha\beta}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta},

(496)

where

T^{\alpha\beta}=\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\right)\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\beta}.

(497)

In a similar fashion, the normal component of the tensor
\boldsymbol{T}
can be written as

\boldsymbol{T}_{\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=T\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},\quad\,T=\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\right)\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}.

(498)

In addition to these, a second-order tensor may also have in general components along
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
and
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
.

D.1.3
Surface differentiation

We denote ordinary differentiation with respect to the surface coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
by
\partial_{\alpha}
. Differentiating Eq. (
488
) gives
\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{R}
, which generally has both tangential and normal components. We denote covariant differentiation by
\nabla_{\beta}
. For the tangent basis, it removes the tangential connection term, leaving the normal component,
i.e.

\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}.

(499)

Using Eqs. (
494
) and (
495
), this can also be expressed in terms of the Christoffel symbols as

\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\boldsymbol{e}_{\gamma},

(500)

where
\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}=\boldsymbol{e}^{\gamma}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
are the
Christoffel symbols
of the second kind, which can be also rewritten in terms of the derivatives of the metric tensor, namely

\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{g^{\gamma\delta}}{2}\left(\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\delta}+\partial_{\alpha}g_{\delta\beta}-\partial_{\delta}g_{\alpha\beta}\right)\!,

(501)

which shows the symmetry property that
\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}=\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\beta\alpha}
.

For the basis vector
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
, its covariant derivative is
\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
. From (
494
) and (
495
), we find

\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}\boldsymbol{e}^{\gamma}.

(502)

For a scalar
w\in\mathbb{R}
, covariant and ordinary differentiation coincide,
\nabla_{\alpha}w=\partial_{\alpha}w
. For a three-dimensional vector field
\boldsymbol{v}
without surface indices, the derivative of the full vector similarly satisfies
\nabla_{\beta}\boldsymbol{v}=\partial_{\beta}\boldsymbol{v}
, whereas the covariant derivatives of its tangential components include the Christoffel symbols:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\beta}v_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta}v_{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}v_{\gamma},

(503)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\beta}v^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta}v^{\alpha}+\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}v^{\gamma}.

(504)

A useful property of the covariant derivative is the fact that the metric tensor and its inverse is constant with respect to the covariant derivative,
i.e.

\nabla_{\gamma}g_{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{\gamma}g^{\alpha\beta}=0,

(505)

which expresses the metric compatibility of the surface connection. In practice, this means that the process of raising and lowering of indices commutes with the covariant differentiation.

Although covariant differentiation gives a tensorial derivative of the tangent basis, successive covariant derivatives do not generally commute, namely

(\nabla_{\beta}\nabla_{\alpha}-\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta})v_{\gamma}=-v^{\delta}R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta},

(506)

where
R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
is the
Riemann curvature tensor
. This is, in general, a nonzero tensor, vanishing for intrinsically flat surfaces, and its components are given by

R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}=\partial_{\gamma}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta\delta}-\partial_{\delta}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta\gamma}-\Gamma^{\omega}_{\alpha\gamma}\Gamma_{\omega\beta\delta}+\Gamma^{\omega}_{\alpha\delta}\Gamma_{\omega\beta\gamma},

(507)

where
\Gamma_{\gamma\alpha\beta}=g_{\gamma\delta}\Gamma^{\delta}_{\phantom{\delta}\alpha\beta}
are the Christoffel symbols of the first kind. Since
R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
depends only on the metric tensor, it is then an intrinsic quantity of the surface.

With the help of the covariant derivative, we can now define the surface analogous of the regular differential operators, encountered in vector calculus: gradients, divergences, and Laplacian operators. The surface gradient of a scalar function
\phi
that lives on
\mathcal{S}
is defined by

\nabla\phi=\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\phi=\partial_{\alpha}\phi\,\boldsymbol{e}^{\alpha}=\nabla_{\alpha}\phi\,\boldsymbol{e}^{\alpha},

(508)

where
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
is the usual gradient operator in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Similarly, the gradient of a vector function
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
can be written as follows
\nabla\boldsymbol{\Phi}=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{\Phi})\cdot\boldsymbol{I}_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=(\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Phi})\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}
. This allows us to define the surface divergence of
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
, namely

\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\nabla\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hskip 0.5pt\mathrel{\rotatebox{90.0}{.{}.}}\boldsymbol{I}=(\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Phi})\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}=(\nabla_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Phi})\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha},

(509)

where
\,\mathrel{\rotatebox{90.0}{.{}.}}\,
denotes the double dot product. Let
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
be solely defined on
\mathcal{S}
, with
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\phi^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
, then Eq. (
509
) can be written in component form as follows:

\text{div}\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\nabla_{\alpha}\phi^{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}\!\left(\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0pt\phi^{\alpha}\right)/\sqrt{g}\,,

(510)

where the former follows from (
500
) and (
501
), while the latter is a result known as the
Voss–Weyl formula
, in which we define that

g=\det[g_{\alpha\beta}].

(511)

Metric compatibility also implies
\partial_{\alpha}\sqrt{g}=\sqrt{g}\,\Gamma^{\beta}_{\beta\alpha}
.

We can define the surface Laplacian for a scalar function
\phi
as follows:

\Delta\phi=\text{div}\,\nabla\phi=g^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}\phi,

(512)

where the latter is given by

\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}\phi=\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\phi-\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{\gamma}\phi.

(513)

Lastly, we note that the most effective way to compute the surface Laplacian of a scalar function is via the Voss–Weyl formula, which allows us to rewrite (
512
) as

\Delta\phi=\partial_{\alpha}\!\left(\sqrt{g}\hskip 1.0ptg^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi\right)/\sqrt{g}.

(514)

D.1.4
Surface curvature

As previously mentioned, the curvature of a surface can be described via the normal component of the second (parametric) derivatives of
\boldsymbol{R}
. Consequently, a second-order tensor can be constructed as
\boldsymbol{B}=b_{\alpha\beta}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\alpha}\otimes\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\beta}
, which is known as the curvature tensor, and its covariant elements
b_{\alpha\beta}
are defined by

b_{\alpha\beta}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{R}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}

(515)

which is a symmetric tensor, and the sign of
b_{\alpha\beta}
depends on the choice of the normal. The mixed components of
\boldsymbol{B}
are thus given by
b_{\alpha}^{\hskip 0.5pt\beta}=b_{\alpha\gamma}\hskip 1.0ptg^{\gamma\beta}
, whereas its contravariant elements are found to be
b^{\alpha\beta}=b^{\alpha}_{\gamma}\hskip 1.0ptg^{\gamma\beta}
. This tensor measures the extrinsic curvature,
i.e.
the bending of the two-dimensional surface in the three-dimensional space. The connection between intrinsic and extrinsic geometry is captured by the
Gauss–Weingarten equations
,
i.e.

\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=b_{\alpha\beta}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},\quad\text{and}\quad\,\nabla_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=-b^{\hskip 0.5pt\beta}_{\alpha}\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta},

(516)

where the former equation follows from the definition of curvature tensor and covariant differentiation, while the latter is derived by differentiating the identities
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\hskip-1.5pt=0
and
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=1
, and then solving for the derivative
\nabla_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
.

The eigenvalues of the matrix
[b^{\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{\beta}]
are commonly called the
principal curvatures
, and their associated eigenvectors yield the
principal directions
. Instead of working with the principal curvatures, we use two scalar invariants: the mean curvature
H
, and the Gaussian curvature
K
, which are defined by

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}\,[b^{\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{\beta}]=\frac{1}{2}b^{\alpha}_{\alpha},\quad\text{and}

(517)

\displaystyle K=\det[b^{\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{\beta}],

(518)

respectively. The Gaussian curvature can also be written as
K=\det[b_{\alpha\beta}]\,/\det[g_{\alpha\beta}]
, or in index notation,

K=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\varepsilon^{\gamma\delta}b_{\alpha\gamma}\hskip 1.0ptb_{\beta\delta},

(519)

where the permutation tensor
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}
is defined by vanishing diagonal terms,
i.e.
\varepsilon^{11}=\varepsilon^{22}=0
, while its off-diagonal components are given by
\varepsilon^{12}=-\varepsilon^{21}=1/{\hskip-1.0pt\sqrt{g}}
. From Eq. (
519
), we can see that the tensor
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\varepsilon^{\gamma\delta}b_{\beta\delta}=\bar{b}^{\alpha\gamma}
acts as the cofactor of the curvature tensor, which can be rewritten as follows:

\bar{b}^{\alpha\beta}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\gamma}\varepsilon^{\beta\delta}b_{\gamma\delta}=2Hg^{\alpha\beta}-b^{\alpha\beta},

(520)

resulting in
\bar{b}^{\alpha\gamma}b_{\gamma\beta}=K\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
. This gives us yet another way to write the Gaussian curvature,
i.e.
K=2H^{2}-\frac{1}{2}b^{\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{\beta}b^{\hskip 0.5pt\beta}_{\alpha}
, which leads to the following useful formula:

b_{\alpha\beta}\hskip 1.0ptb^{\alpha\beta}=b^{\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{\beta}\hskip 1.0ptb^{\hskip 0.5pt\beta}_{\alpha}=4H^{2}-2K.

(521)

In Eq. (
510
), the domain of definition of the vector field
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
has been restricted to the tangent plane of the surface. However, Eqs. (
516
) and (
517
) provide now the tools to extend this to vector fields in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. By using the definition of the surface divergence, as in (
509
), for a vector
\boldsymbol{v}=v^{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+v\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
, then
\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})
is found to be

\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})=\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}-2Hv.

(522)

We note that the tensors
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
are not independent (in other words, not all choices of tensor fields
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
can describe a surface). This can be seen as an integrability condition on the Gauss–Weingarten equations, namely one needs to make sure that the identity

\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\gamma}=\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\gamma}

(523)

is satisfied. Thus, the normal and tangential components of (
523
) leads to
Gauss–Codazzi–Mainardi equations
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}b_{\beta\gamma}-\nabla_{\beta}b_{\alpha\gamma}=0,\quad\text{and}

(524)

\displaystyle b_{\alpha\gamma}b_{\beta\delta}-b_{\alpha\delta}b_{\beta\gamma}=R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}.

(525)

The latter equation tells us that the combination given by
b_{\alpha\gamma}b_{\beta\delta}-b_{\alpha\delta}b_{\beta\gamma}
is an intrinsic quantity of the surface, which can be purely computed from the metric (as
R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
depends only on the metric tensor), despite the fact that the curvature tensor
b_{\alpha\beta}
itself is an extrinsic quantity that requires an embedding. Moreover, by contracting twice the Riemann tensor, we obtain a scalar quantity known as the Ricci scalar,
i.e.
R=g^{\alpha\gamma}g^{\beta\delta}R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
. Using Eq. (
525
), we have
R=4H^{2}-b^{\hskip 0.5pt\alpha}_{\beta}b^{\hskip 0.5pt\beta}_{\alpha}=2K
, where the last equality follows from Eq. (
521
), and thus the Gaussian curvature is an intrinsic geometrical quantity. In fact, for two-dimensional surfaces, the Riemann tensor is completely determined by the Gaussian curvature
K
:

R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}=K\left(g_{\alpha\gamma}\,g_{\beta\delta}-g_{\alpha\delta}\,g_{\beta\gamma}\right)\!.

(526)

On the other hand, Eq. (
524
), which is often called the
Codazzi equation
, states that
\nabla_{\beta}b_{\alpha\gamma}
must be symmetric with respect to the indices
\alpha
and
\beta
. Since the curvature tensor
b_{\alpha\beta}
is also symmetric, then we find that
\nabla_{\beta}b_{\alpha\gamma}
is fully symmetric with respect to all its indices.

D.2
Calculus of Moving Surfaces

The formulation of kinetic and kinematic relationships on a deformable membrane is much easier achieved by parameterizing the surface through a convected coordinate system
\varrho^{\alpha}
with respect to a fixed reference system
\xi^{\alpha}
, where the latter is depicted in Fig.
19
. Specifically, we identify by
\hskip 1.0pt\hat{\!\boldsymbol{R}}(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,t)
the current position at time
t
of a material point that was located at

\hskip 1.0pt\hat{\!\boldsymbol{R}}(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0ptt_{0})\hskip-1.0pt=\boldsymbol{R}(\xi^{\alpha}),

(527)

with the initial (reference) time
t_{0}\hskip-1.0pt\leq t
. In other words, we specify the fixed coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
as functions which depend on both the time and the convected coordinates
\varrho^{\alpha}
, subject to the condition that

\varrho^{\alpha}=\xi^{\alpha}(\varrho^{\beta}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0ptt_{0}).

(528)

In addition, we assume that these mappings between
\varrho^{\alpha}
and
\xi^{\alpha}
are invertible, reflecting the notion that, at any fixed time
t
, a material point that is identified by constant
\varrho^{\alpha}
can be uniquely associated with the local surface coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
at that time. Thus, we write

\hat{\!\boldsymbol{R}}(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0ptt)\hskip-1.0pt=\boldsymbol{R}\!\left(\hskip 1.0pt\xi^{\beta}\hskip-1.0pt(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,t),\hskip 1.0ptt\hskip 1.0pt\right)\!.

(529)

The velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
of a material point that lives solely on the surface is defined by the rate of change of the position vector
\hat{\!\boldsymbol{R}}(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip-1.0ptt)
at fixed
\varrho^{\alpha}
, namely

\boldsymbol{v}=\left.\frac{\partial\hat{\!\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\varrho^{\beta}\}}\!\!=\,\left.\frac{\partial\!\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\xi^{\beta}\}}\!+\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\!\left.\frac{\partial\xi^{\alpha}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\varrho^{\beta}\}},

(530)

where the latter equality follows via the chain rule. Hereinafter, we define the tangential velocity components
v^{\alpha}
as the rate of change of the fixed coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
for a given material point of constant
\varrho^{\alpha}
, that is,

v^{\alpha}=\,\left.\frac{\partial\xi^{\alpha}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\varrho^{\beta}\}}.

(531)

On the other hand, the normal velocity is given by

v=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\hskip-1.0pt\left.\frac{\partial\!\hskip 1.0pt\boldsymbol{R}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\xi^{\alpha}\}},

(532)

so that the surface points of constant
\xi^{\alpha}
change only along the normal direction to the surface, without any loss of generality. Thus, the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
can be written as

\boldsymbol{v}=v\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}+v^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}.

(533)

Any scalar function
\phi(\xi^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0ptt)
that lives on the surface can also be written as a function in terms of the convected coordinates, say
\hat{\phi}(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0ptt)
, where we have that

\hat{\phi}(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,\hskip 1.0ptt)=\phi\!\left(\xi^{\beta}\hskip-1.0pt(\varrho^{\alpha}\hskip-1.0pt,t),\hskip 1.0ptt\right)\!.

(534)

The material derivative of
\phi
is simply the rate of change of
\hat{\phi}
for a given material point of constant
\varrho^{\alpha}
, that is,

\dot{\phi}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\phi}{\mathrm{d}t}=\left.\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\varrho^{\alpha}\}},

(535)

where the
overdot
symbol denotes the material derivative of a function. By utilizing the chain rule, this leads to

\dot{\phi}=\,\left.\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\xi^{\beta}\}}\!+\,\partial_{\alpha}\phi\!\left.\frac{\partial\xi^{\alpha}}{\partial t}\right|_{\{\varrho^{\beta}\}}=\;\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+v^{\alpha}\hskip 1.0pt\partial_{\alpha}\phi.

(536)

Similarly, we introduce the tangent basis in convected coordinates,
\hat{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\partial\hat{\boldsymbol{R}}/\partial\varrho^{\alpha}=(\partial\xi^{\beta}/\partial\varrho^{\alpha})\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}
. For quantities carrying surface indices, the overdot denotes their material rate in convected coordinates, expressed in the fixed-coordinate basis. In particular, the material rate of the tangent basis includes the change in the coordinate mapping, giving

\dot{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+v^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\right)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}v^{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{v}.

(537)

This can be decomposed into the local surface basis,

\dot{\!\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{v}=\left(\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\beta}-v\hskip 1.0ptb_{\alpha}^{\beta}\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}+\left(v^{\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}+\partial_{\alpha}v\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}},

(538)

by employing the form of the velocity field in Eq. (
533
), and by also using the Gauss–Weingarten equations.

By applying the material derivative to the orthogonality condition
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=0
, we have
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=-\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
. Since
\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=0
, the material derivative of the normal unit vector acts only within the tangent plane of the surface, and thus from Eq. (
538
) we obtain

\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}=-\left(v^{\beta}\hskip 0.25ptb^{\alpha}_{\beta}+\nabla^{\alpha}v\right)\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha},

(539)

where
\nabla^{\alpha}=g^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\beta}
. Eqs. (
538
) and (
539
) then allow us to find that

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}\;=\;

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial v^{\alpha}}{\partial t}-\left(v^{\beta}\hskip 0.25ptb^{\alpha}_{\beta}+\nabla^{\alpha}v\right)\hskip-1.0ptv+\left(\nabla_{\beta}v^{\alpha}-v\hskip 1.0ptb_{\beta}^{\alpha}\right)\hskip-1.0ptv^{\beta}\right]\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\;+\;\left[\frac{\partial v}{\partial t}+2v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}v+v^{\alpha}v^{\beta}b_{\alpha\beta}\right]\!\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}.

(540)

For the metric and curvature tensors, the overdot likewise denotes their convected material rates, which include the change in their surface-coordinate components. Explicitly, these rates are defined by

\displaystyle\dot{g}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial g_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial t}+v^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}g_{\alpha\beta}+g_{\gamma\beta}\partial_{\alpha}v^{\gamma}+g_{\alpha\gamma}\partial_{\beta}v^{\gamma},

(541)

\displaystyle\dot{b}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial b_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial t}+v^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}b_{\alpha\beta}+b_{\gamma\beta}\partial_{\alpha}v^{\gamma}+b_{\alpha\gamma}\partial_{\beta}v^{\gamma}.

(542)

Using the evolution of the tangent basis and normal vector, the corresponding expressions become

\displaystyle\dot{g}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\alpha}v_{\beta}+\nabla_{\beta}v_{\alpha}-2v\hskip 1.0ptb_{\alpha\beta},\quad\mathrm{and}

(543)

\displaystyle\dot{b}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=b_{\gamma\beta}\left(\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\gamma}-v\hskip 1.0ptb^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\right)+\nabla_{\beta}\!\left(v^{\gamma}\hskip 0.5ptb_{\gamma\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}v\right),

(544)

respectively. The above expressions follow by applying the product rule to the convected material rates and expanding the definitions of
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
b_{\alpha\beta}
in terms of the local tangent basis and the normal. For instance, we have
\dot{g}_{\alpha\beta}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\dot{\!\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}+\dot{\!\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\beta}
, which together with Eq. (
538
) gives Eq. (
543
). Similarly, from Eq. (
515
), we obtain
\dot{b}_{\alpha\beta}=\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\dot{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}\cdot\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}=-\partial_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}-\partial_{\beta}\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}\cdot\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
, which yields Eq. (
544
) upon substituting Eqs. (
538
) and (
539
).

D.3
Integral and Transport Theorems

The area element
\mathrm{d}S
of a surface that is parameterized by the fixed curvilinear coordinates
(\xi^{1},\xi^{2})
is given by

\mathrm{d}S=\mathrm{d}\xi^{1}\mathrm{d}\xi^{2}\sqrt{g},

(545)

where
g=\det[g_{\alpha\beta}]
, as previously defined in Eq. (
511
). This allows us to compute the total area of a surface
\mathcal{M}
by integrating over the fixed internal coordinates
\xi^{\alpha}
,
i.e.

\mathcal{A}=\!\int_{\mathcal{M}\;\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\,=\iint\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{1}\mathrm{d}\xi^{2}\sqrt{g}\,.

(546)

To determine the rate of change of area, we consider the convected rate of the surface area density. In the fixed-coordinate description, this is given by
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sqrt{g}=\partial_{t}\sqrt{g}+\partial_{\alpha}(\sqrt{g}\,v^{\alpha})
, where the second term accounts for tangential transport. Applying Jacobi’s formula, we obtain

\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\frac{\mathrm{d}(\sqrt{g})}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\beta}\dot{g}_{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}-2vH=\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v}),

(547)

where the last two equalities follow from Eqs. (
543
) and (
522
), respectively. Hence,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sqrt{g}=\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})\hskip 1.0pt\sqrt{g},

(548)

which implies that the rate of change of area of the closed surface is given by

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\iint\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{1}\mathrm{d}\xi^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sqrt{g}=\!\int_{\mathcal{M}\;\;}\!\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})\;\mathrm{d}S.

(549)

The identity in Eq. (
548
) can also be used to derive the
Reynolds transport theorem
, that is,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\hskip 1.0pt\int_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\boldsymbol{T}\,\mathrm{d}S=\!\int_{\mathcal{S}\;\;}\!\mathrm{d}S\left[\boldsymbol{T}\,\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})+\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{T}}{\mathrm{d}t}\hskip 1.0pt\right]\!,

(550)

where
\mathcal{S}
is a material surface patch moving with the membrane, and
\boldsymbol{T}
can be any tensor field defined on
\mathcal{S}
.

Furthermore, the volume
V
enclosed by an arbitrary closed (orientable) surface, say
\mathcal{M}
, can be obtained from the position vector
\boldsymbol{R}
as follows:

V=\!\iiint\!\mathrm{d}V\!=\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathcal{M}\,\;}\!\left(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}\right)\mathrm{d}S,

(551)

where the latter is found by employing the vector calculus identity,
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}=3
, with
\boldsymbol{r}
being any position vector, and lastly making use of the divergence theorem (by also noting that boundary of the volume
\partial V
is trivially the same as the closed surface of the membrane
\mathcal{M}
).

To find the rate of change of volume, we can simply apply the Reynolds transport theorem, which yields

\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathcal{M}\,\;}\!\Big\{v+\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\left[\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\hskip 1.0pt\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})\right]\!\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}S,

(552)

where the term in the square brackets can be written as:
\dot{\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}}+\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\hskip 1.0pt\text{div}\,\hskip-1.0pt(\boldsymbol{v})=-\big(v^{\beta}\hskip 0.25ptb^{\alpha}_{\beta}+\nabla^{\alpha}v\big)\hskip-0.5pt\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}+\big(\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}-2Hv\big)\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}
. Notice that the term
\boldsymbol{R}\cdot(\nabla^{\alpha}v\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha})
can be expressed in terms of a total derivative, namely

\boldsymbol{R}\cdot(\nabla^{\alpha}v\,\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha})=\nabla^{\alpha}\!\left[v\hskip 1.0pt(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{R})\right]-2\hskip 0.5ptv\hskip 1.0pt(1+H\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}),

(553)

where Eq. (
515
) is used to derive the latter. Since
\mathcal{M}
is a closed surface, we obtain that

\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathcal{M}\,\;}\!\Big\{3v+\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\left[\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\hskip 1.0pt\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}-v^{\beta}b^{\alpha}_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}\right]\!\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}S.

(554)

Using the Gauss–Weingarten equation,
\nabla_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}=-b^{\alpha}_{\beta}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{e}}_{\alpha}
, the second term in the integrand of Eq. (
554
) simply becomes
\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\big[\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\hskip 1.0pt\nabla_{\alpha}v^{\alpha}\hskip-0.5pt+v^{\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\big]\!=\hskip-1.0pt\nabla_{\alpha}\!\left[(\hskip 0.2pt{\boldsymbol{n}}\cdot\boldsymbol{R})v^{\alpha}\right]
. Its integral vanishes on the closed surface by the surface divergence theorem. Therefore, this leads to the following simple (and geometrically intuitive) result:

\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}t}=\int_{\mathcal{M}\,\;}\!v\;\mathrm{d}S,

(555)

that is, the rate of change of the volume
V
is identical to the integrated value of the normal velocity over the total area of the membrane surface.
